Hyperbolic p p -sum and Horospherical p p -Brunn-Minkowski theory in hyperbolic space
Haizhong Li, Botong Xu

Abstract
. The classical Brunn-Minkowski theory studies the geometry of convex bodies in Euclidean space by use of the Minkowski sum. It originated from H. Brunn’s thesis in 1887 and H. Minkowski’s paper in 1903. Because there is no universally acknowledged definition of the sum of two sets in hyperbolic space, there has been no Brunn-Minkowski theory in hyperbolic space since 1903. In this paper, for any p > 0 p>0 we introduce a sum of two sets in hyperbolic space, and we call it the hyperbolic p p -sum . Then we develop a Brunn-Minkowski theory in the hyperbolic space by use of our hyperbolic p p -sum, and we call it the horospherical p p -Brunn-Minkowski theory. Let K K be any smooth horospherically convex bounded domain in the hyperbolic space ℍ n + 1 \mathbb{H}^{n+1} . Through calculating the variation of the k k -th modified quermassintegral of K K by use of our hyperbolic p p -sum, we introduce the horospherical k k -th p p -surface area measure associated with K K on the unit sphere 𝕊 n \mathbb{S}^{n} . For k = 0 k=0 , we introduce the horospherical p p -Minkowski problem , which is the prescribed horospherical p p -surface area measure problem. Through designing and studying a new volume preserving flow, we solve the existence of solutions to the horospherical p p -Minkowski problem for all p ∈ ( − ∞ , + ∞ ) p\in(-\infty,+\infty) when the given measure is even. For 1 ≤ k ≤ n − 1 1\leq k\leq n-1 , we introduce the horospherical p p -Christoffel-Minkowski problem , which is the prescribed horospherical k k -th p p -surface area measure problem. We solve the existence of solutions to the horospherical p p -Christoffel-Minkowski problem for p ∈ ( − n , + ∞ ) p\in(-n,+\infty) under appropriate assumption on the given measure. We also study the Brunn-Minkowski inequalities and the Minkowski inequalities for domains in the hyperbolic space.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
52A55; 35K55

1.
Introduction

1.1.
An overview of the Brunn-Minkowski theory in Euclidean space

Convex geometry studies convex bodies in Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
.
The classical Brunn-Minkowski theory is the heart of convex geometry, and it originated from Brunn’s thesis
[
18
]
and Minkowski’s paper
[
65
]
.
By introducing the concept of support functions, Minkowski defined a linear combination of convex bodies, which is called the
Minkowski addition
(i.e. Minkowski sum).
By studying the volume of linear combinations of convex bodies, he defined the mixed volume of convex bodies. Many geometric inequalities can be stated by use of the above concepts, such as the Brunn-Minkowski inequality, the Minkowski inequality, and the Alexandrov-Fenchel inequality. Here we refer readers to
[
34
]
for a survey on the Brunn-Minkowski inequality. Besides, Osserman
[
68
]
emphasized that the Brunn-Minkowski inequality directly implies the isoperimetric inequality. On the other hand, Aleksandrov
[
1
]
, Fenchel, and Jessen
[
27
]
showed that the integral representation of the mixed volume could induce a Borel measure on the unit sphere
\mathbb{S}^{n}
, which is known as the surface area measure. The classical Minkowski problem asks the necessary and sufficient conditions for a Borel measure on
\mathbb{S}^{n}
to be the surface area measure of a convex body. The polytopal case was solved by Minkowski
[
64
,
65
]
. In general cases, the existence of solutions to this problem was solved independently by Aleksandrov
[
2
]
, Fenchel and Jessen
[
27
]
. The regularity of solutions to this problem was studied by Lewy
[
51
]
, Nirenberg
[
67
]
, Cheng, Yau
[
22
]
, and Pogorelov
[
69
]
. Guan and Ma
[
40
]
studied the Christoffel-Minkowski problem and gave a sufficient condition for the existence of solutions.

In 1962, Firey
[
30
]
found a class of combinations of convex bodies containing the origin for each real number
p\geq 1
, which was called the
Minkowski-Firey
L_{p}
-addition
(Firey’s
p
-sum for short).
In 1993, Lutwak
[
59
]
discovered that Firey’s
p
-sums could induce a Brunn-Minkowski theory for
p\geq 1
, called the
L_{p}
Brunn-Minkowski theory (where
p=1
is the classical case), see also
[
60
]
. He proved the
L_{p}
Minkowski inequality and the
L_{p}
Brunn-Minkowski inequality for
p>1
, where the latter was obtained earlier by Firey
[
30
]
. For
0\leq p<1
, the
p
-sums and the Brunn-Minkowski inequalities have also been studied in the recent decade, see e.g.
[
12
,
50
,
21
]
. Now we focus on the prescribed measure problems in the
L_{p}
Brunn-Minkowski theory. Lutwak
[
59
]
defined the
L_{p}
surface area measure for convex bodies containing the origin in their interiors. In the case
p>1
, by use of the
L_{p}
Minkowski inequality, he showed that the
L_{p}
area measures appeared naturally in the integral representation of the mixed
p
-quermassintegrals. The prescribed
L_{p}
surface area measure problem is called the
L_{p}
Minkowski problem, which reduces to the classical Minkowski problem when
p=1
. In the case
p=0
, it is the prescribed cone-volume measure problem and is also called the logarithmic Minkowski problem
[
13
]
. In the case
p=-n-1
, it is called the centro-affine Minkowski problem
[
23
]
. For
n+1\neq p>1
, Lutwak
[
59
]
studied the existence and uniqueness of origin symmetric solutions to the
L_{p}
Minkowski problem. For
p\geq-n-1
, Chou and Wang
[
23
]
studied the problem under various assumptions on the given measure. Besides, the polytopal case of the
L_{p}
Minkowski problem has also been studied, see e.g.
[
49
,
11
,
79
,
80
]
. The
L_{p}
Brunn-Minkowski theory has developed rapidly for decades, see a recent survey by Böröczky
[
14
]
.

In 2016, Huang, Lutwak, Yang, and Zhang
[
48
]
introduced and studied the dual Minkowski problem. For other works contributed to this problem, we refer to, e.g.
[
55
,
58
]
and the references therein. For readers interested in the Brunn-Minkowski theory, we refer to the books by Schneider
[
73
]
and Gardner
[
35
]
.

1.2.
Motivation and construction of hyperbolic
p
-sum

Researchers have tried to figure out the counterpart of results in the Brunn-Minkowski theory for domains in the hyperbolic space
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
over decades.

The Minkowski type problems for smooth convex bodies in Euclidean space can be unified as the prescribed measure problems via the Gauss map, e.g. the classical Minkowski problem, the Christoffel-Minkowski problem, and the
L_{p}
Minkowski problem. For domains in hyperbolic space, once a suitable Gauss map has been defined, one can also study the prescribed measure problems and the prescribed curvature problems, see e.g.
[
26
]
and
[
37
, Chapter 10]
.

In the classical Brunn-Minkowski theory, a special type of the Alexandrov-Fenchel inequalities concerns the comparison of different quermassintegrals of a single convex body, in particular, the classical isoperimetric inequality. For a smooth convex body in Euclidean space, the quermassintegrals are either volume or can be expressed as curvature integrals on the boundary. For a bounded domain (i.e. compact sets with non-empty interior) in hyperbolic space, the quermassintegrals have also been defined, see
[
71
]
. Especially, when the domain is smooth, its quermassintegrals can be expressed as linear combinations of the volume and the curvature integrals on the boundary.
Then people turned to study the comparison of the quermassintegrals and curvature integrals for bounded domains in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
and obtained several Alexandrov-Fenchel type inequalities with suitable convex assumptions on the domains, see e.g.
[
4
,
53
,
77
,
46
,
47
,
45
]
. However, most of the above results in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
are scattered and only for a single domain.

The question arises whether there is a Brunn-Minkowski theory for bounded domains in the hyperbolic space with suitable convex assumptions
.

Our main purpose is to find such a theory so that it can summarize some of the earlier
irrelevant
results for domains in the hyperbolic space. The main difficulty in establishing a Brunn-Minkowski theory for bounded domains in hyperbolic space is that there is no underlying linear structure on the hyperbolic space. Then people do not know how to introduce the concept “sum” of two domains, which is the first and the most important step. Once it is settled, by following the procedure in the classical Brunn-Minkowski theory (see Subsection
1.1
), one can study the corresponding prescribed measure problems, Brunn-Minkowski inequalities, and Minkowski inequalities in hyperbolic space.
To overcome the above difficulty, we introduce the concept of
hyperbolic
p
-sum
by using two different methods.

The first method is to construct the hyperbolic
p
-sum of two domains in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
by use of their “support functions”. In Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, convex bodies can be viewed as intersections of their supporting half spaces (see
[
73
, Corollary 1.3.5]
), and the support function of a given convex body is defined by the distance from the origin to the hyperplanes which are tangential to it. In hyperbolic space
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
, we can use horo-balls to replace “half spaces” and use horospheres to replace “hyperplanes”, where the
horospheres
are the hypersurfaces in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
with principal curvatures equal to
1
everywhere, and the
horo-balls
are domains delimited by horospheres. Therefore, it is natural to study the
horospherically convex bounded domains
, i.e. the intersections of closed horo-balls, and we call them
h-convex bounded domains
for short. The
horospherical support function
of a h-convex bounded domain is defined by the distance from a fixed origin in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
to the horospheres which are tangential to it. These concepts can be found in
[
3
]
.

To introduce the follow-up hyperbolic
p
-sum in Definition
1.1
, let us formulate the definition of the horospherical support function more precisely.
Throughout the paper, we use the hyperboloid model of
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
in Minkowski space
\mathbb{R}^{n+1,1}
. For a smooth h-convex bounded domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{H}^{n+1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1,1}
, let
X\in\partial\Omega
be the position vector and
\nu
be the outward unit normal of
\partial\Omega\subset\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
at
X
. Since
\langle X,X\rangle=-1
and
\langle X,\nu\rangle=0
on
\partial\Omega
, there exist
u\in\mathbb{R}
and
z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
such that

X-\nu=e^{-u}(z,1).

Define the
horospherical Gauss map

G:\partial\Omega\to\mathbb{S}^{n}
by
G\left(X\right)=z
and call
u
the
horospherical support function
of
\Omega
.
For a smooth bounded domain, if the principal curvatures are greater than
1+\delta
for some
\delta>0
on its boundary, then we call it
uniformly horospherically convex
(uniformly h-convex for short). In this case, the map
G
is a diffeomorphism, see
[
3
]
. Then we can view
u
as a function on
\mathbb{S}^{n}
and denote it by
u(z)
when
\Omega
is uniformly h-convex. We emphasize that
a single point in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
can be regarded as a degenerated h-convex bounded domain
, since it can be approximated by a sequence of closed geodesic balls.

Since the horospherical support function has been defined, it is a natural idea to use them to construct the sum of two uniformly h-convex bounded domains. To this end, we introduce the following definition of
hyperbolic
p
-sum
.

Definition 1.1
.

Let
p
,
a
, and
b
be real numbers that satisfy
\frac{1}{2}\leq p\leq 2
,
a\geq 0
,
b\geq 0
and
a+b\geq 1
, and let
K
and
L
be two smooth uniformly h-convex bounded domains in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. Denote by
u_{K}(z)
and
u_{L}(z)
the horospherical support functions of
K
and
L
respectively. Allow that both
K
and
L
can degenerate to a single point. We define the hyperbolic
p
-sum
\Omega:=a\cdot K+_{p}b\cdot L
of
K
and
L
by the h-convex bounded domain with horospherical support function

u_{\Omega}(z):=\frac{1}{p}\log\left(ae^{pu_{K}(z)}+be^{pu_{L}(z)}\right).

We will show that the above Definition
1.1
is well-defined in Theorem
4.1
. Remark that the case
p=1
,
a=1
, and
b=1
of Definition
1.1
was appeared in the paper of Gallego, Solanes, and Teufel
[
31
]
, where they called it the harmonic sum. Since there exists no good definition of “dilation”, their harmonic sum can’t extend to linear combinations of h-convex bounded domains.

The second method to construct the hyperbolic
p
-sum is to give a pointwise definition.
Back to the classical Brunn-Minkowski theory, the pointwise definition of Minkowski addition of convex bodies is valid for general sets in Euclidean space, i.e.

\widehat{\Omega}=a\widehat{K}+b\widehat{L}:=\{ax+by:x\in\widehat{K},\,y\in\widehat{L}\},

where
a,b\geq 0
, and
\widehat{K},\widehat{L}
are sets in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. In the
L_{p}
Brunn-Minkowski theory, Lutwak, Yang, and Zhang
[
57
]
gave a pointwise definition of Firey’s
p
-sum
[
30
]
for each
p\geq 1
. Furthermore, their pointwise addition is also valid for general sets in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
.

Motivated by the above pointwise sums in Euclidean space, for all
p\in(0,+\infty)
, we will give a pointwise definition (see Definition
1.3
below) of the hyperbolic
p
-sum for sets in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. Before we state the specific construction, we would like to give a geometric intuition of the hyperbolic
p
-sum for sets. As in Definition
1.1
, we need two sets
K,L
in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
and need three parameters
p
,
a
and
b
. We first consider the case
p=1
, which is closely related to the classical Minkowski addition from a geometric point of view. For any
X\in K
and
Y\in L
, it seems natural to define the hyperbolic
1
-sum “
a\cdot X+_{1}b\cdot Y
” of
X
and
Y
by
aX+bY
, where we used the vector addition in Minkowski space
\mathbb{R}^{n+1,1}
. However, in general cases, the vector
aX+bY
is no longer on the hyperboloid
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
.

To overcome that obstacle, we
identify the vector
aX+bY
in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1,1}
with a geodesic ball in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
, which is enclosed by the intersection of the past light cone of
aX+bY
and the hyperboloid
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. Then we define
a\cdot K+_{1}b\cdot L
by the union of those geodesic balls. Now we turn to consider the case
p\in(0,1)\cup(1,+\infty)
. For any
X\in K
and
Y\in L
, there exists a unique geodesic segment connecting
X
and
Y
. Then any point on the segment can be parameterized by a new parameter
t\in[0,1]
, see (
1.2
) below. For each point on the segment, we will define a geodesic ball centered at that point whose radius only depends on
\{a,b,p,X,Y,t\}
, see (
1.1
) below. Then we can define the hyperbolic
p
-sum of
K
and
L
by the combination of those geodesic balls.

To introduce the follow-up pointwise hyperbolic
p
-sum in Definition
1.3
, we need to set up some notations. Let
X
and
Y
be two points in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
, and let
p>0
,
t\in[0,1]
,
a\geq 0
, and
b\geq 0
be real numbers. Denote by
d_{\mathbb{H}^{n+1}}\left(\cdot,\cdot\right)
the distance function in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
and by
\overline{XY}
the geodesic segment that connects
X
and
Y
in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
.

•

If
p\in(0,1)\cup(1,+\infty)
, then we define

(1.1)

R(p,t;a,X,b,Y):=\left((1-t)^{\frac{2}{q}}a^{\frac{2}{p}}+t^{\frac{2}{q}}b^{\frac{2}{p}}+2\left(1-t\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}t^{\frac{1}{q}}a^{\frac{1}{p}}b^{\frac{1}{p}}\cosh d_{\mathbb{H}^{n+1}}(X,Y)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

where
q
is the Hölder conjugate of
p
, i.e.
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1
.
Let
P(p,t;a,X,b,Y)
be a point lying on
\overline{XY}
such that the distance between it and
X
satisfies

(1.2)

\cosh d_{\mathbb{H}^{n+1}}\left(P(p,t;a,X,b,Y),X\right)=\frac{(1-t)^{\frac{1}{q}}a^{\frac{1}{p}}+t^{\frac{1}{q}}b^{\frac{1}{p}}\cosh d_{\mathbb{H}^{n+1}}(X,Y)}{R(p,t;a,X,b,Y)}.

•

Define

(1.3)

R(a,X,b,Y):=\left(a^{2}+b^{2}+2ab\cosh d_{\mathbb{H}^{n+1}}(X,Y)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Let
P(a,X,b,Y)
be a point lying on
\overline{XY}
such that the distance between it and
X
satisfies

(1.4)

\cosh d_{\mathbb{H}^{n+1}}\left(P(a,X,b,Y),X\right)=\frac{a+b\cosh d_{\mathbb{H}^{n+1}}(X,Y)}{R(a,X,b,Y)}.

Definition 1.2
.

(1)

If
p\in(0,1)\cup(1,+\infty)
and
R(p,t;a,X,b,Y)\geq 1
, then we define
B(p,t;a,X,b,Y)
as the closed geodesic ball of radius
\log R(p,t;a,X,b,Y)
centered at
P(p,t;a,X,b,Y)
in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
.

(2)

If
p\in(0,1)\cup(1,+\infty)
and
R(p,t;a,X,b,Y)<1
, then we define
B(p,t;a,X,b,Y)
as the empty set.

(3)

If
R(a,X,b,Y)\geq 1
, then we define
B(a,X,b,Y)
as the closed geodesic ball of radius
\log R(a,X,b,Y)
centered at
P(a,X,b,Y)
in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
.

(4)

If
R(a,X,b,Y)<1
, then we define
B(a,X,b,Y)
as the empty set.

After the above preparing works, we are in the position to state the pointwise hyperbolic
p
-sum.

Definition 1.3
.

Let
p>0
,
a\geq 0
and
b\geq 0
be real numbers, and let
K
and
L
be sets in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. Then the hyperbolic
p
-sum
\Omega
of
K
and
L
, i.e.

\Omega:=a\cdot K+_{p}b\cdot L

is defined by

\Omega=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\bigcup_{X\in K,\,Y\in L}\bigcup_{t\in[0,1]}B(p,t;a,X,b,Y),\quad&p>1,\\
&\bigcup_{X\in K,\,Y\in L}B(a,X,b,Y),\quad&p=1,\\
&\bigcup_{X\in K,\,Y\in L}\bigcap_{t\in[0,1]}B(p,t;a,X,b,Y),\quad&0<p<1.\end{aligned}\right.

When
p
,
a
,
b
,
K
, and
L
satisfy the assumptions as in Definition
1.1
, we will show in Theorem
4.4
that the pointwise hyperbolic
p
-sum defined in Definition
1.3
is compatible with the hyperbolic
p
-sum defined in Definition
1.1
.
Then Definition
1.3
implies that Definition
1.1
does not depend on the hyperboloid model. Specifically, the hyperbolic
p
-sum
a\cdot K+_{p}b\cdot L
can be constructed without using the Minkowski space
\mathbb{R}^{n+1,1}
and the horospherical support functions of
K
and
L
, it only depends on the relative position of
K
and
L
in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. Here we remark that our Definition
1.3
is different from the
\lambda
-horocycle Minkowski sum in
\mathbb{H}^{2}
defined recently by Assouline and Klartag
[
9
]
. Fillastre
[
29
]
studied Fuchsian convex bodies in Minkowski space
\mathbb{R}^{n+1,1}
.

In the next two subsections, we will follow the procedure in the classical Brunn-Minkowski theory and study the prescribed measure problems and geometric inequalities that are both generated by our hyperbolic
p
-sum. At the same time, we will continuously discuss the relationship between these new problems and the earlier results in hyperbolic space.

As we concern about the horospherically convex domains, we sum up the related problems in a theory called the
horospherical
p
-Brunn-Minkowski theory
in hyperbolic space.

1.3.
Prescribed measure problems in hyperbolic space

For each integer
k=0,1,\ldots,n
and any h-convex bounded domain
K\subset\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
, the
k
-th modified quermassintegral
\widetilde{W}_{k}\left(K\right)
of
K
was defined by Andrews, Chen, and Wei
[
3
]
, see (
5.1
). They proved that these
\{\widetilde{W}_{k}\left(\cdot\right)\}_{k=0}^{n}
are monotone with respect to the inclusion of h-convex bounded domains.

Let
K
be a smooth uniformly h-convex bounded domain with horospherical support function
u_{K}(z)
. Define
\varphi_{K}(z)=e^{u_{K}(z)}
. We will show in Lemma
5.2
that the variation of
\widetilde{W}_{k}\left(K+_{p}t\cdot L\right)
along any h-convex bounded domain
L
(might be a point) at
t=0
induces a measure

dS_{p,k}\left(K,z\right):=\varphi_{K}^{-p-k}p_{n-k}\left(A[\varphi_{K}]\right)d\sigma

on
\mathbb{S}^{n}
, where
d\sigma
is the area measure of the unit sphere
\mathbb{S}^{n}
; the matrix
A[\varphi_{K}]
is defined by

A_{ij}[\varphi_{K}]:=D_{j}D_{i}\varphi_{K}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{|D\varphi_{K}|^{2}}{\varphi_{K}}\sigma_{ij}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\varphi_{K}-\frac{1}{\varphi_{K}}\right)\sigma_{ij},

where
\sigma_{ij}
denotes the standard metric on
\mathbb{S}^{n}
,
D
denotes the Levi-Civita connection with respect to
\sigma_{ij}
, and
p_{n-k}\left(A[\varphi_{K}]\right)
is the modified
(n-k)
-th elementary symmetric function of the eigenvalues
\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}
of
A[\varphi_{K}]
, i.e.

p_{n-k}\left(A[\varphi_{K}]\right)=\frac{1}{C_{n}^{n-k}}\sigma_{n-k}\left(A[\varphi_{K}]\right)=\frac{1}{C_{n}^{n-k}}\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{n-k}\leq n}x_{i_{1}}x_{i_{2}}\cdots x_{i_{n-k}}.

Then we call
dS_{p,k}\left(K,z\right)
the
k
-th horospherical
p
-surface area measure of
K
. Now we introduce the following prescribed
k
-th horospherical
p
-surface area measure problem.

For a given smooth positive function
f(z)
, we ask the sufficient and necessary conditions for
f(z)
, such that the equation

(1.5)

\varphi^{-p-k}p_{n-k}\left(A[\varphi]\right)=f(z)

admits a smooth positive solution
\varphi(z)
with
A[\varphi(z)]>0
for all
z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
.

We call it the horospherical
p
-Minkowski problem when
k=0
and call it the horospherical
p
-Christoffel-Minkowski problem when
1\leq k\leq n-1
, see the formal statements of the above problems in Problem
5.1
and Problem
5.2
.

1.3.1.
Kazdan-Warner type obstructions

If
p=-n
in (
1.5
), then equation (
1.5
) can be formulated as the following prescribed curvature equation for smooth uniformly h-convex hypersurfaces, i.e.

(1.6)

p_{n-k}\left(\frac{1}{\kappa-1}\right)=f(z),

where
\kappa=(\kappa_{1},\ldots,\kappa_{n})
are the principal curvatures, and
z
is the image of the horospherical Gauss map defined on the hypersurface.

When
k=n-1
, equation (
1.6
) is closely related to the Christoffel problem in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
studied by Espinar, Gálvez, and Mira
[
26
]
. They observed that the Christoffel problem in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
is closely related to the Nirenberg problem, and then they gave a Kazdan-Warner type obstruction to it. Motivated by their result, for general
k=0,1,\ldots,n-1
and constant function
f(z)
, we notice that equation (
1.6
) relates to the
\sigma_{m}
-Yamabe problems
[
75
]
(
m=1,\ldots,n-k
) in conformal geometry. In Theorem
9.1
, we will give Kazdan-Warner type obstructions to equation (
1.6
) for all
0\leq k\leq n-1
. Particularly, for
n\geq 1
and
p=-n
, we will show that the solutions
\varphi(z)
to equation (
1.5
) must satisfy

(1.7)

\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}\varphi^{-n}\langle Df(z),Dx_{i}\rangle d\sigma=0,\quad i=0,1,\ldots,n,

where
x_{0},\ldots,x_{n}
are the coordinate functions of
\mathbb{S}^{n}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
.

We present two different proofs of the obstruction (
1.7
).

•

Our first method is to consider equation (
1.5
) as a linear combination of the equations for
\sigma_{m}
-Nirenberg problem (
m=1,\ldots,n-k
), and then we will prove the obstruction (
1.7
) by
using several Kazdan-Warner type identities proved in
[
44
,
56
,
75
]
.

•

The second method is more direct. After observing a key identity for closed hypersurfaces in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
(see Lemma
9.3
), we can prove the obstruction (
1.7
) by a direct calculation.

The obstruction (
1.7
) shows that, for general function
f(z)
defined on
\mathbb{S}^{n}
, equation (
1.5
) may not have a solution when
p=-n
.

1.3.2.
Uniqueness result

When
f(z)
is a positive constant and
p\geq-n
, we completely classify the solutions to equation (
1.5
) satisfying
\varphi(z)>1
and
A_{ij}[\varphi]>0
, see Theorem
8.1
. Geometrically, the solutions are geodesic balls centered at the origin if
p>-n
, and are geodesic balls if
p=-n
. A general version of Theorem
8.1
will be proved in Proposition
8.1
, where we prove that geodesic balls are the only solutions to a class of curvature equations, see (
8.1
). The new techniques we use are integral formulas (see Lemma
8.1
) for closed hypersurfaces in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
and a Heintze-Karcher type inequality (see Proposition
8.2
) for curves in
\mathbb{H}^{2}
.

1.3.3.
Existence result

Let us consider the equation (
1.5
) when
f(z)
is an even function on
\mathbb{S}^{n}
, i.e.
f(z)=f(-z)
for all
z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
. We will prove in Theorem
7.2
that, for
k=0
and
p\in(-\infty,+\infty)
, there exists a constant
\gamma
such that equation

(1.8)

\varphi^{-p-k}p_{n-k}(A[\varphi])=\gamma f(z)

admits a positive even solution
\varphi(z)
with
A_{ij}[\varphi(z)]>0
, which is an existence result of the horospherical
p
-Minkowski problem. For
1\leq k\leq n-1
and
p\geq-n
, by making appropriate assumptions on
f(z)
(see Assumption
7.1
), we will prove the existence of positive even solutions to equation (
1.8
) in Theorem
7.3
, which is an existence result of the horospherical
p
-Christoffel-Minkowski problem.

To prove the above results, we introduce a new volume preserving flow in (
7.7
). For each real number
p
, we also define a functional
J_{p}(\Omega)
for h-convex bounded domain
\Omega
in (
7.12
). We will show that
J_{p}(\Omega_{t})
is strictly decreasing along the flow (
7.7
) unless the horospherical support function of
\Omega_{t}
solves equation (
1.8
). Then the desired existence results of equation (
1.8
) (see Theorem
7.2
and Theorem
7.3
) can be proved by use of the long time existence and the subsequential convergence of the flow (
7.7
) (see Theorem
7.4
).

1.4.
Geometric inequalities in hyperbolic space

1.4.1.
Steiner formulas and their applications

Steiner formula for convex bodies in Euclidean space gives an explicit formula for the volume bounded by two convex parallel hypersurfaces.
Steiner formulas in the hyperbolic space and the sphere can be found in Santaló’s book
[
71
, Chapter 18]
.

We will derive the Steiner formulas for modified quermassintegrals of h-convex bounded domains in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
in Theorem
6.3
. Moreover, we will derive the Steiner formula for volumes with weight
\cosh r
in Theorem
6.4
, where
r
denotes the distance function in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
from the origin. As an application, we will prove a new weighted Alexandrov-Fenchel type inequality for h-convex bounded domains in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
, see Theorem
6.5
.

1.4.2.
Horospherical
p
-Brunn-Minkowski inequality

Denote by
B(r)
the geodesic ball of radius
r
centered at the origin in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. For any bounded domain
\Omega
in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
, we let
r^{k}_{\Omega}
be the positive number satisfying
\widetilde{W}_{k}(B(r^{k}_{\Omega}))=\widetilde{W}_{k}(\Omega)
for each
0\leq k\leq n
, see also Definition
10.1
.

Let
a
,
b
,
p
,
K
and
L
satisfy the assumptions in Definition
1.1
, we conjecture that the following geometric inequality holds,

(1.9)

\exp(pr^{k}_{a\cdot K+_{p}b\cdot L})\geq a\exp(pr^{k}_{K})+b\exp\left(pr^{k}_{L}\right),\quad k=0,\ldots,n.

We call the above inequality as the horospherical
p
-Brunn-Minkowski inequality, and the formal statement will be given in Conjecture
10.3
. Especially, when
K
and
L
are geodesic balls, inequality (
1.9
) will be proved in Theorem
10.2
.

Curvature flows are powerful tools in proving geometric inequalities, see e.g.
[
3
,
36
,
39
,
47
,
45
,
72
]
. Andrews et al.
[
3
, Corollary 1.9]
proved a class of Alexandrov-Fenchel type inequalities by using a volume preserving flow, see Theorem C in Subsection
10.1
. Later, Hu, Wei, and the first author
[
45
]
proved a class of weighted Alexandrov-Fenchel type inequalities
[
45
, Theorem 1.8]
for h-convex hypersurfaces by using a locally constrained flow, see Theorem E in Subsection
10.2
.

In view of the desired inequality (
1.9
), we will show in Proposition
10.1
that the inequality in Theorem C (
[
3
, Corollary 1.9]
) can be interpreted as

\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}\exp(pr^{k}_{K+_{p}t\cdot K})\geq\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}\left(\exp(pr^{k}_{K})+t\exp\left(pr^{k}_{K}\right)\right),\quad k=0,\ldots,n.

On the other hand, we will show that the inequality in Theorem E (
[
45
, Theorem 1.8]
) can be interpreted as

\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}\exp(r^{k}_{K+_{1}t\cdot N})\geq\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}\left(\exp(r^{k}_{K})+t\exp\left(r^{k}_{N}\right)\right),\quad k=1,\ldots,n,

where
N
is regarded as the origin of
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. In the
L_{p}
Brunn-Minkowski theory, the
L_{p}
Minkowski inequalities are generated from the variation of the
L_{p}
Brunn-Minkowski inequalities. Therefore by using the hyperbolic
p
-sum in Definition
1.1
, we can regard the above Theorem C and Theorem E as two special types of Minkowski inequalities in the horospherical
p
-Brunn-Minkowski theory, see the statements of Conjecture
10.5
and Conjecture
10.6
for general horospherical
p
-Minkowski inequalities in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
.

Motivated by the above observation, by using the curvature flows and geometric inequalities in
[
47
,
45
,
72
]
, we will prove some special cases of Conjectures
10.3
,
10.5
, and
10.6
. The results are summarized in Theorems
10.12
,
10.13
, and
10.14
.

1.5.
Weighted horospherical
p
-Brunn-Minkowski theory

The weight
\cosh r
often appears in geometric inequalities in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
; for example, it occurred in the Minkowski inequalities proved by Brendle, Hung, and Wang
[
16
]
, and Chao Xia
[
78
]
, see also
[
72
]
. Furthermore, Hu and the first author
[
47
]
, Hu, Wei, and the first author
[
45
]
proved a class of Alexandrov-Fenchel type inequalities for curvature integrals with weight
\cosh r
. Now we call the integral of
\cosh r
on domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
the weighted volume of
\Omega
.

So what is the behavior of the weighted volume of domains along with the hyperbolic
p
-sum? To answer that question, we propose Conjecture
11.3
, which is called the weighted horospherical
p
-Brunn-Minkowski inequality. Based on Conjecture
11.3
, we can propose several Minkowski type inequalities and isoperimetric type inequalities with weight
\cosh r
in Conjectures
11.4
–
11.6
.
Then we find that the weighted isoperimetric inequality proved by Scheuer and Xia
[
72
]
can be viewed as a corollary of Conjecture
11.3
. Beyond that observation, we prove a class of weighted isoperimetric type inequalities in Theorem
11.7
, which can be considered as corollaries of the aforementioned Conjecture
11.5
.

In the last part of this paper, we study the relationship between the Minkowski-Firey
L_{p}
-addition for convex bodies in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
and the hyperbolic
p
-sum in Definition
1.1
. We will construct a map
\pi
from h-convex bounded domains in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
to convex bodies in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
that contains the origin in their interiors. Geometrically,
-\pi(\Omega)\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
is the domain enclosed by the intersection of the null hypersurface generated by
\partial\Omega
with
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}:=\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\times\{0\}
, where
\Omega
is a h-convex bounded domain in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. We will prove in Proposition
12.1
that the Euclidean support function of
\pi(\Omega)\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
is precisely
e^{u_{\Omega}(z)}
, where
u_{\Omega}(z)
is the horospherical support function of
\Omega\subset\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. Combining that fact with Definition
1.1
, we will obtain the relationship between the hyperbolic
p
-sum and the Firey’s
p
-sum in Theorem
12.2
. Consequently, we can use the map
\pi
to study the Brunn-Minkowski type inequality and Minkowski type inequalities associated with the hyperbolic
p
-sum, see Theorem
12.4
and Theorem
12.5
. Besides, we will prove geometric inequalities for domains in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
, where equality holds if and only if the domains are geodesic balls in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
, see Theorem
12.6
.

1.6.
Organization of the paper

In Sections
2
and Section
3
, we collect basic concepts and study properties of h-convex domains and h-convex hypersurfaces in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. In Section
4
, we prove that the hyperbolic
p
-sum in Definition
1.1
is well-defined and prove the compatibility of the hyperbolic
p
-sums in Definition
1.1
and Definition
1.3
. In Section
5
, through calculating the variation of the modified quermassintegrals of h-convex domains by use of our hyperbolic
p
-sum, we introduce the horospherical
p
-surface area measures of h-convex bounded domains in the hyperbolic space. Then we introduce the horospherical
p
-Minkowski problem and the horospherical
p
-Christoffel-Minkowski problem, see Problem
5.1
and Problem
5.2
. In Section
6
, we define the hyperbolic
p
-dilation and study the Steiner formulas for h-convex bounded domains.

In Section
7
, we study the existence results of the horospherical
p
-Minkowski problem and the horospherical
p
-Christoffel-Minkowski problem by designing and studying a new volume preserving flow (
7.7
). For the horospherical
p
-Minkowski problem, we prove the existence of origin symmetric solutions for all
p\in(-\infty,\infty)
when the given measure is smooth and even, see Theorem
7.2
. For the horospherical
p
-Christoffel-Minkowski problem, we prove the existence of origin symmetric solutions for
p\in(-n,+\infty)
under appropriate assumption on the given measure, see Theorem
7.3
. In Section
8
, we prove for
p\geq-n
that geodesic balls are the unique solutions to the horospherical
p
-Minkowski problem and the horospherical
p
-Christoffel-Minkowski problem when the given measure is a constant multiple of the standard measure on
\mathbb{S}^{n}
, see Theorem
8.1
. In Section
9
, we use two different methods to prove the Kazdan-Warner type obstructions for the
p=-n
case of the horospherical
p
-Minkowski problem and the horospherical
p
-Christoffel-Minkowski problem, see Theorem
9.1
.

In Section
10
, we propose and study several conjectures about horospherical
p
-Brunn-Minkowski inequalities and horospherical
p
-Minkowski inequalities. The results are summarized in Theorem
10.12
, Theorem
10.13
, and Theorem
10.14
. In Section
11
, we propose several conjectures of weighted geometric inequalities for h-convex bounded domains and prove a class of weighted isoperimetric type inequalities for hypersurfaces in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. In Section
12
, we prove the relationship between the hyperbolic
p
-sum and the Minkowski-Firey
L_{p}
-addition in Theorem
12.2
. Then we prove a Brunn-Minkowski type inequality for h-convex domains along the hyperbolic
p
-sum (
1\leq p\leq 2
), see Theorem
12.4
. We also prove geometric inequalities of Minkowski type for h-convex domains, see Theorem
12.5
.

In Section
13
, we give an intrinsic proof of Lemma
9.2
and another proof of Theorem
4.5
for
1\leq p\leq 2
. Besides, we show that the origin symmetric assumption in Conjecture
11.3
can not be dropped.

Acknowledgments
.

The research of the authors was supported by NSFC grant No. 11831005, NSFC grant No. 12126405 and NSFC-FWO Grant No. 11961131001.

2.
Horospherically convex domains

2.1.
Minkowski space
\mathbb{R}^{n+1,1}
and Hyperbolic space
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}

The Minkowski space
\mathbb{R}^{n+1,1}
is an
(n+2)
-dimensional vector space with the Lorentzian metric

\langle X,Y\rangle=\langle(x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x_{n+1}),(y_{0},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n+1})\rangle=\sum_{i=0}^{n}x_{i}y_{i}-x_{n+1}y_{n+1}.

A vector
X\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1,1}
is called
time-like
,
light-like
or
space-like
if
\langle X,X\rangle<0
,
\langle X,X\rangle=0
or
\langle X,X\rangle>0
respectively. In the following text, we will write a vector
X\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1,1}
as
(x,x_{n+1})
, where
x=(x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. A vector
X
in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1,1}
is called future directed or past directed if
x_{n+1}>0
or
x_{n+1}<0
respectively, and the
future (or past) light cone
of
X
is defined by the set of future (or past) directed light-like vectors issuing from
X
.

The hyperboloid model of the hyperbolic space is given by

\mathbb{H}^{n+1}=\{X=(x,x_{n+1})\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1,1}:\langle X,X\rangle=|x|^{2}-x_{n+1}^{2}=-1,\,x_{n+1}>0\},

and the position vector
X\in\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
is the normal of
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1,1}
. Without additional hypotheses, we always regard the ‘north pole’
N=(0,1)
as the origin of
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. Given two points
X
and
Y
on
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
, the geodesic distance between
X
and
Y
is denoted by
{d}_{\mathbb{H}^{n+1}}\left(X,Y\right)
. Denote by
\mathbb{S}^{n}
the unit sphere. For any
X\in\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
with
r={d}_{\mathbb{H}^{n+1}}\left((0,1),X\right)
, there exists
\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
such that
X=(\sinh r\theta,\cosh r)
, which is exactly the warped product structure of
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
, see Figure
2.1
.

x
x_{n+1}
\sinh r\theta
\cosh r
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
X

Figure 2.1.
Warped product structure of
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}

In this paper, we always assume that
M=\partial\Omega
denotes the boundary of some bounded domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. To distinguish the metrics and the Levi-Civita connections of spaces
\{\mathbb{S}^{n},M,\mathbb{H}^{n+1},\mathbb{R}^{n+1,1}\}
, we set the notations of their connections and metrics as
\left(\mathbb{S}^{n},D,\sigma\right)
,
\left(M,\nabla,g\right)
,
\left(\mathbb{H}^{n+1},\overline{\nabla},\bar{g}\right)
and
\left(\mathbb{R}^{n+1,1},\widetilde{\nabla},\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\right)
. The volume elements of
\mathbb{S}^{n}
,
M
and
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
are denoted by
d\sigma
,
d\mu
and
dv
respectively.
For any smooth function
f
on
\mathbb{S}^{n}
and any local orthonormal frame
\{e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{n}\}
on
\mathbb{S}^{n}
, we use the notations
f_{i}:=D_{i}f
,
f_{ij}:=D_{j}D_{i}f
to denote the covariant derivatives of
f
with respect to the standard metric of
\mathbb{S}^{n}
in the context. For any hypersurface
M\subset\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
, we use
\nu
to denote the outward unit normal of
M
and use
h_{ij}
to denote the second fundamental form of
M\subset\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. The Weingarten matrix
\mathcal{W}=(h_{i}{}^{j})
is given by
h_{i}{}^{j}=g^{jk}h_{kj}
. At last, we remark that the notations with superscript
\widehat{\cdot}
are stand for geometric objects and geometric quantities in Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, e.g.
\left(\widehat{M},\widehat{\nabla},\widehat{g}\right)
denotes a hypersurface
\widehat{M}
with connection
\widehat{\nabla}
and metric
\widehat{g}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
.

2.2.
Horospheres and Horo-balls

Now we study the
horospheres
in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
, which are the spheres centered at infinity
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}_{\infty}
and with the constant principal curvatures equal to
1
everywhere. Then
\nabla_{i}\left(X-\nu\right)=\left(\delta_{i}{}^{j}-h_{i}{}^{j}\right)\partial_{j}X=0
on any horosphere
H
, which implies that
X-\nu
is a constant vector on
H
. Since
\langle X,X\rangle=-1
,
\langle\nu,\nu\rangle=1
and
\langle X,\nu\rangle=0
, we have
\langle X-\nu,X-\nu\rangle=0
. Then there exist a constant
\lambda
and a unit vector
z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
such that
X-\nu=\lambda(z,1)
on
H
and hence

(2.1)

-1=\langle X-\nu,X\rangle=\lambda\langle X,(z,1)\rangle.

Since
\langle X,(z,1)\rangle=-x_{n+1}+\langle x,z\rangle\leq-\sqrt{|x|^{2}+1}+|x|<0
, we have that
\lambda
is positive. Let
\lambda=e^{-r}
.
From now on, we denote this horosphere
H
as
H_{z}(r)
. Then

(2.2)

X-\nu=e^{-r}(z,1)

on
H_{z}(r)
. Now we explain the geometric interpretation of
r
. The above formula (
2.1
) implies that
H_{z}(r)
is the intersection of the hyperplane
\{X\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1,1}:\langle X,(z,1)\rangle=-e^{r}\}
and the hyperboloid
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1,1}
. Suppose that
X\in H_{z}(r)
with

(2.3)

X=(\sinh s\theta,\cosh s),

where
\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
and
s=d_{\mathbb{H}^{n+1}}\left((0,1),X\right)
. If
r\geq 0
, then

-e^{r}=\langle X,(z,1)\rangle=\sinh s\langle\theta,z\rangle-\cosh s\geq-e^{s}.

Therefore,
d_{\mathbb{H}^{n+1}}((0,1),X)=s\geq r
for any
X\in H_{z}(r)
, and equality holds if and only if
X=(-\sinh rz,\cosh r)\in H_{z}(r)
. If
r\leq 0
, then

-e^{r}=\langle X,(z,1)\rangle=\sinh s\langle\theta,z\rangle-\cosh s\leq-e^{-s}.

Therefore
d_{\mathbb{H}^{n+1}}((0,1),X)=s\geq-r
for any
X\in H_{z}(r)
, and equality holds if and only if
X=(-\sinh rz,\cosh r)\in H_{z}(r)
. Hence
r
represents the signed geodesic distance from the ‘north pole’
N=(0,1)
of
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
to
H_{z}(r)
. We conclude that the horosphere
H_{z}(r)
is given by

(2.4)

H_{z}(r)=\{X\in\mathbb{H}^{n+1}:\log\left(-\langle X,(z,1)\rangle\right)=r\},

where
r
is the signed geodesic distance from
N=(0,1)
to
H_{z}(r)
. We define the
horo-ball

B_{z}(r)
bounded by
H_{z}(r)
by
\{X\in\mathbb{H}^{n+1}:\log(-\langle X,(z,1)\rangle)<r\}
, i.e.

(2.5)

B_{z}(r)=\{X\in\mathbb{H}^{n+1}:0>\langle X,(z,1)\rangle>-e^{r}\}.

If we use the Poincaré ball model
\mathbb{B}^{n+1}
of
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
, then
B_{z}(r)
corresponds to an
(n+1)
-dimensional ball which tangents to
\partial\mathbb{B}^{n+1}
at
z
, see Figure
2.2
. Furthermore, if
r>0
, then
B_{z}(r)
contains the origin in its interior.

\mathbb{B}^{n+1}
z
r
B_{z}(r)
H_{z}(r)

Figure 2.2.
Horosphere
H_{z}(r)
and Horo-ball
B_{z}(r)

2.3.
Horospherical Gauss map and Horospherical support function

Comparing the above arguments in Subsection
2.2
with the objects in the classical convex geometry in Euclidean space, we have defined the “hyperplane”
H_{z}(r)
and the “half space”
B_{z}(r)
in the hyperbolic setting. In the next step, we will give the definitions of “convex bodies” and “Gauss map” in the hyperbolic setting.

A bounded domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
(or its boundary
\partial\Omega
) is called
horospherically convex
(or
h-convex
for short) if for every
X\in\partial\Omega
there exists a horo-ball
B
such that
\Omega\subset B
and
X\in\Omega\cap B
. When
\Omega
is smooth, the horospherical convexity (h-convexity for short) of
\Omega
implies that the principal curvatures are greater than or equal to
1
on
\partial\Omega
. For a smooth bounded domain
\Omega
, we say
\Omega
(or
\partial\Omega
) is
uniformly h-convex
if the principal curvatures are greater than
1+\delta
for some
\delta>0
everywhere on
\partial\Omega
.

Now we define the
horospherical Gauss map

G:M\to\mathbb{S}^{n}
for a smooth hypersurface
M=\partial\Omega
in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
, see
[
20
,
25
]
. For each
p\in M
, we know from (
2.1
) that
X(p)-\nu(p)=\lambda_{p}(z_{p},1)
for some
\lambda_{p}>0
and
z_{p}\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
. Then we define the horospherical Gauss map
G
at
p
of
M
by
G(p)=z_{p}
. From (
2.2
), it is simple to see
G\left(H_{z}(r)\right)=z
.

Note that the horospherical Gauss map defined in this paper is the same as that in
[
3
]
. In some other papers (see e.g.,
[
26
]
), one may define the horospherical Gauss map “
G(p)=z^{\prime}_{p}
” by using “
X(p)+\nu(p)=\lambda^{\prime}_{p}(z^{\prime}_{p},1)
”, which means that the horospherical Gauss map is defined locally on
M
, and
\nu
need not be the “outward” unit normal. However, since we deal with the domain which are intersections of horo-balls (not intersections of complements of horo-balls), the boundary of the domain is oriented, and hence we can choose
\nu
to be the outward unit normal.

Let
\Omega
be a bounded domain in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. The
horospherical support function

u_{\Omega}:\mathbb{S}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
of
\Omega
is defined by (see Figure
2.3
)

u_{\Omega}(z):=\inf\{s\in\mathbb{R}:\Omega\subset\overline{B}_{z}(s)\},\quad z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}.

By (
2.5
), that is equivalent to

(2.6)

u_{\Omega}(z)=\sup\{\log\left(-\langle X,(z,1)\rangle\right):X\in\Omega\}.

We will write
u(z)
for
u_{\Omega}(z)
if there is no confusion about the choice of the domain.

z
\Omega
u(z)

Figure 2.3.
Horo-convex bounded domain
\Omega

Assume that
\Omega
is a h-convex bounded domain, and let
M=\partial\Omega
. If
X\in H_{z}(u(z))\cap M
for some
z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
, then

(2.7)

\langle X,(z,1)\rangle=-e^{u(z)}=-\varphi(z),

where we defined
\varphi(z):=e^{u(z)}
. We call
H_{z}(u(z))
the
supporting horosphere
of
\Omega
at
X
. Furthermore, if
M
is smooth, then (
2.2
) implies

(2.8)

X-\nu=e^{-u(z)}(z,1)=\frac{1}{\varphi(z)}(z,1).

When
M=\partial\Omega
is smooth and uniformly h-convex, we can choose an orthonormal frame
\{\partial_{1},\ldots,\partial_{n}\}
at
p\in M
such that the Weingarten matrix
\mathcal{W}=\left(h_{i}{}^{j}\right)
is diagonal at
p
, i.e.
h_{i}{}^{j}=\kappa_{i}\delta_{i}{}^{j}
and
\kappa_{i}>1
. Then we have
\partial_{i}\left(X-\nu\right)(p)=(1-\kappa_{i})\partial_{i}\neq 0
. Hence
\ker T_{p}G\subset T_{p}M\cap\mathbb{R}(z_{p},1)=0
, which means that the horospherical Gauss map
G
is a locally diffeomorphism. Therefore, we can parameterize
M
by
\mathbb{S}^{n}
locally via the inverse of horospherical Gauss map
G^{-1}
when
M
is smooth and uniformly h-convex, see
[
3
]
.

In the following lemma, we recover
\partial\Omega
by using the horospherical support function
u(z)
of the smooth uniformly h-convex bounded domain
\Omega
(see
[
3
,
26
]
).

Lemma 2.1
.

Assume that
M=\partial\Omega
is a smooth uniformly h-convex hypersurface in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
with horospherical support function
u(z)
. Then for each
z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
,
M\cap H_{z}(u(z))
only contains one point, which we denote it as
X(z)
. We let
\nu(z)
denote the outward unit normal of
M\subset\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
at
X(z)
and choose normal coordinates for
\mathbb{S}^{n}
around
z
such that the coordinate frame
\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}\}
is an orthonormal basis at
z
. Then we have

(2.9)

\displaystyle X(z)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\varphi(-z,1)+\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{|D\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}+\frac{1}{\varphi}\right)(z,1)-(D\varphi,0),

(2.10)

\displaystyle\nu(z)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\varphi(-z,1)+\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{|D\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}-\frac{1}{\varphi}\right)(z,1)-(D\varphi,0),

(2.11)

\displaystyle\partial_{i}X(z)=

\displaystyle-\sum_{j=1}^{n}A_{ij}[\varphi](e_{j},0)+\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{n}A_{ij}[\varphi]D_{j}\varphi}{\varphi}(z,1),

where we used
z
to parameterize
M
locally,
\varphi(z):=e^{u(z)}
and

(2.12)

A_{ij}[\varphi]:=\varphi_{ij}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{|D\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}\sigma_{ij}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\varphi-\frac{1}{\varphi}\right)\sigma_{ij}.

Proof.

For any
z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
, vectors
\{(-z,1),(z,1),(e_{1},0),\ldots,(e_{n},0)\}
form a basis of
\mathbb{R}^{n+1,1}
. Then for any
X\in H_{z}(u(z))\cap M
, we can assume that

X=\alpha(-z,1)+\beta(z,1)+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\gamma_{j}(e_{j},0),

where
\alpha
,
\beta
and
\gamma_{j}
are real numbers. By (
2.7
), we have
-2\alpha=-e^{u}
, which induces
\alpha=\frac{1}{2}e^{u}
. Differentiating (
2.7
) with respect to
e_{i}
, we obtain

\langle\partial_{i}X,(z,1)\rangle+\langle X,(e_{i},0)\rangle=-D_{i}\varphi.

This together with the fact
\langle\partial_{i}X,X-\nu\rangle=0
and (
2.8
) gives

\langle X,(e_{i},0)\rangle=-D_{i}\varphi-\varphi\langle\partial_{i}X,X-\nu\rangle=-D_{i}\varphi.

Therefore
\gamma_{j}=-D_{j}\varphi
. Now we arrive at

X=\frac{1}{2}\varphi(-z,1)+\beta(z,1)-(D\varphi,0).

Because of
X\in\mathbb{H}^{n+1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1,1}
, we have

-1=\langle X,X\rangle=|D\varphi|^{2}+\beta\varphi\langle(z,1),(-z,1)\rangle=|D\varphi|^{2}-2\beta\varphi,

which induces
\beta=\frac{1}{2}\frac{|D\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}+\frac{1}{2\varphi}
. Then

X=\frac{1}{2}\varphi(-z,1)+\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{|D\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}+\frac{1}{\varphi}\right)(z,1)-(D\varphi,0).

It follows that
X(z)
is uniquely determined by
\varphi(z)
, and (
2.9
) is proved. Formula (
2.10
) follows by substituting (
2.9
) into (
2.8
).

Differentiating (
2.9
) with respect to
e_{i}
, we have

\displaystyle\partial_{i}X=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\partial_{i}\left(\varphi(-z,1)\right)+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{i}\left(\left(\frac{|D\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}+\frac{1}{\varphi}\right)(z,1)\right)-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\partial_{i}\left(\varphi_{j}e_{j},0\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\varphi_{i}(-z,1)-\frac{1}{2}\varphi(e_{i},0)+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{i}\left(\frac{|D\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}+\frac{1}{\varphi}\right)(z,1)+\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{|D\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}+\frac{1}{\varphi}\right)(e_{i},0)

\displaystyle-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\varphi_{ij}\left(e_{j},0\right)+\varphi_{i}(z,0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(\varphi_{ij}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{|D\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}\delta_{ij}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\varphi-\frac{1}{\varphi}\right)\delta_{ij}\right)\left(e_{j},0\right)+\partial_{i}\left(\frac{1}{2}\frac{|D\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\varphi+\frac{1}{\varphi}\right)\right)(z,1)

(2.13)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sum_{j=1}^{n}A_{ij}[\varphi](e_{j},0)+\partial_{i}\left(\frac{1}{2}\frac{|D\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\varphi+\frac{1}{\varphi}\right)\right)(z,1),

where we used
\partial_{i}(e_{j},0)=-\delta_{ij}(z,0)
in the second equality. A direct calculation shows

\displaystyle\partial_{i}\left(\frac{1}{2}\frac{|D\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\varphi+\frac{1}{\varphi}\right)\right)=

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{n}\varphi_{j}\varphi_{ji}}{\varphi}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{|D\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi^{2}}\varphi_{i}+\frac{1}{2}\varphi_{i}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\varphi_{i}}{\varphi^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\varphi_{j}}{\varphi}\left(\varphi_{ij}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{|D\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}\delta_{ij}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\varphi-\frac{1}{\varphi}\right)\delta_{ij}\right)

(2.14)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{A_{ij}[\varphi]\varphi_{j}}{\varphi}.

Then (
2.11
) follows by substituting (
2.14
) into (
2.13
). We complete the proof of Lemma
2.1
.
∎

As we have proved that
G
is bijective and locally diffeomorphic for the smooth closed and uniformly h-convex hypersurface
M
, we have that
G:M\to\mathbb{S}^{n}
is a diffeomorphism. In the context, we always assume that
M
is parameterized by the unit sphere
\mathbb{S}^{n}
, i.e., the image of the horospherical Gauss map.

In the following lemma, we show the geometric property of
A_{ij}[\varphi(z)]
defined in (
2.12
).

Lemma 2.2
.

Using the same notations and assumptions as in Lemma
2.1
,
it holds that

(2.15)

(h_{i}{}^{j}-\delta_{i}{}^{j})A_{jk}[\varphi(z)]=\frac{1}{\varphi(z)}\sigma_{ik},

where
\left(h_{i}{}^{j}\right)
was evaluated at
X(z)\in M
. The above formula (
2.15
) shows that, if a smooth closed hypersurface
M
is uniformly h-convex, then
A_{ij}[\varphi(z)]>0
on
\mathbb{S}^{n}
.

Proof.

Differentiating (
2.8
) with respect to
e_{i}
, we have

\partial_{i}X-\partial_{i}\nu=-\frac{\varphi_{i}}{\varphi^{2}}(z,1)+\frac{1}{\varphi}(e_{i},0).

Taking the inner product of the both sides with
(e_{k},0)
gives

(h_{i}{}^{j}-\delta_{i}{}^{j})\langle-\partial_{j}X,(e_{k},0)\rangle=\frac{1}{\varphi}\sigma_{ik}.

Then (
2.15
) follows by substituting (
2.11
) into the above formula. We complete the proof of Lemma
2.2
.
∎

Corollary 2.1
.

Let
M
be a smooth uniformly h-convex hypersurface in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. Then, via the horospherical Gauss map
G:M\to\mathbb{S}^{n}
, we have

(2.16)

d\mu=\det A[\varphi(z)]d\sigma,

where
d\mu
denoted the area element of
M
at
X(z)=G^{-1}(z)
,
d\sigma
denoted the area element of
\mathbb{S}^{n}
at
z
, and
A[\varphi(z)]
was defined in (
2.12
).

Proof.

Since
\langle(z,1),(z,1)\rangle=0
and
\langle(z,1),(e_{i},0)\rangle=0
for any
e_{i}\in T_{z}\mathbb{S}^{n}
, formula (
2.11
) implies

(2.17)

g_{ij}:=\langle\partial_{i}X,\partial_{j}X\rangle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}A_{ik}[\varphi]A_{kj}[\varphi].

Since we have proved that
A_{ij}[\varphi]
is positive definite in Lemma
2.2
, the area element of
M
can be given by

d\mu=\sqrt{\det g_{ij}}d\sigma=\sqrt{\det A^{2}[\varphi]}d\sigma=\det A[\varphi]d\sigma.

Thus we proved formula (
2.16
).
∎

For the following Corollary
2.2
, readers may refer to
[
3
, Section 5.4]
.

Corollary 2.2
.

Given a smooth function
u(z)
defined on
\mathbb{S}^{n}
, we let
\varphi(z)=e^{u(z)}
. Then
u(z)
is the horospherical support function of some smooth uniformly h-convex bounded domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
if and only if

A_{ij}[\varphi(z)]>0
on
\mathbb{S}^{n}
. Furthermore, domain
\Omega
is uniquely determined by
u(z)
.

Proof.

The uniqueness of
\Omega
follows from Lemma
2.1
. Hence we only need to prove the existence of such a domain
\Omega
with horospherical support function
u(z)
. We let
X(z)
be defined as in (
2.9
). From (
2.17
), the tensor
g_{ij}=\langle\partial_{i}X,\partial_{j}X\rangle
is positive definite provided that
A_{ij}[\varphi]
is non-singular. Hence the map
X(z)
is an immersion from
\mathbb{S}^{n}
to
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
with normal vector field
\nu(z)
. Note that (
2.15
) implies that
\mathcal{W}-I=\left(h_{i}{}^{j}-\delta_{i}{}^{j}\right)
is positive definite at
X(z)
if and only if
A_{ij}[\varphi]
is positive definite at
z
. Thus, it suffices to show that
X(z)
is an embedding provided that
A_{ij}[\varphi(z)]>0
.

When
n\geq 2
, the Gauss equation implies that the sectional curvatures of the range of
X(z)
are positive provided that
\mathcal{W}-I>0
. Then we know that
X(z)
is an embedding by a theorem of do Carmo and Warner
[
24
, Section 5]
.

When
n=1
, we identify
z\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
with
t\in[0,2\pi)
by
z=(\cos t,\sin t)
. Let
\gamma
be the closed curve generated by
X(t)
, and let
\widehat{\gamma}
be the planar curve generated by
x(t)
, where
x(t)=(x_{0}(t),x_{1}(t))
is the spacial component of
X(t)
. Define
\widehat{\nu}(t)
by

\widehat{\nu}(t)=\frac{-\cos t\left(1,\frac{\varphi^{\prime}}{\varphi}\right)+\sin t\left(\frac{\varphi^{\prime}}{\varphi},-1\right)}{\left(1+\left(\frac{\varphi^{\prime}}{\varphi}\right)^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}}.

Formula (
2.11
) shows that

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}x(t)=

\displaystyle-A[\varphi]\frac{d}{dt}z+A[\varphi]\frac{\varphi^{\prime}}{\varphi}z

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-A[\varphi](-\sin t,\cos t)+A[\varphi]\frac{\varphi^{\prime}}{\varphi}(\cos t,\sin t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle A[\varphi]\cos t\left(\frac{\varphi^{\prime}}{\varphi},-1\right)+A[\varphi]\sin t\left(1,\frac{\varphi^{\prime}}{\varphi}\right).

Thus
\left|\frac{d}{dt}x(t)\right|=A[\varphi]\left(1+(\frac{\varphi^{\prime}}{\varphi})^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}
, and
\widehat{\nu}(t)
is the outward unit normal of
\widehat{\gamma}
at
x(t)
. Then the curvature of
\widehat{\gamma}
at
x(t)
is given by

(2.18)

\widehat{\kappa}(t)=-\langle\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}x(t),\widehat{\nu}(t)\rangle\left|\frac{d}{dt}x(t)\right|^{-2}=\frac{A[\varphi]\left(\left(\frac{\varphi^{\prime}}{\varphi}\right)^{\prime}+\left(\frac{\varphi^{\prime}}{\varphi}\right)^{2}+1\right)}{A[\varphi]^{2}\left(1+(\frac{\varphi^{\prime}}{\varphi})^{2}\right)^{\frac{3}{2}}}=\frac{\varphi^{\prime\prime}+\varphi}{\varphi A[\varphi]\left(1+\left(\frac{\varphi^{\prime}}{\varphi}\right)^{2}\right)^{\frac{3}{2}}}.

By (
2.12
), the assumption
A[\varphi]>0
implies

\varphi^{\prime\prime}+\varphi>\frac{1}{2}\frac{\left(\varphi^{\prime}\right)^{2}}{\varphi}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\varphi+\varphi^{-1}\right)>0.

Hence
\widehat{\kappa}(t)>0
for all
t\in[0,2\pi)
. Then by using (
2.18
), we have

\displaystyle\int_{\gamma}|\widehat{\kappa}|d\widehat{\mu}=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}\widehat{\kappa}\left|\frac{d}{dt}x(t)\right|dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{\varphi^{\prime\prime}+\varphi}{\varphi\left(1+\left(\frac{\varphi^{\prime}}{\varphi}\right)^{2}\right)}dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{1+\left(\frac{\varphi^{\prime}}{\varphi}\right)^{2}+\left(\frac{\varphi^{\prime}}{\varphi}\right)^{\prime}}{1+\left(\frac{\varphi^{\prime}}{\varphi}\right)^{2}}dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\pi+\left.\arctan\left(\frac{\varphi^{\prime}(t)}{\varphi(t)}\right)\right|_{t=0}^{t=2\pi}=2\pi.

Then Fenchel’s theorem
[
28
]
implies that
\widehat{\gamma}
must be an embedded, closed convex curve in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, in particular,
\widehat{\gamma}
is a simple closed curve. Since the hyperboloid model of
\mathbb{H}^{2}
can be viewed as a graph defined on
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\{0\}
in
\mathbb{R}^{2,1}
, it is easy to see that the vertical projection gives a diffeomorphism from
\mathbb{H}^{2}
to
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and maps
\gamma
to
\widehat{\gamma}
. Consequently, the curve
\gamma
is an embedding from
\mathbb{S}^{1}
to
\mathbb{H}^{2}
.

Therefore, we conclude that
X(z)
is an embedding from
\mathbb{S}^{n}
to the boundary of a uniformly h-convex bounded domain
\Omega
if and only if
A_{ij}[\varphi]>0
on
\mathbb{S}^{n}
. This completes the proof of Corollary
2.2
.
∎

2.4.
Horospherical Wulff shape

In Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, the Wulff shape is defined by the intersection of a family of half spaces, which are determined by a given positive continuous function on
\mathbb{S}^{n}
. In hyperbolic space
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
, by replacing the half spaces by horo-balls in the above argument, we can define the
horospherical Wulff shape
of a continuous function
u(z)
defined on
\mathbb{S}^{n}
. Remark that we do not require
u(z)
to be positive in the following definition. Recall the definition of
B_{z}(r)
in (
2.5
).

Definition 2.1
.

Given any continuous function
u(z)
defined on
\mathbb{S}^{n}
, the horospherical Aleksandrov body (or horospherical Wulff shape) of
u(z)
is defined by

\Omega:=\bigcap_{z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}}\overline{B}_{z}(u(z))=\bigcap_{z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}}\{X\in\mathbb{H}^{n+1}:\log\left(-\langle X,z,1\rangle\right)\leq u(z)\}.

Now we use Definition
2.1
to characterize points in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
.

Lemma 2.3
.

Given a smooth function
u(z)
defined on
\mathbb{S}^{n}
, we let
\varphi(z)=e^{u(z)}
. Then
u(z)
is the horospherical support function of a point
X
in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
if and only if
A_{ij}[\varphi(z)]=0
. Furthermore,
X
is the horospherical Wulff shape of
u(z)
.

Proof.

Assume that
u(z)
is a smooth function that satisfies
A_{ij}[\varphi(z)]=0
for all
z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
. Let us prove that
\cap_{z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}}\overline{B}_{z}(u(z))
is a point in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
.

Let
X(z)
be a vector-valued function on
\mathbb{S}^{n}
defined by expression (
2.9
). Since
A_{ij}[\varphi]=0
, formula (
2.11
) shows that
X(z)
is constant, and we denote it by
X=(x,x_{n+1})
. It is easy to check that

\langle-X,(z,1)\rangle=\langle-X(z),(z,1)\rangle=\varphi(z).

Thus
u(z)
is the horospherical support function of
X
, and then
X\in\cap_{z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}}\overline{B}_{z}(u(z))
.

If there exists a point
X^{\prime}=(x^{\prime},x_{n+1}^{\prime})
in
\cap_{z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}}\overline{B}_{z}(u(z))
with
X^{\prime}\neq X
, then we let
z^{\prime}=\frac{x-x^{\prime}}{|x-x^{\prime}|}
. Hence

\displaystyle\langle-X^{\prime},(z^{\prime},1)\rangle-\langle-X,(z^{\prime},1)\rangle=

\displaystyle\langle X-X^{\prime},(z^{\prime},1)\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle x-x^{\prime},z^{\prime}\rangle-(x_{n+1}-x_{n+1}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\langle x-x^{\prime},z^{\prime}\rangle-|x_{n+1}-x_{n+1}^{\prime}|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|x-x^{\prime}|-\left|\left(|x|^{2}+1\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}-\left(|x^{\prime}|^{2}+1\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right|

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\left||x|-|x^{\prime}|\right|\left(1-\frac{|x|+|x^{\prime}|}{\left(|x|^{2}+1\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\left(|x^{\prime}|^{2}+1\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\right)>0.

Consequently,

\langle-X^{\prime},(z^{\prime},1)\rangle>\langle-X,(z^{\prime},1)\rangle=\varphi(z^{\prime}),

which contradicts the assumption
X^{\prime}\in\cap_{z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}}\overline{B}_{z}(u(z))
. Thus
X=\cap_{z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}}\overline{B}_{z}(u(z))
.

On the other hand, for any point
X=(x,x_{n+1})\in\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
, let us prove that
A_{ij}[\varphi_{X}]=0
.

Using (
2.7
), we have

\varphi_{X}(z)=\langle-X,(z,1)\rangle=-\langle x,z\rangle+x_{n+1}.

Then

(2.19)

D_{i}\varphi_{X}=-\langle x,e_{i}\rangle,\quad D_{j}D_{i}\varphi_{X}=-D_{j}\langle x,e_{i}\rangle=\langle x,z\rangle\delta_{ij}.

Therefore

\displaystyle A_{ij}[\varphi_{X}(z)]=

\displaystyle D_{j}D_{i}\varphi_{X}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{|D\varphi_{X}|^{2}}{\varphi_{X}}\delta_{ij}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\varphi_{X}-\frac{1}{\varphi_{X}}\right)\delta_{ij}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle x,z\rangle\delta_{ij}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle x,e_{i}\rangle^{2}}{-\langle x,z\rangle+x_{n+1}}\delta_{ij}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\left(-\langle x,z\rangle+x_{n+1}\right)-\frac{1}{-\langle x,z\rangle+x_{n+1}}\right)\delta_{ij}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle x,z\rangle\delta_{ij}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{|x|^{2}-\langle x,z\rangle^{2}+1}{-\langle x,z\rangle+x_{n+1}}\delta_{ij}+\frac{1}{2}\left(-\langle x,z\rangle+x_{n+1}\right)\delta_{ij}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle x,z\rangle\delta_{ij}-\frac{1}{2}\left(\langle x,z\rangle+x_{n+1}\right)\delta_{ij}+\frac{1}{2}\left(-\langle x,z\rangle+x_{n+1}\right)\delta_{ij}=0,

where we used
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle x,e_{i}\rangle^{2}=|x|^{2}-\langle x,z\rangle^{2}
in the third equality and used
|x|^{2}+1=x_{n+1}^{2}
in the last equality. Hence we obtain the desired equality
A_{ij}[\varphi_{X}(z)]=0
for any point
X\in\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
.

We complete the proof of Lemma
2.3
.
∎

Without the smooth assumption of the domain
\Omega
in Lemma
2.1
, we will show in the following Lemma
2.4
that the expression (
2.9
) is also valid to describe
\partial\Omega
.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
\Omega
be a h-convex bounded domain with smooth horospherical support function
u(z)
.
Then the expression (
2.9
) gives a surjective map from
\mathbb{S}^{n}
to
\partial\Omega
.

Proof.

By the h-convexity of
\Omega
, for any
Y\in\partial\Omega
, there exists
z_{Y}\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
such that
Y\in\partial\Omega\cap H_{z_{Y}}\left(u(z_{Y})\right)
. By use of the expression (
2.9
), let us prove
Y=X(z_{Y})
.

By using (
2.4
) and (
2.5
), we have
\langle-Y,(z_{Y},1)\rangle=\varphi(z_{Y})
, and
\langle-Y,(z,1)\rangle\leq\varphi(z)
for all
z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
. Hence

\varphi(z)\geq\varphi(z_{Y})+\langle-Y,(z-z_{Y},0)\rangle,\quad\forall\ z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}.

This together with the smoothness of
\varphi(z)
deduces that the projection of
-Y
on
T_{z_{Y}}\mathbb{S}^{n}
is exactly
D\varphi(z_{Y})
. Hence we can assume

(2.20)

Y=(y,y_{n+1})=\left(-D\varphi(z_{Y})+\alpha z_{Y},\beta\right)

for some constants
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
and
\beta\geq 1
. Since
Y\in H_{z_{Y}}\left(u(z_{Y})\right)
, we have from (
2.7
) that

\varphi(z_{Y})=-\langle Y,(z_{Y},1)\rangle=\beta-\alpha.

Besides, the fact
Y\in\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
implies

-1=\langle Y,Y\rangle=|D\varphi(z_{Y})|^{2}+\alpha^{2}-\beta^{2}.

Solving the above two equations gives

\alpha=\frac{1}{2}\frac{|D\varphi(z_{Y})|^{2}}{\varphi(z_{Y})}-\frac{1}{2}\left(\varphi(z_{Y})-\frac{1}{\varphi(z_{Y})}\right),\quad\beta=\frac{1}{2}\frac{|D\varphi(z_{Y})|^{2}}{\varphi(z_{Y})}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\varphi(z_{Y})+\frac{1}{\varphi(z_{Y})}\right).

Substituting the above
\alpha,\beta
into (
2.20
) and using expression (
2.9
), we have
Y=X(z_{Y})
. Consequently, we obtain
\partial\Omega\subset X(\mathbb{S}^{n})
.

Now, it suffices to show
X(\mathbb{S}^{n})\subset\partial\Omega
. For any
z_{0}\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
, there exists
Y_{0}\in\partial\Omega\cap H_{z_{0}}(u(z_{0}))
. The above argument also shows
Y_{0}=X(z_{0})
. Thus, we have that the image of expression (
2.9
) lies on
\partial\Omega
.

Putting the above facts together, we complete the proof of Lemma
2.4
.
∎

We are now in a position to show that the domain
\Omega
in Corollary
2.2
is exactly the horospherical Wulff shape of
u(z)
.

Corollary 2.3
.

Let
\Omega
be a h-convex bounded domain with smooth horospherical support function
u(z)
.
Assume that
A_{ij}[\varphi(z)]>0
for all
z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
, where
\varphi(z)=e^{u(z)}
. Then
\Omega=\cap_{z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}}\overline{B}_{z}(u(z))
, and expression (
2.9
) gives a diffeomorphism from
\mathbb{S}^{n}
to
\partial\Omega
.

Proof.

By Corollary
2.2
, we can let
\widetilde{\Omega}
denote the unique smooth uniformly h-convex bounded domain with horospherical support function
u(z)
. By (
2.6
), we have
\widetilde{\Omega}\subset\cap_{z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}}\overline{B}_{z}(u(z))
. Consequently, the horospherical support function of
\cap_{z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}}\overline{B}_{z}(u(z))
is exactly
u(z)
. So far, the domains
\Omega
,
\cap_{z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}}\overline{B}_{z}(u(z))
and
\widetilde{\Omega}
are equipped with the same smooth horospherical support function
u(z)
. Then we can apply Lemma
2.4
to get

\Omega=\cap_{z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}}\overline{B}_{z}(u(z))=\widetilde{\Omega}.

Therefore, Corollary
2.3
follows from Corollary
2.2
.
∎

In the following Proposition
2.1
, we will extend the assumption
A_{ij}[\varphi(z)]>0
in Corollary
2.3
to
A_{ij}[\varphi(z)]\geq 0
, and we will prove the
1
-
1
correspondence between smooth function
u(z)
satisfying
A_{ij}[\varphi(z)]\geq 0
and the h-convex domain with smooth horospherical support function
u(z)
.

Proposition 2.1
.

Let
u(z)
be a smooth function on
\mathbb{S}^{n}
and
\varphi(z)=e^{u(z)}
. Assume that
A_{ij}[\varphi(z)]\geq 0
for all
z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
. Then the horospherical Wulff shape
\cap_{z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}}\overline{B}_{z}(u(z))
is the unique h-convex domain with horospherical support function
u(z)
. Furthermore, expression (
2.9
) gives a surjective map from
\mathbb{S}^{n}
to
\partial\left(\cap_{z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}}\overline{B}_{z}(u(z))\right)
.

Proof.

For each integer
k\geq 1
, we define
u_{k}(z):=\log\left(\varphi(z)+\frac{1}{k}\right)
. Then
\varphi_{k}(z):=e^{u_{k}(z)}=\varphi(z)+\frac{1}{k}
converges smoothly and uniformly to
\varphi(z)
as
k\to+\infty
. By a direct calculation, we have

\displaystyle A_{ij}[\varphi_{k}(z)]=

\displaystyle\left(\varphi_{k}\right)_{ij}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{|D\varphi_{k}|^{2}}{\varphi_{k}}\delta_{ij}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\varphi_{k}-\frac{1}{\varphi_{k}}\right)\delta_{ij}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varphi_{ij}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{|D\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi+\frac{1}{k}}\delta_{ij}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\varphi+\frac{1}{k}-\frac{1}{\varphi+\frac{1}{k}}\delta_{ij}\right)

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle\varphi_{ij}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{|D\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}\delta_{ij}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\varphi-\frac{1}{\varphi}\right)\delta_{ij}

(2.21)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle A_{ij}[\varphi(z)]=0.

For any integer
k\geq 1
, we know from Corollary
2.2
that
u_{k}(z)
uniquely determines a smooth uniformly h-convex bounded domain
\Omega_{k}
. At the same time, Corollary
2.3
implies that
\Omega_{k}=\cap_{z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}}\overline{B}_{z}(u_{k}(z))
.

Let
\Omega:=\cap_{z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}}\overline{B}_{z}(u(z))
. We will show that the horospherical support function of
\Omega
is
u(z)
.

By the above argument, we have

\Omega=\bigcap_{z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}}\overline{B}_{z}(u(z))=\bigcap_{z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}}\bigcap_{k=1}^{+\infty}\overline{B}_{z}(u_{k}(z))=\bigcap_{k=1}^{+\infty}\bigcap_{z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}}\overline{B}_{z}(u_{k}(z))=\bigcap_{k=1}^{+\infty}\Omega_{k}.

Since
\Omega_{i}\subset\Omega_{j}
for all
i>j\geq 1
,
\Omega
is nonempty. Replacing
\varphi(z)
and
X(z)
by
\varphi_{k}(z)
and
X_{k}(z)
respectively in expression (
2.9
), we know that
\partial\Omega_{k}
is parameterized by
X_{k}(z)
. Taking
k\to+\infty
and by the definition of
\varphi_{k}(z)
, we have that
X_{k}(z)
converges smoothly and uniformly to
X(z)
for all
z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
, and hence
X(z)\in\Omega
. Besides, it is easy to see that
\langle-X(z),(z,1)\rangle=\varphi(z)
, which implies that the horospherical support function of
\Omega
is
u(z)
. Note that Lemma
2.4
implies that the above
\Omega
is the unique h-convex domain with horospherical support function
u(z)
. This completes the proof of Proposition
2.1
.
∎

3.
Horospherically convex hypersurfaces

3.1.
Symmetric functions

For a symmetric function
s
defined on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, there is a
GL(n)
-invariant function
S
defined on the space of
n\times n
symmetric matrices
{\rm Sym}(n)
, such that
s\left(\lambda\left(A\right)\right)=S\left(A\right)
, where
\lambda\left(A\right)=\left(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n}\right)
are the eigenvalues of
A
. Then we can define

\dot{S}^{pq}=\frac{\partial S}{\partial A_{pq}},\quad\ddot{S}^{pq,rs}=\frac{\partial^{2}S}{\partial A_{rs}\partial A_{pq}}.

At
A={\rm diag}(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n})
, we have

(3.1)

\dot{S}^{pq}=\frac{\partial s}{\partial\lambda_{p}}\delta_{p}{}^{q}.

In the context, we will write
S
for
s
if there is no confusion.

Let
n\geq 1
and
1\leq m\leq n
be integers, and let
\lambda=\left(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n}\right)
be a point in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. The normalized
m
-th elementary symmetric function
p_{m}
is defined by

p_{m}(\lambda):=\frac{1}{C_{n}^{m}}\sigma_{m}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{C_{n}^{m}}\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{m}\leq n}\lambda_{i_{1}}\cdots\lambda_{i_{m}}.

It is convenience to set
p_{0}(\lambda)=1
. By the above argument,
p_{m}
induces a function
p_{m}(A):=p_{m}(\lambda(A))
defined on
{\rm Sym}(n)
. The following Lemma
3.1
can be found in
[
45
]
.

Lemma 3.1
.

If
1\leq m\leq n
, then

(3.2)

\displaystyle\dot{p}_{m}^{ij}\delta_{i}{}^{j}=mp_{m-1},

(3.3)

\displaystyle\dot{p}_{m}^{ij}A_{ij}=mp_{m}.

If
0\leq k<l\leq n
and
G(A):=\left(\frac{p_{l}\left(\lambda(A)\right)}{p_{k}\left(\lambda(A)\right)}\right)^{\frac{1}{l-k}}
, then

(3.4)

G=\dot{G}^{ij}A_{ij}.

Define the positive cone
\Gamma^{+}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
by

\Gamma^{+}:=\{\lambda=\left(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}\right):\lambda_{i}>0,\,1\leq i\leq n\}.

The following Newton-MacLaurin inequalities are well known, see e.g.
[
76
]
.

Lemma 3.2
.

For
1\leq k<l\leq n
and
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n})\in\Gamma^{+}
, the following inequalities hold:

(3.5)

\displaystyle p_{k}(\lambda)p_{l-1}(\lambda)\geq

\displaystyle p_{k-1}(\lambda)p_{l}(\lambda).

(3.6)

\displaystyle p_{k}(\lambda)\geq

\displaystyle p_{l}^{\frac{k}{l}}(\lambda).

Equality holds in the both (
3.5
) and (
3.6
) if and only if
\lambda_{i}=\lambda_{j}
for all
1\leq i,j\leq n
.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
0\leq k<l\leq n
,
G(A):=\left(\frac{p_{l}(\lambda(A))}{p_{k}(\lambda(A))}\right)^{\frac{1}{l-k}}
, and
\lambda(A)\in\Gamma^{+}
. Then

(3.7)

\dot{G}^{ij}\delta_{i}{}^{j}\geq 1,

with equality if and only if
\lambda_{1}(A)=\cdots=\lambda_{n}(A)>0
.

Proof.

By (
3.5
), we have
\frac{p_{k+1}}{p_{k}}\geq\cdots\geq\frac{p_{l}}{p_{l-1}}
. Then

\frac{p_{l}}{p_{k}}=\prod_{i=k}^{l-1}\frac{p_{i+1}}{p_{i}}\geq\left(\frac{p_{l}}{p_{l-1}}\right)^{l-k},

which is equivalent to

(3.8)

p_{k}p_{l-1}\geq p_{k}^{1+\frac{1}{l-k}}p_{l}^{1-\frac{1}{l-k}}.

Using (
3.2
), (
3.5
) and (
3.8
), we have

\displaystyle\dot{G}^{ij}\delta_{i}{}^{j}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{l-k}\left(\frac{p_{l}}{p_{k}}\right)^{\frac{1}{l-k}-1}\frac{p_{k}\dot{p}_{l}^{ij}\delta_{i}{}^{j}-p_{l}\dot{p}_{k}^{ij}\delta_{i}{}^{j}}{p_{k}^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{l-k}\left(\frac{p_{l}}{p_{k}}\right)^{\frac{1}{l-k}-1}\frac{lp_{k}p_{l-1}-kp_{l}p_{k-1}}{p_{k}^{2}}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{l-k}\left(\frac{p_{l}}{p_{k}}\right)^{\frac{1}{l-k}-1}\frac{\left(l-k\right)p_{k}p_{l-1}}{p_{k}^{2}}

(3.9)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\left(\frac{p_{l}}{p_{k}}\right)^{\frac{1}{l-k}-1}\frac{p_{k}^{1+\frac{1}{l-k}}p_{l}^{1-\frac{1}{l-k}}}{p_{k}^{2}}=1.

By Lemma
3.2
, equality holds in the first inequality of (
3.9
) if and only if
\lambda_{1}(A)=\cdots=\lambda_{n}(A)
. Conversely, equality holds in (
3.9
) when
\lambda_{1}(A)=\cdots=\lambda_{n}(A)
. This completes the proof of Lemma
3.3
.
∎

Let
s=s(\lambda)
be a symmetric function defined on the positive cone
\Gamma^{+}
. Then the dual function
s_{*}
of
s
is defined by
s_{*}(\lambda):=s^{-1}(\lambda_{1}^{-1},\ldots,\lambda_{n}^{-1})
. For example, if
s(\lambda)=\left(\frac{p_{l}(\lambda)}{p_{k}(\lambda)}\right)^{\frac{1}{l-k}}
for
0\leq k<l\leq n
and
\lambda\in\Gamma^{+}
, then

(3.10)

s_{*}(\lambda)=\left(\frac{p_{l}(\lambda_{1}^{-1},\ldots,\lambda_{n}^{-1})}{p_{k}(\lambda_{1}^{-1},\ldots,\lambda_{n}^{-1})}\right)^{-\frac{1}{l-k}}=\left(\frac{p_{n-l}(\lambda)}{p_{n}(\lambda)}\cdot\frac{p_{n}(\lambda)}{p_{n-k}(\lambda)}\right)^{-\frac{1}{l-k}}=\left(\frac{p_{n-k}(\lambda)}{p_{n-l}(\lambda)}\right)^{\frac{1}{l-k}}.

We call
s
inverse-concave if its dual function
s_{*}
is concave on
\Gamma^{+}
.

Andrews
[
8
]
proved the following lemma.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
0\leq k<l\leq n
,
s(\lambda)=\left(\frac{p_{l}(\lambda)}{p_{k}(\lambda)}\right)^{\frac{1}{l-k}}
and
\lambda\in\Gamma^{+}
. Then
s(\lambda)
is monotone increasing and concave on
\Gamma^{+}
. Let
G(A):=\left(\frac{p_{l}(\lambda(A))}{p_{k}(\lambda(A))}\right)^{\frac{1}{l-k}}
and
\lambda(A)\in\Gamma^{+}
. Then

(3.11)

\dot{G}^{ij}>0.

For any
B\in{\rm Sym}(n)
, there holds

(3.12)

\ddot{G}^{ij,kl}B_{ij}B_{kl}\leq 0.

Let
\left(M^{n},g\right)
be a Riemannian manifold. A
GL(n)
-invariant function
S
defined on
{\rm Sym}(n)
can induce a function
\widehat{S}
defined on symmetric
(0,2)
-tensors on
M
. Precisely, for a symmetric
(0,2)
-tensor
A
on
M
, we define

\widehat{S}\left(A,g\right):=S\left(\lambda\left(g^{-\frac{1}{2}}Ag^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right)\right)=S(\lambda(g^{-1}A)).

Due to the
GL(n)
-invariance of
S
, we have
\widehat{S}\left(A,g\right)=\widehat{S}\left(g^{-\frac{1}{2}}Ag^{-\frac{1}{2}},I\right)
. For
\widehat{S}=\widehat{S}(A,g)
, we define

\dot{\widehat{S}}^{pq}=\frac{\partial\widehat{S}}{\partial A_{ij}},\quad\ddot{\widehat{S}}^{pq,rs}=\frac{\partial^{2}\widehat{S}}{\partial A_{rs}\partial A_{pq}},

which are tensors on
(M,g)
. If
g=I
, then

\widehat{S}(A,I)=S(A),\quad\dot{\widehat{S}}^{pq}=\dot{S}^{pq},\quad\ddot{\widehat{S}}^{pq,rs}=\ddot{S}^{pq,rs}.

In the following text, we will write
S
for
\widehat{S}
if there is no confusion. For more detailed conventions and properties about curvature functions, we refer readers to
[
37
, Chapter 2]
and
[
5
, Section 2]
.

3.2.
Hypersurfaces in hyperbolic space

Let
M
be a smooth hypersurface in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
with second fundamental form
\left(h_{ij}\right)
and principal curvatures
\kappa=\left(\kappa_{1},\ldots,\kappa_{n}\right)
. We call
\tilde{h}_{ij}:=h_{ij}-g_{ij}
the
shifted second fundamental form
of
M\subset\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. The eigenvalues
\tilde{\kappa}=\left(\tilde{\kappa}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{\kappa}_{n}\right)
of
g^{-1}\tilde{h}
are called the
shifted principal curvatures
of
M\subset\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. It is easy to see that
\tilde{\kappa}_{i}=\kappa_{i}-1
,
i=1,\ldots,n
. For a
GL(n)
-invariant function
G
, we can view
G=G(\tilde{\kappa})=G(\tilde{h}_{ij},g_{ij})
. By the above argument, the following Lemma
3.5
follows directly from Lemma
3.1
and Lemma
3.3
.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
M
be a smooth hypersurface in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
and
\tilde{\kappa}_{i}=\kappa_{i}-1
be the shifted principal curvatures of
M
, and let
1\leq m\leq n
be an integer. Then

(3.13)

\displaystyle\dot{p}_{m}^{ij}\left(\tilde{\kappa}\right)g_{ij}=

\displaystyle mp_{m-1}(\tilde{\kappa}),

(3.14)

\displaystyle\dot{p}_{m}^{ij}\left(\tilde{\kappa}\right)\tilde{h}_{ij}=

\displaystyle mp_{m}\left(\tilde{\kappa}\right).

Let
0\leq k<l\leq n
and
F(\tilde{\kappa})=\left(\frac{p_{l}(\tilde{\kappa})}{p_{k}(\tilde{\kappa})}\right)^{\frac{1}{l-k}}
. Then

(3.15)

F=\dot{F}^{ij}\tilde{h}_{ij}.

In addition, if
\tilde{\kappa}\in\Gamma^{+}
, then

(3.16)

\dot{F}^{ij}g_{ij}\geq 1,

with equality if and only if
\tilde{\kappa}_{i}=\tilde{\kappa}_{j}
for all
1\leq i,j\leq n
.

Note that the shifted second fundamental form
\left(\tilde{h}_{ij}\right)
of a hypersurface
M
in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
is a Codazzi tensor, i.e.
\nabla_{k}\tilde{h}_{ij}=\nabla_{j}\tilde{h}_{ik}
. This implies that
\dot{p}_{m}^{ij}(\tilde{\kappa})
is divergence-free for
1\leq m\leq n
, i.e.
\nabla_{j}\dot{p}_{m}^{ij}(\tilde{\kappa})=0
on
M
, see
[
70
]
.

The following Lemmas
3.6
–
3.8
are well-known and can be found in
[
45
]
.

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
V=\sinh r\partial_{r}
be a conformal Killing vector field in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. Then

(3.17)

\displaystyle\langle\overline{\nabla}_{X}V,Y\rangle=

\displaystyle\cosh r\langle X,Y\rangle,

(3.18)

\displaystyle\mathrm{div}_{\mathbb{H}^{n+1}}V=

\displaystyle(n+1)\cosh r,

where
\mathrm{div}_{\mathbb{H}^{n+1}}V
denoted the divergence of vector field
V
in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
M
be a smooth closed hypersurface in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
and
\tilde{u}=\langle V,\nu\rangle
be the classical support function of
M
. Then

(3.19)

\int_{M}\cosh rp_{m}(\kappa)d\mu=\int_{M}\tilde{u}p_{m+1}(\kappa)d\mu,\quad m=0,1,\ldots,n-1.

The following Lemma
3.8
is a direct consequence of Lemma
3.7
, see
[
45
]
. However, we will give a direct proof of it.

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
M
be a smooth closed hypersurface in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
and
\tilde{\kappa}_{i}=\kappa_{i}-1
be the shifted principal curvatures. Then

(3.20)

\int_{M}(\cosh r-\tilde{u})p_{m}(\tilde{\kappa})d\mu=\int_{M}\tilde{u}p_{m+1}(\tilde{\kappa})d\mu,\quad m=0,1,\ldots,n-1.

Proof.

By
\nabla_{i}\cosh r=\langle V,\partial_{i}X\rangle
and (
3.17
), we have

\nabla_{j}\nabla_{i}\cosh r=\nabla_{j}\langle V,\partial_{i}X\rangle=\cosh rg_{ij}-\tilde{u}h_{ij}=\left(\cosh r-\tilde{u}\right)g_{ij}-\tilde{u}\tilde{h}_{ij}.

This together with (
3.13
) and (
3.14
) deduces

\displaystyle\dot{p}_{m+1}^{ij}(\tilde{\kappa})\nabla_{j}\nabla_{i}\cosh r=

\displaystyle(\cosh r-\tilde{u})\dot{p}_{m+1}^{ij}(\tilde{\kappa})g_{ij}-\tilde{u}\dot{p}_{m+1}^{ij}(\tilde{\kappa})\tilde{h}_{ij}

(3.21)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(m+1)\left((\cosh r-\tilde{u})p_{m}(\tilde{\kappa})-\tilde{u}p_{m+1}(\tilde{\kappa})\right).

Since
\dot{p}_{m+1}^{ij}(\tilde{\kappa})
is divergence-free, integration by parts and (
3.21
) imply

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{m+1}\int_{M}\nabla_{j}\dot{p}_{m+1}^{ij}(\tilde{\kappa})\nabla_{i}\cosh rd\mu

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m+1}\int_{M}\dot{p}_{m+1}^{ij}(\tilde{\kappa})\nabla_{j}\nabla_{i}\cosh rd\mu

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{M}(\cosh r-\tilde{u})p_{m}(\tilde{\kappa})d\mu-\int_{M}\tilde{u}p_{m+1}(\tilde{\kappa})d\mu.

This completes the proof of Lemma
3.8
.
∎

In the classical convex geometric theory, researchers would like to transform the geometric quantities of convex bodies to functions of the support function on
\mathbb{S}^{n}
by using the Gauss map. In the next part of Section
3
, we will do the corresponding work in the hyperbolic setting.

For the radial function
\widehat{r}
of a smooth convex hypersurface in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, one has “
\widehat{r}=\sqrt{\widehat{u}^{2}+|D\widehat{u}|^{2}}
” (see
[
73
, Section 1.7]
), here
\widehat{u}
denotes the Euclidean support function. Conversely,
\widehat{u}
can be viewed as a function defined on a smooth convex hypersurface in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
by “
\widehat{u}=\langle\widehat{X},\widehat{\nu}\rangle=\widehat{r}\langle\partial\widehat{r},\widehat{\nu}\rangle
”. Motivated by the above formulas, for the smooth uniformly h-convex hypersurfaces in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
, we have the following Lemma
3.9
.

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
M
be a smooth closed and uniformly h-convex hypersurface
M
in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
, let
r
denote the radial function of
M
, and let
\tilde{u}=\langle V,\nu\rangle
denote the classical support function of
M
. Then

(3.22)

\displaystyle\cosh r=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{|D\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}(z)+\frac{1}{2}\left(\varphi(z)+\frac{1}{\varphi(z)}\right),

(3.23)

\displaystyle\tilde{u}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{|D\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}(z)+\frac{1}{2}\left(\varphi(z)-\frac{1}{\varphi(z)}\right),

where
\cosh r
and
\tilde{u}
were evaluated at
X(z)\in M
, and
\varphi
was evaluated at
z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
.
Conversely, for the horospherical support function
u(z)
of
M
defined on
\mathbb{S}^{n}
, we have

(3.24)

e^{-u(z)}=\frac{1}{\varphi(z)}=\cosh r-\tilde{u}.

Proof.

For the position vector
X(z)=G^{-1}(z)\in M
, there exists
\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
such that

\displaystyle X(z)=

\displaystyle(\sinh r\theta,\cosh r),

\displaystyle X(z)=

\displaystyle\left(\left(\frac{1}{2}\frac{|D\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}-\frac{1}{2}\left(\varphi-\frac{1}{\varphi}\right)\right)z-D\varphi,\frac{1}{2}\frac{|D\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\varphi+\frac{1}{\varphi}\right)\right),

where the second formula is (
2.9
). Comparing these two formulas, we have

\displaystyle\cosh r=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{|D\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\varphi+\frac{1}{\varphi}\right),

(3.25)

\displaystyle\sinh r\langle\theta,z\rangle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{|D\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}-\frac{1}{2}\left(\varphi-\frac{1}{\varphi}\right).

Thus we obtain (
3.22
).

Since
\partial_{r}=\partial_{r}X=(\cosh r\theta,\sinh r)
, we know
\langle\partial_{r},X\rangle=0
. Then by (
2.8
), we have

\displaystyle\tilde{u}=

\displaystyle\langle V,\nu\rangle=\sinh r\langle\partial_{r},\nu\rangle=-\sinh r\langle\partial_{r},X-\nu\rangle

(3.26)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\sinh r}{\varphi}\langle\partial_{r},(z,1)\rangle=\frac{1}{\varphi}(\sinh^{2}r-\cosh r\sinh r\langle\theta,z\rangle).

Using (
3.26
), (
3.25
) and (
3.22
), we have

\tilde{u}=\frac{1}{\varphi}\left(\cosh^{2}r-1-\cosh r\left(\cosh r-\varphi\right)\right)=\cosh r-\frac{1}{\varphi}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{|D\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\varphi-\frac{1}{\varphi}\right).

Thus we obtain (
3.23
). Formula (
3.24
) follows directly from (
3.22
) and (
3.23
). Then we complete the proof of Lemma
3.9
.
∎
