Corollary 3.1
.

Let
M
be a smooth uniformly h-convex hypersurface in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
with horospherical support function
u(z)
. Then

(3.27)

\displaystyle\cosh r\nu-\widetilde{u}X=

\displaystyle-\left(\frac{D\varphi}{\varphi}+z,0\right),

(3.28)

\displaystyle\frac{\widetilde{u}X-\cosh r\nu}{\cosh r-\widetilde{u}}=

\displaystyle\left(D\varphi+\varphi z,0\right).

Proof.

Using (
3.24
) and (
2.8
), we have

(3.29)

\cosh r\nu-\widetilde{u}X=\cosh r\left(\nu-X\right)+\left(\cosh r-\widetilde{u}\right)X=\frac{X-\cosh r(z,1)}{\varphi}.

Formulas (
2.9
) and (
3.22
) yield that

\displaystyle X-\cosh r(z,1)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\varphi\left(-z,1\right)+\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{|D\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}+\frac{1}{\varphi}\right)\left(z,1\right)-\left(D\varphi,0\right)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{|D\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}+\varphi+\frac{1}{\varphi}\right)\left(z,1\right)

(3.30)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\left(D\varphi+\varphi z,0\right).

Then formula (
3.27
) follows by substituting (
3.30
) into (
3.29
), and thus formula (
3.28
) follows from (
3.27
) and (
3.24
). We complete the proof of Corollary
3.1
.
∎

We call a hypersurface
M\subset\mathbb{H}^{n+1}

star-shaped
if it can be represented as a graph of a positive radial function
r(\theta)
defined on
\mathbb{S}^{n}
. In this case, for any
\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
, there exist a unique point
X(\theta)
on
M
and a positive number
r
such that
X(\theta)=\left(\sinh r\theta,\cosh r\right)
. It is well-known that a closed hypersurface
M\subset\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
is star-shaped if and only if
\tilde{u}=\langle V,\nu\rangle
is positive on
M
.

Corollary 3.2
.

Let
M=\partial\Omega
be a smooth closed and uniformly h-convex hypersurface in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. Assume that
\Omega
contains the origin in its interior. Then
M
is star-shaped.

Proof.

Since
\Omega
contains the origin in its interior, we have
u(z)>0
for all
z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
, where
u(z)
is the horospherical support function of
\Omega
. Hence
\varphi(z)=e^{u(z)}>1
, and then (
3.23
) implies

\tilde{u}\left(X(z)\right)=\frac{1}{2}\frac{|D\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}(z)+\frac{1}{2}\left(\varphi(z)-\frac{1}{\varphi(z)}\right)>0

for all
z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
,
which is equivalent to the fact that
M
is star-shaped.
∎

There are formulas
\widehat{\nabla}_{i}\widehat{\nu}=\widehat{h}_{i}{}^{j}\partial_{j}\widehat{X}
and
\widehat{\nabla}_{j}\widehat{\nabla}_{i}\widehat{\nu}=\widehat{\nabla}^{l}\widehat{h}_{ij}\partial_{l}\widehat{X}-\widehat{h}_{i}{}^{l}\widehat{h}_{lj}\widehat{\nu}
on any smooth hypersurfaces
\widehat{M}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
(see e.g.,
[
19
, p. 9]
), where
\widehat{X}
,
\widehat{\nu}
,
\widehat{\nabla}
and
\widehat{h}_{ij}
denote the position vector, outward unit normal, connection and second fundamental form of
\widehat{M}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
respectively. In the following Lemma
3.10
, we derive the corresponding formulas in the hyperbolic setting.

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
M
be a smooth hypersurface in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. In this lemma, we use the connection of the pullback bundle induced by the inclusion map
M\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1,1}
, i.e.

\nabla_{i}U:=\widetilde{\nabla}_{i}U,\quad\nabla_{j}\nabla_{i}U:=\widetilde{\nabla}_{j}(\widetilde{\nabla}_{i}U)-\widetilde{\nabla}_{\nabla_{j}\partial_{i}}U,

where
\partial_{i}
,
\partial_{j}
are tangential vector fields on
M
, and
U
is a vector field in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1,1}
defined on
M
. Then we have

(3.31)

\displaystyle\nabla_{i}(X-\nu)=

\displaystyle-\tilde{h}_{i}{}^{j}\partial_{j}X,

(3.32)

\displaystyle{\nabla}_{i}(z,1)=

\displaystyle-\varphi\tilde{h}_{i}{}^{j}\partial_{j}X+\varphi^{2}\tilde{h}_{i}{}^{j}\langle V,\partial_{j}X\rangle(X-\nu),

\displaystyle{\nabla}_{j}{\nabla}_{i}(z,1)=

\displaystyle-\varphi\nabla^{k}\tilde{h}_{ij}\partial_{k}X+\varphi\tilde{h}_{i}{}^{k}\tilde{h}_{kj}\nu-\varphi^{2}\tilde{h}_{i}{}^{k}\tilde{h}_{j}{}^{l}\langle V,\partial_{l}X\rangle\partial_{k}X

(3.33)

\displaystyle-\varphi^{2}\tilde{h}_{i}{}^{k}\tilde{h}_{j}{}^{l}\langle V,\partial_{k}X\rangle\partial_{l}X+T_{ij}(X-\nu),

where we viewed
\varphi
as a function defined on
M
by (
3.24
) and set

T_{ij}=-\varphi\tilde{h}_{ij}+\varphi^{2}\nabla_{j}(\tilde{h}_{i}{}^{k}\langle V,\partial_{k}X\rangle)+2\varphi^{3}\tilde{h}_{i}{}^{k}\tilde{h}_{j}{}^{l}\langle V,\partial_{k}X\rangle\langle V,\partial_{l}X\rangle.

Proof.

In the proof of Lemma
3.10
, we will use (
3.24
) frequently without mention. Let us work in normal coordinates for
M
about
X\in M
. By a direct calculation, we have

\nabla_{i}(X-\nu)=\partial_{i}X-h_{i}{}^{j}\partial_{j}X=-\tilde{h}_{i}{}^{j}\partial_{j}X.

Thus we obtain (
3.31
).

By using (
3.17
), we have

(3.34)

\nabla_{i}(\cosh r-\tilde{u})=\langle V,\partial_{i}X\rangle-\nabla_{i}\langle V,\nu\rangle=\langle V,\partial_{i}X\rangle-h_{i}{}^{j}\langle V,\partial_{j}X\rangle=-\tilde{h}_{i}{}^{j}\langle V,\partial_{j}X\rangle.

By the definition of the horospherical Gauss map (
2.8
) and (
3.24
), we have

(z,1)=\frac{X-\nu}{\cosh r-\tilde{u}}.

This together with (
3.31
) and (
3.34
) yields

\displaystyle\nabla_{i}(z,1)=

\displaystyle\nabla_{i}\left(\frac{X-\nu}{\cosh r-\tilde{u}}\right)=\varphi\nabla_{i}(X-\nu)-\varphi^{2}\nabla_{i}(\cosh r-\tilde{u})(X-\nu)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\varphi\tilde{h}_{i}{}^{j}\partial_{j}X+\varphi^{2}\tilde{h}_{i}{}^{j}\langle V,\partial_{j}X\rangle(X-\nu),

thus we obtain (
3.32
).

Differentiating the above formula with respect to
\partial_{j}
gives

(3.35)

\nabla_{j}\nabla_{i}(z,1)=-\nabla_{j}\left(\frac{\tilde{h}_{i}{}^{k}\partial_{k}X}{\cosh r-\tilde{u}}\right)+\nabla_{j}\left(\frac{\tilde{h}_{i}{}^{k}\langle V,\partial_{k}X\rangle(X-\nu)}{(\cosh r-\tilde{u})^{2}}\right).

Since

\displaystyle\langle\widetilde{\nabla}_{j}\partial_{i}X,X\rangle=

\displaystyle-\langle\partial_{i}X,\partial_{j}X\rangle=-g_{ij},

\displaystyle\langle\widetilde{\nabla}_{j}\partial_{i}X,\nu\rangle=

\displaystyle-h_{j}{}^{l}\langle\partial_{i}X,\partial_{l}X\rangle=-h_{ij},

we have

(3.36)

\widetilde{\nabla}_{j}\partial_{i}X=-h_{ij}\nu+g_{ij}X=-\tilde{h}_{ij}\nu+g_{ij}(X-\nu).

For the first term in the right-hand side of (
3.35
), by using the Codazzi equation, (
3.34
) and (
3.36
), we have

\displaystyle-\nabla_{j}\left(\frac{\tilde{h}_{i}{}^{k}\partial_{k}X}{\cosh r-\tilde{u}}\right)=

\displaystyle-\frac{\nabla^{k}\tilde{h}_{ij}\partial_{k}X}{\cosh r-\tilde{u}}-\frac{\tilde{h}_{i}{}^{k}(-\tilde{h}_{kj}\nu+g_{kj}(X-\nu))}{\cosh r-\tilde{u}}+\frac{\tilde{h}_{i}{}^{k}\partial_{k}X(-\tilde{h}_{j}{}^{l}\langle V,\partial_{l}X\rangle)}{(\cosh r-\tilde{u})^{2}}

(3.37)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\varphi\nabla^{k}\tilde{h}_{ij}\partial_{k}X+\varphi\tilde{h}_{i}{}^{k}\tilde{h}_{kj}\nu-\varphi\tilde{h}_{ij}(X-\nu)-\varphi^{2}\tilde{h}_{i}{}^{k}\tilde{h}_{j}{}^{l}\langle V,\partial_{l}X\rangle\partial_{k}X.

For the second term in the right-hand side of (
3.35
), by using (
3.31
) and (
3.34
), we have

\displaystyle\nabla_{j}\left(\frac{\tilde{h}_{i}{}^{k}\langle V,\partial_{k}X\rangle(X-\nu)}{(\cosh r-\tilde{u})^{2}}\right)=

\displaystyle\varphi^{2}\nabla_{j}(\tilde{h}_{i}{}^{k}\langle V,\partial_{k}X\rangle)(X-\nu)+\varphi^{2}\tilde{h}_{i}{}^{k}\langle V,\partial_{k}X\rangle(-\tilde{h}_{j}{}^{l}\partial_{l}X)

\displaystyle+2\varphi^{3}\tilde{h}_{i}{}^{k}\langle V,\partial_{k}X\rangle\tilde{h}_{j}{}^{l}\langle V,\partial_{l}X\rangle(X-\nu)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\varphi^{2}\nabla_{j}(\tilde{h}_{i}{}^{k}\langle V,\partial_{k}X\rangle)+2\varphi^{3}\tilde{h}_{i}{}^{k}\tilde{h}_{j}{}^{l}\langle V,\partial_{k}X\rangle\langle V,\partial_{l}X\rangle\right)(X-\nu)

(3.38)

\displaystyle-\varphi^{2}\tilde{h}_{i}{}^{k}\tilde{h}_{j}{}^{l}\langle V,\partial_{k}X\rangle\partial_{l}X.

Therefore formula (
3.33
) follows by inserting (
3.37
) and (
3.38
) into (
3.35
).

Then we complete the proof of Lemma
3.10
.
∎

For a smooth hypersurface
\widehat{M}
in Euclidean space, one has (see e.g.
[
72
, Equation (3.7)]
)

\displaystyle\widehat{\nabla}_{i}\widehat{u}=

\displaystyle\widehat{h}_{i}{}^{j}\langle\widehat{X},\partial_{j}\widehat{X}\rangle,

\displaystyle\widehat{\nabla}_{j}\widehat{\nabla}_{i}\widehat{u}=

\displaystyle\widehat{\nabla}^{k}\widehat{h}_{ij}\langle\widehat{X},\partial_{k}\widehat{X}\rangle+\widehat{h}_{ij}-\widehat{h}_{i}{}^{k}\widehat{h}_{kj}\widehat{u},

where
\widehat{u}:=\langle\widehat{X},\widehat{\nu}\rangle
is the support function of
\widehat{M}
. The following Lemma
3.11
gives the corresponding formulas for smooth hypersurfaces in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
.

Lemma 3.11
.

Let
M
be a smooth hypersurface in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. Then

(3.39)

\displaystyle\nabla_{i}\cosh r=

\displaystyle\langle V,\partial_{i}X\rangle,

(3.40)

\displaystyle\nabla_{i}(\cosh r-\tilde{u})=

\displaystyle-\tilde{h}_{i}{}^{j}\langle V,\partial_{j}X\rangle,

(3.41)

\displaystyle\nabla_{j}\nabla_{i}(\cosh r-\tilde{u})=

\displaystyle-\nabla^{k}\tilde{h}_{ij}\langle V,\partial_{k}X\rangle-(\cosh r-\tilde{u})\tilde{h}_{ij}+\tilde{h}_{i}{}^{k}\tilde{h}_{kj}\tilde{u}.

Proof.

In the proof of Lemma
3.11
, we use local normal coordinates for
M
. Formula (
3.39
) follows from the definition of
V
, i.e.
V:=\sinh r\partial_{r}=\overline{\nabla}\cosh r
. Formula (
3.40
) was proved in (
3.34
). Using (
3.17
) and the Codazzi equation, we have

\displaystyle\nabla_{j}\nabla_{i}(\cosh r-\tilde{u})=

\displaystyle-\nabla^{k}\tilde{h}_{ij}\langle V,\partial_{k}X\rangle-\tilde{h}_{i}{}^{k}\nabla_{j}\langle V,\partial_{k}X\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\nabla^{k}\tilde{h}_{ij}\langle V,\partial_{k}X\rangle-\cosh r\tilde{h}_{ij}+\tilde{h}_{i}{}^{k}(\tilde{h}_{kj}+g_{kj})\tilde{u}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\nabla^{k}\tilde{h}_{ij}\langle V,\partial_{k}X\rangle-(\cosh r-\tilde{u})\tilde{h}_{ij}+\tilde{h}_{i}{}^{k}\tilde{h}_{kj}\tilde{u},

thus we obtain (
3.41
). This completes the proof of Lemma
3.11
.
∎

Lemma 3.12
.

By use of the horospherical Gauss map, we parameterize a smooth uniformly h-convex hypersurface
M
about
X(z)
by local coordinates for
\mathbb{S}^{n}
about
z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
. Let
\partial_{i}z=e_{i}
at
z
. Then

(3.42)

\displaystyle\tilde{h}_{i}{}^{j}\partial_{j}X=

\displaystyle\frac{\varphi_{i}}{\varphi^{2}}(z,1)-\frac{1}{\varphi}(e_{i},0),

(3.43)

\displaystyle\tilde{h}_{i}{}^{j}\langle V,\partial_{j}X\rangle=

\displaystyle\frac{\varphi_{i}}{\varphi^{2}}.

Proof.

Differentiating (
2.8
) with respect to
\partial_{i}
, we have

(3.44)

\nabla_{i}(X-\nu)=-\frac{\varphi_{i}}{\varphi^{2}}(z,1)+\frac{1}{\varphi}(e_{i},0).

This together with
\nabla_{i}(X-\nu)=-\tilde{h}_{i}{}^{j}\partial_{j}X
implies (
3.42
).

By (
2.8
) and (
3.32
), we know

\displaystyle(e_{i},0)=

\displaystyle\nabla_{i}(z,1)=-\varphi\tilde{h}_{i}{}^{j}\partial_{j}X+\varphi^{2}\tilde{h}_{i}{}^{j}\langle V,\partial_{j}X\rangle(X-\nu)

(3.45)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\varphi\tilde{h}_{i}{}^{j}\partial_{j}X+\varphi\tilde{h}_{i}{}^{j}\langle V,\partial_{j}X\rangle(z,1).

Then (
3.43
) follows by substituting (
3.45
) into the right-hand side of (
3.42
). We complete the proof of Lemma
3.12
.
∎

3.3.
C^{0}
estimates of h-convex hypersurfaces

Let
M=\partial\Omega
be a smooth uniformly h-convex hypersurface such that
\Omega
contains the origin in its interior. We have proved that
M
is star-shaped in Corollary
3.2
, i.e.

M=\{\left(\sinh r(\theta)\theta,\cosh r(\theta)\right)\in\mathbb{H}^{n+1}:\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{n}\}

for some positive function
r(\theta)
. Here
r(\theta)
is called the radial function of
M
. We define

\displaystyle r_{\max}:=

\displaystyle\max_{\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{n}}r(\theta),\quad r_{\min}:=

\displaystyle\min_{\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{n}}r(\theta),

\displaystyle u_{\max}:=

\displaystyle\max_{z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}}u(z),\quad u_{\min}:=

\displaystyle\min_{z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}}u(z),

where
r(\theta)
is the radial function, and
u(z)
is the horospherical support function of
M
. Thanks to Corollary
2.2
and Corollary
3.2
, we can regard
r
and
u
as functions defined on
M
.

When researchers study the
C^{0}
estimate of a star-shaped hypersurface
\widehat{M}
in Euclidean space, they often use the formulas (see e.g.
[
55
, Lemma 3.5]
)

\widehat{u}_{\max}=\widehat{r}_{\max},\quad\widehat{u}_{\min}=\widehat{r}_{\min}.

In the following Lemma
3.13
, we derive the corresponding formulas for smooth uniformly h-convex hypersurfaces in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
.

Lemma 3.13
.

Let
M=\partial\Omega
be a smooth uniformly h-convex hypersurface in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. If
\Omega
contains the origin in its interior, then

(3.46)

u_{\max}=r_{\max},\quad u_{\min}=r_{\min}.

Proof.

By (
3.22
), we have

\cosh r(X(z))=\frac{1}{2}\frac{|D\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}(z)+\frac{1}{2}\left(\varphi(z)+\frac{1}{\varphi(z)}\right)\geq\cosh u(z),

and hence
u(z)\leq r(X(z))
for all
z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
. Then we have
u\leq r
on
M
.

Assume that
u(z)
attains its minimum at
z_{0}\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
. By (
3.22
), we have
\cosh r(X(z_{0}))=\cosh u(z_{0})
, which means
u_{\min}=u=r
at
X(z_{0})
by
u(z_{0})>0
. Hence

u_{\min}=r_{\min}.

By (
3.24
), we have

e^{-u}=\cosh r-\tilde{u}=\cosh r-\sinh r\langle\partial_{r},\nu\rangle.

Choose
\theta_{0}\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
such that
r(\theta_{0})=r_{\max}
, and let
X_{0}=(\sin r(\theta_{0})\theta_{0},\cosh r(\theta_{0}))\in M
.
By the above formula, we have
e^{-u}=e^{-r}=e^{-r_{\max}}
at
X_{0}
. This together with the fact that
u\leq r
on
M
implies

u_{\max}=r_{\max}.

Then we complete the proof of Lemma
3.13
.
∎

In
[
10
, Theorem 1]
, Borisenko and Miquel estimated the maximal distance from the center of an inball to the points on the boundary for h-convex bounded domains, which implies the following lemma.

Lemma 3.14
.

Let
\Omega:=\overline{B}_{z}(u)\cap\overline{B}_{-z}(u)
be the intersection of two horo-balls. Then the radial function
r(\theta)
of
\partial\Omega
satisfies

(3.47)

\cosh r(\theta)\leq e^{u},\quad\forall\ \theta\in\mathbb{S}^{n},

with equality if and only if
\theta
satisfies
\langle\theta,z\rangle=0
.

Proof.

Here we give an elementary geometric proof of Lemma
3.14
by using the Euclidean norm of the Poincaré ball model
\mathbb{B}^{n+1}
, see Figure
3.1
.
Let
O
be the center of
\mathbb{B}^{n+1}
and
A
be a point on
H_{z}(u)\cap H_{-z}(u)
. Then the direction
\theta_{A}
of
A
satisfies
\langle\theta_{A},z\rangle=0
. For any point
B\in H_{z}(u)\cap\overline{B}_{-z}(u)
, we can extend the ray
\overrightarrow{BO}
such that it intersects with
H_{-z}(u)
at point
C
. It is easy to see
|OC|\geq|OB|
. Note that the points
A
,
B
,
C
,
O
lie on the boundary of a two-dimensional flat disc
{\rm span}\{OA,OB\}\cap\overline{B}_{-z}(u)
from the Euclidean point of view. By the Intersecting Chords Theorem, we have

(3.48)

|OA|^{2}=|OB|\cdot|OC|\geq|OB|^{2},

with equality if and only if
B\in H_{z}(u)\cap H_{-z}(u)
. Denote by
r_{A}
the hyperbolic norm of segment
OA
. Then we have
r_{A}=\max_{\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{n}}r(\theta)
.

In the remainder of this proof, we use the hyperboloid model of
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1,1}
. Let
X_{A}
denote the position vector of
A
. Since
X_{A}\in H_{z}(u)\cap H_{-z}(u)
, formula (
2.4
) implies

(3.49)

\langle-X_{A},(z,1)\rangle=e^{u},\quad\langle-X_{A},(-z,1)\rangle=e^{u}.

By using (
2.3
), the formulas in (
3.49
) imply
\cosh r_{A}=\langle-X_{A},(0,1)\rangle=e^{u}
. Then inequality (
3.48
) deduces that inequality
\cosh r(\theta)\leq e^{u}
holds for all
\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
, and equality holds if and only if
\langle\theta,z\rangle=0
. This completes the proof of Lemma
3.14
.
∎

B_{-z}(u)
B_{z}(u)
z
-z
\mathbb{B}^{n+1}
O
A
B
C

Figure 3.1.
Intersection of horo-balls

4.
Hyperbolic
p
-sum

4.1.
Hyperbolic
p
-sum for h-convex bounded domains

The main result in Subsection
4.1
is the following Theorem
4.1
, and the proof of it will be given in the last part of Subsection
4.1
.

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
p
,
a
,
b
,
K
and
L
satisfy the assumptions in Definition
1.1
. Then there exists a unique h-convex bounded domain with horospherical support function

u(z)=\frac{1}{p}\log\left(ae^{pu_{K}(z)}+be^{pu_{L}(z)}\right).

Consequently, Definition
1.1
is well-defined.

Let
\Omega=a\cdot K+_{p}b\cdot L
be the hyperbolic
p
-sum of
K
and
L
defined by Definition
1.1
. Then for any point
X\in\partial\Omega
, there exists
z_{X}\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
such that

X=X(z_{X})=\frac{1}{2}\varphi(-z_{X},1)+\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{|D\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}+\frac{1}{\varphi}\right)(z_{X},1)-(D\varphi,0),

where
\varphi(z)=\left(a\varphi_{K}^{p}(z)+b\varphi_{L}^{p}(z)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}
, and
X(z_{X})
was defined by expression (
2.9
). Moreover, except the following special cases in Definition
1.1
:

(1)

\frac{1}{2}\leq p\leq 2
,
a=1
,
b=0
and
K
is a point;

(2)

\frac{1}{2}\leq p\leq 2
,
a=0
,
b=1
and
L
is a point;

(3)

p\in\{\frac{1}{2},2\}
,
a>0
,
b>0
,
a+b=1
, and both of
K
and
L
are points,

we have that
\Omega
is a smooth, non-degenerate and uniformly h-convex bounded domain.

By Corollary
2.2
, in order to show that the smooth function
u(z)
in Theorem
4.1
determines a unique smooth uniformly h-convex bounded domain in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
, we only need to provide that
A_{ij}[e^{u(z)}]>0
for all
z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
p>0
,
a>0
and
b\geq 0
be real numbers, and let
K
and
L
be two different smooth uniformly h-convex bounded domains in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. Here
K
and
L
can degenerate to points. Define

u(z):=\frac{1}{p}\log\left(ae^{pu_{K}(z)}+be^{pu_{L}(z)}\right).

In addition, we require that
p
,
a
,
b
,
K
and
L
satisfy one of the following conditions,

(1)

p>0
,
a>1
, and
b=0
;

(2)

\frac{1}{2}\leq p\leq 2
,
a>0
,
b>0
, and
a+b>1
;

(3)

\frac{1}{2}\leq p\leq 2
,
a>0
,
b>0
,
a+b=1
, and either
K
or
L
is non-degenerate;

(4)

\frac{1}{2}<p<2
,
a>0
,
b>0
,
a+b=1
, and both
K
and
L
are degenerate.

Then
A[e^{u(z)}]>0
, and
u(z)
is the horospherical support function of a smooth, non-degenerate, uniformly h-convex bounded domain
\Omega
in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
.

Proof.

Let
f(z):=\varphi^{p}(z)=e^{pu(z)}
,
f_{K}(z):=\varphi_{K}^{p}(z)=e^{pu_{K}(z)}
and
f_{L}(z):=\varphi_{L}^{p}(z)=e^{pu_{L}(z)}
. Then

f(z)=af_{K}(z)+bf_{L}(z).

We use normal coordinates for
\mathbb{S}^{n}
around
z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
with
\sigma_{ij}=\delta_{ij}
at
z
. Then

\displaystyle A_{ij}[f^{\frac{1}{p}}]=

\displaystyle\left(f^{\frac{1}{p}}\right)_{ij}-\frac{1}{2}f^{-\frac{1}{p}}|Df^{\frac{1}{p}}|^{2}\delta_{ij}+\frac{1}{2}\left(f^{\frac{1}{p}}-f^{-\frac{1}{p}}\right)\delta_{ij}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p}f^{\frac{1}{p}-1}f_{ij}+\frac{1}{p}\left(\frac{1}{p}-1\right)f^{\frac{1}{p}-2}f_{i}f_{j}-\frac{1}{2p^{2}}f^{\frac{1}{p}-2}|Df|^{2}\delta_{ij}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\left(f^{\frac{1}{p}}-f^{-\frac{1}{p}}\right)\delta_{ij}.

Since we assumed
p>0
, the inequality
A_{ij}[f^{\frac{1}{p}}]\geq 0
is equivalent to

(4.1)

B_{ij}[f^{\frac{1}{p}}]:=f_{ij}+\frac{1-p}{p}\frac{f_{i}f_{j}}{f}-\frac{1}{2p}\frac{|Df|^{2}}{f}\delta_{ij}+\frac{p}{2}\left(f-f^{1-\frac{2}{p}}\right)\delta_{ij}\geq 0.

At this time, we have
B_{ij}[f_{K}^{\frac{1}{p}}]\geq 0
and
B_{ij}[f_{L}^{\frac{1}{p}}]\geq 0
by the uniform h-convexity of
K
and
L
, respectively. By Corollary
2.2
(see also
[
3
]
), it is enough to show

(4.2)

B_{ij}[f^{\frac{1}{p}}(z)]>0,\quad\forall z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}.

A direct calculation yields

(4.3)

B_{ij}[f^{\frac{1}{p}}]-aB_{ij}[f_{K}^{\frac{1}{p}}]-bB_{ij}[f_{L}^{\frac{1}{p}}]=(T_{1})_{ij}+(T_{2})_{ij}+(T_{3})_{ij},

where

\displaystyle(T_{1})_{ij}:=

\displaystyle\frac{p-1}{p}\left(\frac{a(f_{K})_{i}(f_{K})_{j}}{f_{K}}+\frac{b(f_{L})_{i}(f_{L})_{j}}{f_{L}}-\frac{(af_{K}+bf_{L})_{i}(af_{K}+bf_{L})_{j}}{af_{K}+bf_{L}}\right),

\displaystyle(T_{2})_{ij}:=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2p}\left(a\frac{|Df_{K}|^{2}}{f_{K}}+b\frac{|Df_{L}|^{2}}{f_{L}}-\frac{|D(af_{K}+bf_{L})|^{2}}{af_{K}+bf_{L}}\right)\delta_{ij},

\displaystyle(T_{3})_{ij}:=

\displaystyle\frac{p}{2}\left(af_{K}^{1-\frac{2}{p}}+bf_{L}^{1-\frac{2}{p}}-(af_{K}+bf_{L})^{1-\frac{2}{p}}\right)\delta_{ij}

are tensors on
\mathbb{S}^{n}
. Simplifying tensor
T_{1}
gives

\displaystyle\frac{p}{p-1}(T_{1})_{ij}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{f_{K}f_{L}(af_{K}+bf_{L})}\left(\left(af_{L}(f_{K})_{i}(f_{K})_{j}+bf_{K}(f_{L})_{i}(f_{L})_{j}\right)(af_{K}+bf_{L})\right.

\displaystyle\left.-f_{K}f_{L}\left(a^{2}(f_{K})_{i}(f_{K})_{j}+b^{2}(f_{L})_{i}(f_{L})_{j}+ab(f_{K})_{i}(f_{L})_{j}+ab(f_{K})_{j}(f_{L})_{i}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{f_{K}f_{L}(af_{K}+bf_{L})}\left(abf_{K}^{2}(f_{L})_{i}(f_{L})_{j}+abf_{L}^{2}(f_{K})_{i}(f_{K})_{j}-abf_{K}f_{L}(f_{K})_{i}(f_{L})_{j}\right.

\displaystyle\left.-abf_{K}f_{L}(f_{K})_{j}(f_{L})_{i}\right)

(4.4)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{ab}{f_{K}f_{L}(af_{K}+bf_{L})}\mathcal{V}_{i}\mathcal{V}_{j},

where
\mathcal{V}:=f_{L}Df_{K}-f_{K}Df_{L}
. Analogously,

(4.5)

(T_{2})_{ij}=\frac{1}{2p}\frac{ab}{f_{K}f_{L}(af_{K}+bf_{L})}|\mathcal{V}|^{2}\delta_{ij}.

The following argument is divided into four cases.

Case 1.

p>0
,
a>1
and
b=0
.
Using (
4.4
) and (
4.5
), we have
T_{1}=0
and
T_{2}=0
in this case. Assumptions
a>1
and
b=0
imply

(4.6)

(T_{3})_{ij}=\frac{p}{2}\left(af_{K}^{1-\frac{2}{p}}-a^{1-\frac{2}{p}}f_{K}^{1-\frac{2}{p}}\right)\delta_{ij}=\frac{p}{2}\left(1-a^{-\frac{2}{p}}\right)af_{K}^{1-\frac{2}{p}}\delta_{ij}>0.

Then we obtain the desired inequality (
4.2
) by use of (
4.3
) and (
4.6
).

For the follow-up three cases, let us begin to assume that

\frac{1}{2}\leq p\leq 2
,
a>0
,
b>0
and
a+b\geq 1
. The assumption
\frac{1}{2}\leq p\leq 2
provides that
\zeta(t)=t^{1-\frac{2}{p}}
is a convex function on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
. This together with the assumption
a+b\geq 1
gives

(4.7)

af_{K}^{1-\frac{2}{p}}+bf_{L}^{1-\frac{2}{p}}\geq(a+b)\left(\frac{af_{K}+bf_{L}}{a+b}\right)^{1-\frac{2}{p}}\geq(af_{K}+bf_{L})^{1-\frac{2}{p}},

which induces
T_{3}\geq 0
. On the other hand, the assumption
\frac{1}{2}\leq p\leq 2
also implies
\frac{1}{2p}+1-\frac{1}{p}\geq 0
, and hence

(4.8)

(T_{1})_{ij}+(T_{2})_{ij}=\frac{ab}{f_{K}f_{L}(af_{K}+bf_{L})}\left(\frac{1}{2p}|\mathcal{V}|^{2}\delta_{ij}+\left(1-\frac{1}{p}\right)\mathcal{V}_{i}\mathcal{V}_{j}\right)\geq 0.

Case 2.

\frac{1}{2}\leq p\leq 2
,
a>0
,
b>0
and
a+b>1
.
As
a+b>1
, the second inequality in (
4.7
) is strict, which induces
T_{3}>0
. The desired inequality (
4.2
) therefore follows from (
4.8
) and (
4.3
).

Case 3.

\frac{1}{2}\leq p\leq 2
,
a>0
,
b>0
,
a+b=1
, and either
K
or
L
is non-degenerate. Since either
K
or
L
is non-degenerate, we have
aB_{ij}[f_{K}^{\frac{1}{p}}]+bB_{ij}[f_{L}^{\frac{1}{p}}]>0
. Then (
4.7
), (
4.8
) and (
4.3
) imply the desired inequality (
4.2
).

Case 4.

\frac{1}{2}<p<2
,
a>0
,
b>0
,
a+b=1
, and
K,L
are both degenerate. Let
K=X=(x,x_{n+1})
and
L=Y=(y,y_{n+1})
be two different points in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. Using (
2.7
), we have

\displaystyle\varphi_{K}(z)=

\displaystyle\varphi_{X}(z)=\langle-X,(z,1)\rangle=x_{n+1}-\langle x,z\rangle,

\displaystyle\varphi_{L}(z)=

\displaystyle\varphi_{Y}(z)=\langle-Y,(z,1)\rangle=y_{n+1}-\langle y,z\rangle.

If the inequality in (
4.7
) is strict for all
z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
, then the desired inequality (
4.2
) follows from the argument in Case 2. Hence we can assume that equality holds in (
4.7
) for some
z_{0}\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
without loss of generality.

Since
p<2
, we have
f_{K}(z_{0})=f_{L}(z_{0})
in this case, which is equivalent to
\varphi_{X}(z_{0})=\varphi_{Y}(z_{0})
. Then we have
x_{n+1}-\langle x,z_{0}\rangle=y_{n+1}-\langle y,z_{0}\rangle
, which deduces
y_{n+1}-x_{n+1}=\langle y-x,z_{0}\rangle
. On the other hand, the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality yields

\displaystyle\left(y_{n+1}-x_{n+1}\right)^{2}=

\displaystyle\left(\sqrt{|y|^{2}+1}-\sqrt{|x|^{2}+1}\right)^{2}=|y|^{2}+|x|^{2}+2-2\sqrt{\left(|y|^{2}+1\right)\left(|x|^{2}+1\right)}

(4.9)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|y|^{2}+|x|^{2}+2-2\left(|y||x|+1\right)=\left(|y|-|x|\right)^{2}\leq|y-x|^{2},

with equality if and only if
x=y
. Then the assumption
x\neq y
implies that the vector
z_{0}
is not parallel to
y-x
. Besides, at
z_{0}
we have

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{V},e_{i}\rangle=

\displaystyle f_{L}D_{i}f_{K}-f_{K}D_{i}f_{L}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varphi_{Y}^{p}D_{i}\left(x_{n+1}-\langle x,z\rangle\right)^{p}-\varphi_{X}^{p}D_{i}\left(y_{n+1}-\langle y,z\rangle\right)^{p}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p\varphi_{Y}^{p}\varphi_{X}^{p}\left(\frac{-\langle x,e_{i}\rangle}{\varphi_{X}}-\frac{-\langle y,e_{i}\rangle}{\varphi_{Y}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p\varphi_{X}^{2p-1}(z_{0})\langle y-x,e_{i}\rangle.

Then
|\mathcal{V}|\neq 0
at
z_{0}
, and hence inequality (
4.8
) is strict at
z_{0}
as
p>\frac{1}{2}
. Therefore we have
T_{1}+T_{2}+T_{3}>0
for all
z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
, which implies the desired inequality (
4.2
).

We complete the proof of Proposition
4.1
.
∎

Remark 4.1
.

(1)

If
K=L
in Definition
1.1
, then we can view
a\cdot K+_{p}b\cdot L
as
(a+b)\cdot_{p}K
. Hence we assumed that
K\neq L
in Proposition
4.1
.

(2)

Since
a\cdot K+_{p}b\cdot L=b\cdot L+_{p}a\cdot K
and
ab\neq 0
in Definition
1.1
, we assumed that
a>0
and
b\geq 0
in Proposition
4.1
.

(3)

The case
p>0
,
a=1
, and
b=0
is trivial in Proposition
4.1
, since
\Omega=1\cdot K=K
. Hence we assumed that
a>1
in the first case of Proposition
4.1
.

The above Proposition
4.1
has already covered the generic cases in Definition
1.1
. However, the reason that we can not assume that either
p=\frac{1}{2}
or
p=2
in Case (4) of Proposition
4.1
will be given in the following Corollary
4.1
, where we use the same notations as in the proof of Proposition
4.1
.

Corollary 4.1
.

Let
X=(x,x_{n+1})
and
Y=(y,y_{n+1})
be two different points in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
.

•

If
p=\frac{1}{2}
,
a>0
,
b>0
and
a+b=1
, then
A_{ij}[\varphi(z)]\geq 0
for all
z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
. Furthermore,
A_{ij}[\varphi(z_{0})]
has a
1
-dimensional null space for some
z_{0}\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
if and only if
z_{0}
satisfies
y_{n+1}-x_{n+1}=\langle y-x,z_{0}\rangle
.

•

If
p=2
,
a>0
,
b>0
and
a+b=1
, then
A_{ij}[\varphi(z)]\geq 0
for all
z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
. Furthermore,
A_{ij}[\varphi(z_{0})]
has a null space for some
z_{0}\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
if and only if
z_{0}
is the critical point of the function
\frac{\langle-X,(z,1)\rangle}{\langle-Y,(z,1)\rangle}
defined on
\mathbb{S}^{n}
. Indeed,
A_{ij}[\varphi(z_{0})]=0
in this case, and the function
\frac{\langle-X,(z,1)\rangle}{\langle-Y,(z,1)\rangle}
only has finite critical points.

Proof.

Inserting (
4.7
), (
4.8
),
B_{ij}[\varphi_{X}(z)]=0
and
B_{ij}[\varphi_{Y}(z)]=0
into (
4.3
), we have
B_{ij}[\varphi(z)]\geq 0
, which is equivalent to
A_{ij}[\varphi(z)]\geq 0
. Furthermore,
A_{ii}[\varphi(z_{0})]=0
for some
z_{0}\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
and
e_{i}\in T_{z_{0}}\mathbb{S}^{n}
if and only if both equality holds in (
4.7
) at
z_{0}
, and equality holds in (
4.8
) along direction
e_{i}
at
z_{0}
. We divide the rest proof into two cases.

Case 1.

p=\frac{1}{2}
,
a>0
,
b>0
and
a+b=1
. Let
z_{0}\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
satisfy that
A_{ij}[\varphi(z_{0})]
has a null space. Since
\zeta(t)=t^{1-\frac{2}{p}}=t^{-3}
is strictly convex on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
, equality holds in (
4.7
) at
z_{0}
if and only if
f_{K}(z_{0})=f_{L}(z_{0})
, which means
\langle-X,(z_{0},1)\rangle^{p}=\langle-Y,(z_{0},1)\rangle^{p}
. That is equivalent to
y_{n+1}-x_{n+1}=\langle y-x,z_{0}\rangle
.

The assumption
X\neq Y
and inequality (
4.9
) show that
y-x
is not parallel to
z_{0}
. Nevertheless, as
p=\frac{1}{2}
, we have from (
4.8
) that

(T_{1})_{ii}+(T_{2})_{ii}=\frac{ab}{f_{K}f_{L}\left(af_{K}+bf_{L}\right)}\left(|\mathcal{V}|-(\mathcal{V}_{i})^{2}\right)\geq 0,

and equality holds if and only if

e_{i}:=\pm\frac{y-x-\langle y-x,z_{0}\rangle}{|{y-x-\langle y-x,z_{0}\rangle|}}.

Using these special
e_{i}
, at
z_{0}
we have
(T_{1}+T_{2}+T_{3})_{ii}=0
, and hence
A_{ii}[\varphi(z_{0})]=0
. This proves Corollary
4.1
in Case 1.

Case 2.

p=2
,
a>0
,
b>0
and
a+b=1
.
In this case, inequality (
4.7
) is trivial. Thus,
A_{ij}[\varphi(z)]
has a null space if and only if equality holds in (
4.8
) along some unit vector in
T_{z}\mathbb{S}^{n}
. Furthermore,
A_{ij}[\varphi(z)]=0
in this case. Since
\frac{1}{2p}+1-\frac{1}{p}=\frac{3}{4}>0
, we have that
A_{ij}[\varphi(z)]=0
is equivalent to
\mathcal{V}=0
at
z
. Since
\mathcal{V}:=f_{L}Df_{K}-f_{K}Df_{L}
, we have

\frac{\mathcal{V}}{f_{L}^{2}}=D\left(\frac{f_{K}}{f_{L}}\right)=D\left(\frac{\langle-X,(z,1)\rangle^{2}}{\langle-Y,(z,1)\rangle^{2}}\right).

Hence
A_{ij}[\varphi(z)]=0
if and only if
D\left(\frac{\langle-X,(z,1)\rangle}{\langle-Y,(z,1)\rangle}\right)=0
at
z
, equivalently,
z
is a critical point of the function

\chi(z):=\log\langle-X,(z,1)\rangle-\log\langle-Y,(z,1)\rangle=\log\varphi_{X}(z)-\log\varphi_{Y}(z).

Let
z_{0}
be a critical point of
\chi(z)
.
By using (
2.19
), we have

(4.10)

D_{i}\chi(z_{0})=\frac{D_{i}\varphi_{X}(z_{0})}{\varphi_{X}(z_{0})}-\frac{D_{i}\varphi_{X}(z_{0})}{\varphi_{X}(z_{0})}=\frac{-\langle x,e_{i}\rangle}{\varphi_{X}(z_{0})}-\frac{-\langle y,e_{i}\rangle}{\varphi_{Y}(z_{0})}=0,

and thus

\displaystyle D_{j}D_{i}\chi(z_{0})=

\displaystyle\frac{D_{j}D_{i}\varphi_{X}}{\varphi_{X}}-\frac{D_{j}\varphi_{X}D_{i}\varphi_{X}}{\varphi_{X}^{2}}-\frac{D_{j}D_{i}\varphi_{Y}}{\varphi_{Y}}+\frac{D_{j}\varphi_{Y}D_{i}\varphi_{Y}}{\varphi_{Y}^{2}}

(4.11)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{D_{j}D_{i}\varphi_{X}}{\varphi_{X}}-\frac{D_{j}D_{i}\varphi_{Y}}{\varphi_{Y}}=\left(\frac{\langle x,z_{0}\rangle}{\varphi_{X}(z_{0})}-\frac{\langle y,z_{0}\rangle}{\varphi_{Y}(z_{0})}\right)\delta_{ij}.

Suppose for a contradiction
D^{2}\chi(z_{0})
is neither positive definite nor negative definite. Then
equations (
4.10
) and (
4.11
) imply that

(4.12)

\varphi_{Y}(z_{0})x-\varphi_{X}(z_{0})y=0.

Hence

0=\langle\varphi_{Y}x-\varphi_{X}y,z_{0}\rangle=\varphi_{Y}(x_{n+1}-\varphi_{X})-\varphi_{X}(y_{n+1}-\varphi_{Y})=\varphi_{Y}(z_{0})x_{n+1}-\varphi_{X}(z_{0})y_{n+1}.

This together with (
4.12
) gives

0=\varphi_{Y}^{2}x_{n+1}^{2}-\varphi_{X}^{2}y_{n+1}^{2}=|\varphi_{Y}x|^{2}+\varphi_{Y}^{2}-|\varphi_{X}y|^{2}-\varphi_{X}^{2}=\varphi_{Y}^{2}(z_{0})-\varphi_{X}^{2}(z_{0}).

Then we get
\varphi_{Y}(z_{0})=\varphi_{X}(z_{0})
, and thus
x=y
. This contradicts the assumption
X\neq Y
. Therefore
D^{2}\chi(z_{0})
is either positive definite or negative definite, which implies that the critical points of
\chi(z)
are discrete and form a finite subset of
\mathbb{S}^{n}
. We complete the proof of Corollary
4.1
.
∎

Now, we proceed to prove Theorem
4.1
.

Proof of Theorem
4.1
.

Proposition
4.1
and Corollary
4.1
show that
A_{ij}[\varphi]\geq 0
for all cases in Definition
1.1
. Then Proposition
2.1
asserts that

u(z)
uniquely determines a h-convex bounded domain
\Omega
with horospherical support function
u(z)
and
\Omega=\cap_{z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}}\overline{B}_{z}\left(u(z)\right)
. Hence, Definition
1.1
is well-defined. Furthermore, the boundary of
\Omega
can be described by using expression (
2.9
). Except the three cases we mentioned in Theorem
4.1
, the regularity of
\Omega
follows from Proposition
4.1
. Then we complete the proof of Theorem
4.1
.
∎

4.2.
Hyperbolic
p
-sum for sets

In
[
57
]
, Lutwak et al. found a pointwise addition for nonconvex sets in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, which extended the Minkowski-Firey
L_{p}
addition from convex bodies containing the origin to arbitrary subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. For real
p\geq 1
,
a\geq 0
,
b\geq 0
, and
\widetilde{K},\widehat{L}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, they defined

a\cdot\widehat{K}+_{p}b\cdot\widehat{L}=\{a^{\frac{1}{p}}(1-t)^{\frac{1}{q}}x+b^{\frac{1}{p}}t^{\frac{1}{q}}y:x\in\widehat{K},\ y\in\widehat{L},\ {\rm and}\ t\in[0,1]\},

where
q
is the Hölder conjugate of
p
. If
p=1
, then
q=+\infty
, and hence the above definition degenerates to the pointwise Minkowski sum
a\widehat{K}+b\widehat{L}
.

This subsection aims to show that the pointwise addition for general sets in Definition
1.3
is compatible with Definition
1.1
for h-convex bounded domains.

The following Assumption
4.2
will be needed.

Assumption 4.2
.

Let
p>0
,
t\in[0,1]
,
a\geq 0
and
b\geq 0
be real numbers, and let
X=(x,x_{n+1})
,
Y=(y,y_{n+1})
and
W=(w,w_{n+1})
be points on
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1,1}
. We suppose that

\displaystyle(1-t)^{\frac{1}{q}}a^{\frac{1}{p}}x_{n+1}+t^{\frac{1}{q}}b^{\frac{1}{p}}y_{n+1}-w_{n+1}\geq

\displaystyle\left|(1-t)^{\frac{1}{q}}a^{\frac{1}{p}}x+t^{\frac{1}{q}}b^{\frac{1}{p}}y-w\right|,\quad

\displaystyle p\in(0,1)\cup(1,+\infty),

\displaystyle ax_{n+1}+by_{n+1}-w_{n+1}\geq

\displaystyle\left|ax+by-w\right|,\quad

\displaystyle p=1,

where
q
is the Hölder conjugate of
p
, i.e.
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1
. Equivalently, it means that the vector
(1-t)^{\frac{1}{q}}a^{\frac{1}{p}}X+t^{\frac{1}{q}}b^{\frac{1}{p}}Y-W
lies inside the future light cone of
(0,0)
when
p\in(0,1)\cup(1,+\infty)
, and the vector
X+Y-W
lies inside the future light cone of
(0,0)
when
p=1
.

Now we proceed to give a geometric explanation of Assumption
4.2
. The following Lemma
4.1
can be found in
[
63
, p. 49]
.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
X
,
Y
be two points on
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1,1}
and
d_{\mathbb{H}^{n+1}}(X,Y)
be the geodesic distance between
X
and
Y
on
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. Then

(4.13)

\cosh\left(d_{\mathbb{H}^{n+1}}(X,Y)\right)=-\langle X,Y\rangle.

Proof.

It is well-known that the geodesic segment from
X
to
Y
is given by (see e.g.,
[
63
, p. 48]
)

(4.14)

\cosh tX+\sinh tv,\quad t\in[0,d_{\mathbb{H}^{n+1}}(X,Y)],

where
v
is a unit vector in
T_{X}\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. Since
\langle X,v\rangle=0
, we have

\langle X,Y\rangle=\langle X,\cosh\left(d(X,Y)\right)X+\sinh\left(d_{\mathbb{H}^{n+1}}(X,Y)\right)v\rangle=-\cosh\left(d_{\mathbb{H}^{n+1}}(X,Y)\right).

We complete the proof of Lemma
4.1
.
∎

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
p>0
,
t\in[0,1]
,
a\geq 0
and
b\geq 0
be real numbers,
let
X=(x,x_{n+1})
and
Y=(y,y_{n+1})
be two points in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
, and let
T=(1-t)^{\frac{1}{q}}a^{\frac{1}{p}}X+t^{\frac{1}{q}}b^{\frac{1}{p}}Y
.

(1)

If
\langle T,T\rangle\leq-1
, then

(4.15)

\left\{W=(w,w_{n+1})\in\mathbb{H}^{n+1}:p,\,t,\,a,\,X,\,b,\,Y,\,{\rm and}\,W{\rm satisfy\ Assumption\ \ref{assump-p-sum}}\right\}.

is a geodesic ball of radius
\log\sqrt{-\langle T,T\rangle}
centered at
T/\sqrt{-\langle T,T\rangle}
. Moreover,
T/\sqrt{-\langle T,T\rangle}
lies on the geodesic segment connecting
X
and
Y
.

(2)

If
\langle T,T\rangle>-1
, then the set (
4.15
) is empty.

Proof.

By Assumption
4.2
and
\langle W,W\rangle=-1
, we have

(4.16)

0\geq\langle T-W,T-W\rangle=\langle T,T\rangle-1-2\langle T,W\rangle.

Using (
4.13
) and (
4.16
), we have

(4.17)

\cosh d_{\mathbb{H}^{n+1}}(T^{\prime},W)=-\langle T^{\prime},W\rangle\leq\frac{1-\langle T,T\rangle}{2\sqrt{-\langle T,T\rangle}},

where we set
T^{\prime}=T/\sqrt{-\langle T,T\rangle}\in\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. If
\langle T,T\rangle>-1
, then the right-hand side of inequality (
4.17
) is less than
1
, which implies that (
4.15
) is empty.

It is easy to see that

(4.18)

\cosh\left(\log\sqrt{-\langle T,T\rangle}\right)=\frac{1-\langle T,T\rangle}{2\sqrt{-\langle T,T\rangle}}.

If
\langle T,T\rangle\leq-1
, then combining (
4.17
), (
4.18
) with (
4.13
), we have that
W
lies in a geodesic ball of radius
\log\sqrt{-\langle T,T\rangle}
centered at
T^{\prime}
. Conversely, for any
W
in this ball, one can easily check that
W
satisfies Assumption
4.2
by the same argument as above.

From
(1-t)^{\frac{1}{q}}a^{\frac{1}{p}}\geq 0
and
t^{\frac{1}{q}}b^{\frac{1}{p}}\geq 0
, we have that
T
and
T^{\prime}
both locate in the cone generated by the linear combinations of the vectors
X
and
Y
with non-negative coefficients. Besides, it is well-known that the geodesic lines in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
are given by the intersection of
2
-dimensional vector subspaces of
\mathbb{R}^{n+1,1}
with the hyperboloid model of
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
, see e.g.
[
63
, Section 2.1]
. Consequently,
T^{\prime}
is on the geodesic segment connecting
X
and
Y
. We complete the proof of Proposition
4.2
.
∎

Here we provide Figure
4.1
(
p=2
case) and Figure
4.2
(
p=1
case) to show the geometric explanation of Assumption
4.2
, as we proved in Proposition
4.2
.

T
x=(x_{0},\ldots,x_{n})
x_{n+1}
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
X
Y
W
T^{\prime}

Figure 4.1.
In this figure, we let
p=2
(thus
q=2
),
t_{0}=\frac{16}{25}
,
a=1
,
b=1
,
X=(0,0,\ldots,0,1)
and
Y=(1,0,\ldots,0,\sqrt{2})
. The blue curve in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1,1}
represents
\{(1-t)^{\frac{1}{2}}X+t^{\frac{1}{2}}Y:t\in[0,1]\}
, and
T=(1-t_{0})^{\frac{1}{q}}X+t_{0}^{\frac{1}{q}}Y=(\frac{4}{5},0,\ldots,0,\frac{3+4\sqrt{2}}{5})
lies on the curve. The green shade represents the domain enclosed by the past light cone of
T
; the red domain represents the geodesic ball of radius
\log\sqrt{-\langle T,T\rangle}
centered at
T^{\prime}=T/\sqrt{-\langle T,T\rangle}
. Proposition
4.2
implies that,
W
together with the chosen
p
,
t_{0}
,
a
and
b
satisfies Assumption
4.2
if and only if
W
lies in the red domain.

T^{\prime}
T
x=(x_{0},\ldots,x_{n})
x_{n+1}
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
X
Y
W
T^{\prime}

Figure 4.2.
In this figure, we let
p=1
,
a=\frac{4}{5}
,
b=\frac{1}{5}
,
X=(-1,0,\ldots,0,\sqrt{2})
and
Y=(2,0,\ldots,0,\sqrt{5})
. Then
T=aX+bY=(-0.4,0,\ldots,0,\frac{4\sqrt{2}+\sqrt{5}}{5})
. The green shade represents the domain enclosed by the past light cone of
T
; the red domain represents the geodesic ball of radius
\log\sqrt{-\langle T,T\rangle}
centered at
T^{\prime}=T/\sqrt{-\langle T,T\rangle}
. Proposition
4.2
implies that,
W
together with the chosen
a
and
b
satisfies Assumption
4.2
if and only if
W
lies in the red domain.

Now we can define a pointwise
p
-sum for general sets in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
.

Definition 4.3
.

Let
p>0
,
a\geq 0
and
b\geq 0
be real numbers, and let
K
and
L
be sets in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. We define

a\cdot K\widetilde{+}_{p}b\cdot L=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\underset{X\in K,\ Y\in L}{\bigcup}\bigcup_{t\in[0,1]}\left\{W\in\mathbb{H}^{n+1}:p,\,t,\,a,\,X,\,b,\,Y\,{\rm and}\,W{\rm satisfy\ Assumption\ \ref{assump-p-sum}}\right\},\hskip 9.24994ptp\geq 1,\\
&\underset{X\in K,\ Y\in L}{\bigcup}\bigcap_{t\in[0,1]}\left\{W\in\mathbb{H}^{n+1}:p,\,t,\,a,\,X,\,b,\,Y\,{\rm and}\,W{\rm satisfy\ Assumption\ \ref{assump-p-sum}}\right\},\hskip 9.24994pt0<p<1.\end{aligned}\right.

Proposition 4.3
.

Let
p>0
,
a\geq 0
and
b\geq 0
be a real numbers, and let
K
and
L
be sets in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. Then

a\cdot K\widetilde{+_{p}}b\cdot L=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\bigcup_{X\in K,\,Y\in L}\bigcup_{t\in[0,1]}B(p,t;a,X,b,Y),\quad&p>1,\\
&\bigcup_{X\in K,\,Y\in L}B(a,X,b,Y),\quad&p=1,\\
&\bigcup_{X\in K,\,Y\in L}\bigcap_{t\in[0,1]}B(p,t;a,X,b,Y),\quad&0<p<1,\end{aligned}\right.

Consequently, Definition
4.3
is equivalent to Definition
1.3
.

Proof.

Let
t\in[0,1]
be a real number, and let
X\in K
and
Y\in L
be two points. For abbreviation, let

E=\left\{W\in\mathbb{H}^{n+1}:p,\,t,\,a,\,X,\,b,\,Y\,{\rm and}\,W{\rm satisfy\ Assumption\ \ref{assump-p-sum}}\right\}.

We first consider the case
p\in(0,1)\cup(1,+\infty)
.
Let
T:=(1-t)^{\frac{1}{q}}a^{\frac{1}{p}}X+t^{\frac{1}{q}}b^{\frac{1}{p}}Y
be defined as in Proposition
4.2
.
Then Proposition
4.2
asserts that
E
is a geodesic ball whose center
T^{\prime}
lies on the geodesic segment connecting
X
and
Y
, and the radius of
E
is given by

\displaystyle\log\sqrt{-\langle T,T\rangle}=

\displaystyle\log\left(-\langle(1-t)^{\frac{1}{q}}a^{\frac{1}{p}}X+t^{\frac{1}{q}}b^{\frac{1}{p}}Y,(1-t)^{\frac{1}{q}}a^{\frac{1}{p}}X+t^{\frac{1}{q}}b^{\frac{1}{p}}Y\rangle\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log\left(-(1-t)^{\frac{2}{q}}a^{\frac{2}{p}}\langle X,X\rangle-t^{\frac{2}{q}}b^{\frac{2}{p}}\langle Y,Y\rangle-2(1-t)^{\frac{1}{q}}t^{\frac{1}{q}}a^{\frac{1}{p}}b^{\frac{1}{p}}\langle X,Y\rangle\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log\left((1-t)^{\frac{2}{q}}a^{\frac{2}{p}}+t^{\frac{2}{q}}b^{\frac{2}{p}}+2(1-t)^{\frac{1}{q}}t^{\frac{1}{q}}a^{\frac{1}{p}}b^{\frac{1}{p}}\cosh d_{\mathbb{H}^{n+1}}(X,Y)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log R(p,t,a,X,b,Y),

where we used (
4.13
) and (
1.1
). If
\langle T,T\rangle>-1
, then Proposition
4.2
implies that
E
is an empty set. Hence we can assume
\langle T,T\rangle\leq-1
. Using (
4.13
), we have that the distance between
T^{\prime}
and
X
is given by

\displaystyle\cosh d_{\mathbb{H}^{n+1}}(T^{\prime},X)=

\displaystyle-\langle T^{\prime},X\rangle=\frac{-\langle T,X\rangle}{\sqrt{-\langle T,T\rangle}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{-(1-t)^{\frac{1}{q}}a^{\frac{1}{p}}\langle X,X\rangle-t^{\frac{1}{q}}b^{\frac{1}{p}}\langle X,Y\rangle}{R(p,t,a,X,b,Y)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(1-t)^{\frac{1}{q}}a^{\frac{1}{p}}+t^{\frac{1}{q}}b^{\frac{1}{p}}\cosh d_{\mathbb{H}^{n+1}}(X,Y)}{R(p,t,a,X,b,Y)}.

Hence
T^{\prime}=P(p,t,a,X,b,Y)
and
E=B(p,t,a,X,b,Y)
, where we used Definition
1.2
.

In the case
p=1
, we have
E=B(a,X,b,Y)
by viewing
(1-t)^{\frac{1}{q}}=t^{\frac{1}{q}}=1
in the above proof, where
B(a,X,b,Y)
was defined in Definition
1.2
. Then Proposition
4.3
follows from Definition
4.3
.
∎

4.3.
Compatibility of different definitions of hyperbolic
p
-sum

The following Theorem
4.4
shows the relationship between Definition
4.3
and Definition
1.1
.

Theorem 4.4
.

Let
p
,
a
, and
b
be real numbers such that
\frac{1}{2}\leq p\leq 2
,
a\geq 0
,
b\geq 0
and
a+b\geq 1
. Let
K
and
L
be two smooth uniformly h-convex bounded domains in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. Then Definition
4.3
is compatible with Definition
1.1
, i.e.

a\cdot K\widetilde{+}_{p}b\cdot L=a\cdot K+_{p}b\cdot L.

Consequently, Definition
1.3
is compatible with Definition
1.1
.

For the convenience of readers, we give an outline of the proof of Theorem
4.4
.

•

Step 1
. We will prove that
a\cdot K\widetilde{+}_{p}b\cdot L\subset a\cdot K+_{p}b\cdot L
in Proposition
4.4
. Then, it suffices to show the opposite direction.

•

Step 2
. Since any point
W
on
\partial(a\cdot K+_{p}b\cdot L)
can be given explicitly by expression (
2.9
), we intend to find suitable
X\in K
,
Y\in L
and
t\in[0,1]
that satisfies Assumption
4.2
. Then we will prove that
\partial(a\cdot K+_{p}b\cdot L)\subset a\cdot K\widetilde{+}_{p}b\cdot L
in Lemma
4.3
.

•

Step 3
. In Proposition
4.5
, we will prove that
a\cdot X\widetilde{+}_{p}b\cdot Y=a\cdot X+_{p}b\cdot Y
for any two points
X
,
Y
.

•

Step 4
. Let
X\in K
,
Y\in L
and
W\in a\cdot K+_{p}b\cdot L
be points. If
W\in a\cdot X+_{p}b\cdot Y
, then the result in Step 3 implies
W\in a\cdot X\widetilde{+}_{p}b\cdot Y
. If
W\in a\cdot K+_{p}b\cdot L\backslash\left(a\cdot X+_{p}b\cdot Y\right)
, then we will find two h-convex bounded domains
K_{0}\subset K
and
L_{0}\subset L
such that
W\in\partial(a\cdot K_{0}+_{p}b\cdot L_{0})
. Then the fact in Step 2 implies
W\in a\cdot K\widetilde{+}_{p}b\cdot L
. Hence we will get
a\cdot K+_{p}b\cdot L\subset a\cdot K\widetilde{+}_{p}b\cdot L
.

•

Theorem
4.4
will follow from the results in Step 1 and Step 4.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
p
,
A
,
B
be positive numbers and
t\in[0,1]
. Then

(4.19)

\displaystyle(A^{p}+B^{p})^{\frac{1}{p}}\geq

\displaystyle(1-t)^{\frac{1}{q}}A+t^{\frac{1}{q}}B,

\displaystyle\quad p>1,

(4.20)

\displaystyle(A^{p}+B^{p})^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq

\displaystyle(1-t)^{\frac{1}{q}}A+t^{\frac{1}{q}}B,

\displaystyle\quad 0<p<1,

where
q
satisfies
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1
. In (
4.19
) and (
4.20
), equality holds if and only if
t=\frac{B^{p}}{A^{p}+B^{p}}
.

Proof.

Let
m=\frac{B^{p}}{A^{p}+B^{p}}\in(0,1)
. Since
\zeta(s)=s^{\frac{1}{p}}
is strictly concave on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
for
p>1
, Jensen’s inequality implies

(1-t)\left(\frac{1-m}{1-t}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}+t\left(\frac{m}{t}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq 1,

with equality if and only if
t=m
. This fact shows the desired inequality (
4.19
). The proof of inequality (
4.20
) follows similarly. This completes the proof of Lemma
4.2
.
∎

Proposition 4.4
.

Let
p>0
be a real number, let
K
and
L
be sets in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
, and let
u(z)=\frac{1}{p}\log\left(a\varphi_{K}^{p}(z)+b\varphi_{L}^{p}(z)\right)
be a function on
\mathbb{S}^{n}
. Then

(4.21)

a\cdot K\widetilde{+}_{p}b\cdot L\subset\bigcap_{z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}}\overline{B}_{z}(u(z)).

Proof.

It is easy to see that the Assumption
4.2
is equivalent to

\displaystyle\langle-(1-t)^{\frac{1}{q}}X-t^{\frac{1}{q}}Y,(z,1)\rangle-\langle-W,(z,1)\rangle

(4.22)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1-t)^{\frac{1}{q}}x_{n+1}-t^{\frac{1}{q}}y_{n+1}-w_{n+1}-\langle(1-t)^{\frac{1}{q}}x-t^{\frac{1}{q}}y-w,z\rangle\geq 0,\quad\forall\ z\in\mathbb{S}^{n},

where
q
satisfies
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1
.

We consider the case
p>1
at first. Apparently,

\langle-X,z\rangle=x_{n+1}-\langle x,z\rangle\geq\left(|x|^{2}+1\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}-|x|>0.

Similarly,
\langle-Y,z\rangle>0
. Taking
A=a^{\frac{1}{p}}\langle-X,(z,1)\rangle
and
B=b^{\frac{1}{q}}\langle-Y,(z,1)\rangle
in Lemma
4.2
, we have

(4.23)

\left(a\langle-X,(z,1)\rangle^{p}+b\langle-Y,(z,1)\rangle^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}=\max_{t\in(0,1)}\langle-(1-t)^{\frac{1}{q}}a^{\frac{1}{p}}X-t^{\frac{1}{q}}b^{\frac{1}{p}}Y,(z,1)\rangle.

By Definition
4.3
, we have

\displaystyle a\cdot K\widetilde{+}_{p}b\cdot L=

\displaystyle\underset{X\in K,\ Y\in L}{\bigcup}\bigcup_{t\in[0,1]}\bigcap_{z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}}\left\{W\in\mathbb{H}^{n+1}:\langle-W,(z,1)\rangle\leq\langle-(1-t)^{\frac{1}{q}}a^{\frac{1}{p}}X-t^{\frac{1}{q}}b^{\frac{1}{p}}Y,(z,1)\rangle\right\}

\displaystyle\subset

\displaystyle\underset{X\in K,\ Y\in L}{\bigcup}\bigcap_{z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}}\bigcup_{t\in[0,1]}\left\{W\in\mathbb{H}^{n+1}:\langle-W,(z,1)\rangle\leq\langle-(1-t)^{\frac{1}{q}}a^{\frac{1}{p}}X-t^{\frac{1}{q}}b^{\frac{1}{p}}Y,(z,1)\rangle\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underset{X\in K,\ Y\in L}{\bigcup}\bigcap_{z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}}\left\{W\in\mathbb{H}^{n+1}:\langle-W,(z,1)\rangle\leq\max_{t\in[0,1]}\langle-(1-t)^{\frac{1}{q}}a^{\frac{1}{p}}X-t^{\frac{1}{q}}b^{\frac{1}{p}}Y,(z,1)\rangle\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underset{X\in K,\ Y\in L}{\bigcup}\bigcap_{z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}}\left\{W\in\mathbb{H}^{n+1}:\langle-W,(z,1)\rangle\leq\left(a\langle-X,(z,1)\rangle^{p}+b\langle-Y,(z,1)\rangle^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\right\}

\displaystyle\subset

\displaystyle\bigcap_{z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}}\underset{X\in K,\ Y\in L}{\bigcup}\left\{W\in\mathbb{H}^{n+1}:\langle-W,(z,1)\rangle\leq\left(a\langle-X,(z,1)\rangle^{p}+b\langle-Y,(z,1)\rangle^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigcap_{z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}}\left\{W\in\mathbb{H}^{n+1}:\langle-W,(z,1)\rangle\leq\left(\max_{X\in K}a\langle-X,(z,1)\rangle^{p}+\max_{Y\in L}b\langle-Y,(z,1)\rangle^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigcap_{z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}}\left\{W\in\mathbb{H}^{n+1}:\langle-W,(z,1)\rangle\leq\left(a\varphi_{K}^{p}(z)+b\varphi_{L}^{p}(z)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigcap_{z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}}\overline{B}_{z}\left(u(z)\right),

where we used (
4.22
) and (
4.23
) in the first and the third equality respectively, and the last two equalities came from (
2.6
) and the definition of
u(z)
. We note that the above argument is also valid for the case
p=1
by viewing
\left(1-t\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}=t^{\frac{1}{q}}=1
.

Since the proof of the case
0<p<1
of Proposition
4.4
follows similarly, we write it down briefly.

\displaystyle K\widetilde{+}_{p}L=

\displaystyle\underset{X\in K,\ Y\in L}{\bigcup}\bigcap_{t\in[0,1]}\bigcap_{z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}}\left\{W\in\mathbb{H}^{n+1}:\langle-W,(z,1)\rangle\leq\langle-(1-t)^{\frac{1}{q}}a^{\frac{1}{p}}X-t^{\frac{1}{q}}b^{\frac{1}{p}}Y,(z,1)\rangle\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underset{X\in K,\ Y\in L}{\bigcup}\bigcap_{z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}}\left\{W\in\mathbb{H}^{n+1}:\langle-W,(z,1)\rangle\leq\min_{t\in[0,1]}\langle-(1-t)^{\frac{1}{q}}a^{\frac{1}{p}}X-t^{\frac{1}{q}}b^{\frac{1}{p}}Y,(z,1)\rangle\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underset{X\in K,\ Y\in L}{\bigcup}\bigcap_{z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}}\left\{W\in\mathbb{H}^{n+1}:\langle-W,(z,1)\rangle\leq\left(a\langle-X,(z,1)\rangle^{p}+b\langle-Y,(z,1)\rangle^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\right\}

(4.24)

\displaystyle\subset

\displaystyle\bigcap_{z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}}\overline{B}_{z}\left(u(z)\right).

We complete the proof of Proposition
4.4
.
∎

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
\frac{1}{2}\leq p\leq 2
be a real number, and let
a\geq 0
and
b\geq 0
be two real numbers with
a+b\geq 1
. Assume that
K
and
L
are two smooth uniformly h-convex bounded domains in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
, here the both
K
and
L
can degenerate to a single point. Let
\Omega=a\cdot K+_{p}b\cdot L
. Then

(4.25)

\partial\Omega\subset a\cdot K\widetilde{+}_{p}b\cdot L.

Proof.

Let
u(z)=\frac{1}{p}\log\left(a\varphi_{K}^{p}(z)+b\varphi_{L}^{p}(z)\right)
be the horospherical support function of
\Omega
, and let
W
be any point on
\partial\Omega
. Then there exists
z_{0}\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
such that

-\langle W,(z_{0},1)\rangle=\varphi(z_{0}),

where
\varphi(z)=e^{u(z)}
, and we used (
2.7
). By Lemma
2.1
, there also exist unique
X\in\partial K
and
Y\in\partial L
such that

(4.26)

-\langle X,(z_{0},1)\rangle=\varphi_{K}(z_{0}),\quad-\langle Y,(z_{0},1)\rangle=\varphi_{L}(z_{0}).

Note that

\varphi^{p}(z_{0})=a\varphi_{K}^{p}(z_{0})+b\varphi_{L}^{p}(z_{0}).

Furthermore, Proposition
2.1
asserts that the points
X
,
Y
and
W
can be given by use of expression (
2.9
).

At first, we consider the case
1\leq p\leq 2
. Let

t=\frac{b\varphi_{L}^{p}(z_{0})}{a\varphi_{K}^{p}(z_{0})+b\varphi_{L}^{p}(z_{0})}=\frac{b\varphi_{L}^{p}(z_{0})}{\varphi^{p}(z_{0})}.

Then

\displaystyle(1-t)^{\frac{1}{q}}a^{\frac{1}{p}}\varphi_{K}(z_{0})+t^{\frac{1}{q}}b^{\frac{1}{p}}\varphi_{L}(z_{0})=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{a\varphi_{K}^{p}(z_{0})}{\varphi^{p}(z_{0})}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}a^{\frac{1}{p}}\varphi_{K}(z_{0})+\left(\frac{b\varphi_{L}^{p}(z_{0})}{\varphi^{p}(z_{0})}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}b^{\frac{1}{p}}\varphi_{L}(z_{0})

(4.27)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{a\varphi_{K}^{p}(z_{0})+b\varphi_{L}^{p}(z_{0})}{\varphi^{p-1}(z_{0})}=\varphi(z_{0}),

where
q
satisfies
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1
. If
p=1
, then we view
(1-t)^{\frac{1}{q}}=t^{\frac{1}{q}}=1
. Using (
4.26
) and (
4.27
), we obtain

\langle-W,(z_{0},1)\rangle=\left(1-t\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}a^{\frac{1}{p}}\langle-X,(z_{0},1)\rangle+t^{\frac{1}{q}}b^{\frac{1}{p}}\langle-Y,(z_{0},1)\rangle,

which is equivalent to

(4.28)

(1-t)^{\frac{1}{q}}a^{\frac{1}{p}}x_{n+1}+t^{\frac{1}{q}}b^{\frac{1}{p}}y_{n+1}-w_{n+1}=\langle(1-t)^{\frac{1}{q}}a^{\frac{1}{p}}x+t^{\frac{1}{q}}b^{\frac{1}{p}}y-w,z_{0}\rangle.

The key idea of the remaining proof is to show

(4.29)

\langle(1-t)^{\frac{1}{q}}a^{\frac{1}{p}}x+t^{\frac{1}{q}}b^{\frac{1}{p}}y-w,z_{0}\rangle=\left|(1-t)^{\frac{1}{q}}a^{\frac{1}{p}}x+t^{\frac{1}{q}}b^{\frac{1}{p}}y-w\right|.

If (
4.29
) is true, then (
4.28
) and Definition
4.3
imply that
W\in a\cdot K\widetilde{+}_{p}b\cdot L
.

From the definitions of
\varphi(z)
and
t
in this proof, we have

(4.30)

(1-t)^{\frac{1}{q}}a^{\frac{1}{p}}D\varphi_{K}(z_{0})+t^{\frac{1}{q}}b^{\frac{1}{p}}D\varphi_{L}(z_{0})=\frac{a\varphi_{K}^{p-1}D\varphi_{K}(z_{0})}{\varphi^{p-1}(z_{0})}+\frac{b\varphi_{L}^{p-1}D\varphi_{L}(z_{0})}{\varphi^{p-1}(z_{0})}=D\varphi(z_{0}).

Combining (
4.30
) with (
2.9
), we deduce that the vector
(1-t)^{\frac{1}{q}}a^{\frac{1}{p}}x+t^{\frac{1}{q}}b^{\frac{1}{p}}y-w
is parallel to
z_{0}
. From (
4.28
), it remains to show

(4.31)

(1-t)^{\frac{1}{q}}a^{\frac{1}{p}}x_{n+1}+t^{\frac{1}{q}}b^{\frac{1}{p}}y_{n+1}-w_{n+1}\geq 0.

Since
1\leq p\leq 2
, we know that
\zeta(s)=s^{\frac{p-2}{p}}
is a convex function on
(0,+\infty)
, then (see also (
4.7
))

a\varphi_{K}^{p-2}(z_{0})+b\varphi_{L}(z_{0})^{p-2}\geq(a+b)\left(\frac{a\varphi_{K}^{p}(z_{0})+b\varphi_{L}^{p}(z_{0})}{a+b}\right)^{\frac{p-2}{p}}\geq\varphi^{p-2}(z_{0}),

where we used the assumption
a+b\geq 1
in the second inequality.
Hence

(4.32)

(1-t)^{\frac{1}{q}}a^{\frac{1}{p}}\varphi^{-1}_{K}(z_{0})+t^{\frac{1}{q}}b^{\frac{1}{p}}\varphi^{-1}_{L}(z_{0})=\frac{a\varphi^{p-2}_{K}(z_{0})+b\varphi^{p-2}_{L}(z_{0})}{\varphi^{p-1}(z_{0})}\geq\varphi^{-1}(z_{0}).

Using (
4.27
), the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and (
4.30
), we get

\displaystyle\varphi(z_{0})\left((1-t)^{\frac{1}{q}}a^{\frac{1}{p}}\frac{|D\varphi_{K}(z_{0})|^{2}}{\varphi_{K}(z_{0})}+t^{\frac{1}{q}}b^{\frac{1}{p}}\frac{|D\varphi_{L}(z_{0})|^{2}}{\varphi_{L}(z_{0})}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left((1-t)^{\frac{1}{q}}a^{\frac{1}{p}}\varphi_{K}(z_{0})+t^{\frac{1}{q}}b^{\frac{1}{p}}\varphi_{L}(z_{0})\right)\left((1-t)^{\frac{1}{q}}a^{\frac{1}{p}}\frac{|D\varphi_{K}(z_{0})|^{2}}{\varphi_{K}(z_{0})}+t^{\frac{1}{q}}b^{\frac{1}{p}}\frac{|D\varphi_{L}(z_{0})|^{2}}{\varphi_{L}(z_{0})}\right)

(4.33)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\left|(1-t)^{\frac{1}{q}}a^{\frac{1}{p}}D\varphi_{K}(z_{0})+t^{\frac{1}{q}}b^{\frac{1}{p}}D\varphi_{L}(z_{0})\right|^{2}=|D\varphi(z_{0})|^{2}.

Using (
2.9
), (
4.30
), (
4.27
), (
4.32
) and (
4.3
), we have

\displaystyle(1-t)^{\frac{1}{q}}a^{\frac{1}{p}}x_{n+1}+t^{\frac{1}{q}}b^{\frac{1}{p}}y_{n+1}-w_{n+1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1-t)^{\frac{1}{q}}a^{\frac{1}{p}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\frac{|D\varphi_{K}(z_{0})|^{2}}{\varphi_{K}(z_{0})}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\varphi_{K}(z_{0})+\frac{1}{\varphi_{K}(z_{0})}\right)\right)

\displaystyle+t^{\frac{1}{q}}b^{\frac{1}{p}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\frac{|D\varphi_{L}(z_{0})|^{2}}{\varphi_{L}(z_{0})}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\varphi_{L}(z_{0})+\frac{1}{\varphi_{L}(z_{0})}\right)\right)

\displaystyle-\left(\frac{1}{2}\frac{|D\varphi(z_{0})|^{2}}{\varphi(z_{0})}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\varphi(z_{0})+\frac{1}{\varphi(z_{0})}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left((1-t)^{\frac{1}{q}}a^{\frac{1}{p}}\frac{|D\varphi_{K}(z_{0})|^{2}}{\varphi_{K}(z_{0})}+t^{\frac{1}{q}}b^{\frac{1}{p}}\frac{|D\varphi_{L}(z_{0})|^{2}}{\varphi_{L}(z_{0})}-\frac{|D\varphi(z_{0})|^{2}}{\varphi(z_{0})}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\left((1-t)^{\frac{1}{q}}a^{\frac{1}{p}}\varphi_{K}(z_{0})+t^{\frac{1}{q}}b^{\frac{1}{p}}\varphi_{L}(z_{0})-\varphi(z_{0})\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\left((1-t)^{\frac{1}{q}}a^{\frac{1}{p}}\varphi^{-1}_{K}(z_{0})+t^{\frac{1}{q}}b^{\frac{1}{p}}\varphi^{-1}_{L}(z_{0})-\varphi^{-1}(z_{0})\right)\geq 0.

Thus we obtain the desired inequality (
4.31
).
Therefore, in the case
1\leq p\leq 2
, we conclude that
W\in a\cdot K\widetilde{+}_{p}b\cdot L
for any
W\in\partial\Omega
, and hence
\partial\Omega\subset a\cdot K\widetilde{+}_{p}b\cdot L
.

Now we consider the case
\frac{1}{2}\leq p<1
. The third equality in (
4.24
) implies that

a\cdot X\widetilde{+}_{p}b\cdot Y=a\cdot X+_{p}b\cdot Y,

where
X
and
Y
are the same as those in (
4.26
). Hence we only need to prove
W\in a\cdot X+_{p}b\cdot Y
. Since
X\in\partial K
, we have that
\varphi_{K}(z)-\langle-X,(z,1)\rangle
is nonnegative for all
z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
and attains its minimum
0
at
z_{0}
. Then
\varphi_{K}(z_{0})=\varphi_{X}(z_{0})
and
D\varphi_{K}(z_{0})=D\varphi_{X}(z_{0})
. Similarly,
\varphi_{L}(z_{0})=\varphi_{Y}(z_{0})
and
D\varphi_{L}(z_{0})=D\varphi_{Y}(z_{0})
. Define

\tilde{\varphi}(z):=\left(a\varphi_{X}^{p}(z)+b\varphi_{Y}^{p}(z)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

It is easy to show that
\tilde{\varphi}(z_{0})=\varphi(z_{0})
and
D\tilde{\varphi}(z_{0})=D\varphi(z_{0})
. On the other hand, we have proved in Theorem
4.1
that
\partial\left(a\cdot X+_{p}b\cdot Y\right)
can be characterized by use of
\tilde{\varphi}(z)
and expression (
2.9
). Besides, as
W\in\partial\Omega
, we can write
W
explicitly by use of
\varphi(z_{0})
,
D\varphi(z_{0})
,
z_{0}
and expression (
2.9
). Putting the above facts together, we have
W\in a\cdot X+_{p}b\cdot Y
. This completes the proof of Lemma
4.3
.
∎

Proposition 4.5
.

Let
n\geq 1
,
\frac{1}{2}\leq p\leq 2
,
a\geq 0
,
b\geq 0
with
a+b\geq 1
. Let
X=(x,x_{n+1})
and
Y=(y,y_{n+1})
be two points in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. Then

a\cdot X\widetilde{+}_{p}b\cdot Y=a\cdot X+_{p}b\cdot Y.

Proof.

Since Proposition
4.5
follows from the third equality in (
4.24
) when
\frac{1}{2}\leq p<1
, we can assume
1\leq p\leq 2
in the remaining proof.

Let
u(z)=\frac{1}{p}\log\left(a\varphi_{X}^{p}(z)+b\varphi_{Y}^{p}(z)\right)
. Theorem
4.1
asserts that
a\cdot X+_{p}b\cdot Y
is a h-convex bounded domain with horospherical support function
u(z)
.
Then (
4.21
) implies that

a\cdot X\widetilde{+}_{p}b\cdot Y\subset\cap_{z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}}\overline{B}_{z}\left(u(z)\right)=a\cdot X+_{p}b\cdot Y.

However, we have from (
4.25
) that
\partial\left(a\cdot X+_{p}b\cdot Y\right)\subset a\cdot X\widetilde{+}_{p}b\cdot Y
. Therefore, it is enough to show that
a\cdot X\widetilde{+}_{p}b\cdot Y
is contractible.

Now we use the upper half-space model of
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
, i.e.

\mathbb{H}^{n+1}=\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{\xi_{n}>0}=\{\Xi=(\xi,\xi_{n})=(\xi_{0},\ldots,\xi_{n-1},\xi_{n}):\xi_{n}>0\}.

By Proposition
4.3
, we can assume that
X
and
Y
both locate on the geodesic line
\Gamma:=\{\Xi\in\mathbb{H}^{n+1}:\xi=0\}
without loss of generality. Denote by
\overline{XY}
the geodesic segment connecting
X
and
Y
. Proposition
4.3
also implies that
a\cdot X\widetilde{+}_{p}b\cdot Y
is the union of a continuous family of geodesic balls. Furthermore, each of the balls is symmetric with respect to
\overline{XY}
. Therefore, for any point
W\in a\cdot X\widetilde{+}_{p}b\cdot Y
, there exists a geodesic ball
\mathscr{B}\subset a\cdot X\widetilde{+}_{p}b\cdot Y
such that
W\in\mathscr{B}
, and the center of
\mathscr{B}
lies on
\overline{XY}
. Denote
W=(\xi^{W},\xi^{W}_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{\xi_{n}>0}
. Since
\mathscr{B}
is also a Euclidean ball in the upper half-space model, we know that the “segment” connecting
(0,\xi^{W}_{n})
and
W
lies in
\mathscr{B}
; hence it lies in
a\cdot X\widetilde{+}_{p}b\cdot Y
. This shows that
a\cdot X\widetilde{+}_{p}b\cdot Y
can shrink continuously to
\left(a\cdot X\widetilde{+}_{p}b\cdot Y\right)\cap\Gamma
.

We claim that
\left(a\cdot X\widetilde{+}_{p}b\cdot Y\right)\cap\Gamma
is connected for
1\leq p\leq 2
. If
p=1
, then the claim follows directly from Definition
1.3
. If
1<p\leq 2
, then Proposition
4.2
implies that
B(p,t;a,X,b,Y)
is non-empty if and only if

\displaystyle-1\geq

\displaystyle\langle(1-t)^{\frac{1}{q}}a^{\frac{1}{p}}X+t^{\frac{1}{q}}b^{\frac{1}{p}}Y,(1-t)^{\frac{1}{q}}a^{\frac{1}{p}}X+t^{\frac{1}{q}}b^{\frac{1}{p}}Y\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(1-t)^{\frac{2}{q}}a^{\frac{2}{p}}-t^{\frac{2}{q}}b^{\frac{2}{p}}-2\left(1-t\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}t^{\frac{1}{q}}a^{\frac{1}{p}}b^{\frac{1}{p}}\cosh d_{\mathbb{H}^{n+1}}(X,Y):=\chi(t),

where
q
satisfies
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1
, and we used (
4.13
). Since
1<p\leq 2
, we have
q\geq 2
. Then the functions
-(1-t)^{\frac{2}{q}}
,
-t^{\frac{2}{q}}
and
-2(1-t)^{\frac{1}{q}}t^{\frac{1}{q}}
are convex on
[0,1]
. Hence
\chi(t)
is convex on
[0,1]
, and thus
I:=\{t\in[0,1]:\chi(t)\leq-1\}
is a connected interval. Then by (
1.2
), Definition
1.2
and Definition
1.3
, we have that
\left(a\cdot X\widetilde{+}_{p}b\cdot Y\right)\cap\Gamma
is a segment. Therefore
a\cdot X\widetilde{+}_{p}b\cdot Y
is contractible.

We complete the proof of Proposition
4.5
.
∎

We now turn to prove Theorem
4.4
.

Proof of Theorem
4.4
.

Let
X\in K
and
Y\in L
be two points. Let
K_{\alpha}=\alpha\cdot K+_{p}(1-\alpha)\cdot X
and
L_{\alpha}=\alpha\cdot L+_{p}(1-\alpha)\cdot Y
be h-convex bounded domains, where
0\leq\alpha\leq 1
. Since
\varphi_{K}(z)\geq\varphi_{X}(z)
and
\varphi_{L}(z)\geq\varphi_{Y}(z)
, we know
K_{\alpha}\subset K
and
L_{\alpha}\subset L
.
According to Theorem
4.1
, we have the following inclusions of h-convex bounded domains, i.e.

(4.34)

a\cdot X+_{p}b\cdot Y\subset a\cdot K_{\alpha_{1}}+_{p}b\cdot L_{\alpha_{1}}\subset a\cdot K_{\alpha_{2}}+_{p}b\cdot L_{\alpha_{2}}\subset a\cdot K+_{p}b\cdot L

for all
0\leq\alpha_{1}\leq\alpha_{2}\leq 1
. Note that the horospherical support functions of the above h-convex domains are smooth. Let
W
be an arbitrary point in
a\cdot K+_{p}b\cdot L
.
By Proposition
4.4
and Proposition
4.5
, we only need to show that if
W\in a\cdot K+_{p}b\cdot L\backslash\left(a\cdot X+_{p}b\cdot Y\right)
, then
W\in a\cdot K\widetilde{+}_{p}b\cdot L
.

The above inclusions in (
4.34
) imply that
W\in\partial\left(a\cdot K_{\alpha_{0}}+_{p}b\cdot L_{\alpha_{0}}\right)
for some
0<\alpha_{0}\leq 1
. Then by Lemma
4.3
, we have
W\in a\cdot K_{\alpha_{0}}\widetilde{+}_{p}b\cdot L_{\alpha_{0}}\subset a\cdot K\widetilde{+}_{p}b\cdot L
. This completes the proof of Theorem
4.4
.
∎

Proposition 4.6
.

Fix real numbers
p
,
a
,
b
that satisfy the assumptions in Definition
1.1
(Definition
4.3
resp.). Then the hyperbolic
p
-sum in Definition
1.1
(Definition
4.3
resp.) only depends on the relative position of the smooth uniformly h-convex bounded domains
K
and
L
.

Proof.

From Definition
1.2
, we have that the geodesic balls
B(p,t;a,X,b,Y)
and
B(a,X,b,Y)
only depend on the relative position of
X
and
Y
for fixed
p
,
t
,
a
and
b
. Then Proposition
4.6
follows from Proposition
4.3
and Theorem
4.4

∎

4.4.
Hyperbolic
p
-sum of geodesic balls

This subsection studies the geometric properties of the hyperbolic
p
-sum of geodesic balls.

Let
X
be a point in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. Denote by
B(X,r)
the geodesic ball of radius
r
centered at
X
in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. If
X
is treated as the origin of
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
, e.g.,
X=(0,1)
in the hyperboloid model, then we will write
B(r)
for
B(X,r)
.

We first derive the horospherical support functions of geodesic balls in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
X=(x,x_{n+1})
and
\Omega=B(X,r)\subset\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. Then the horospherical support function
u_{\Omega}(z)
of
\Omega
is given by

(4.35)

\varphi_{\Omega}(z)=e^{u_{\Omega}(z)}=e^{r}\left(x_{n+1}-\langle x,z\rangle\right).

If we set
T=e^{r}X
, then
\varphi_{\Omega}(z)=-\langle T,(z,1)\rangle
.

Proof.

By (
4.14
), we know that the geodesic ball
\Omega\subset\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
is given by

\Omega=\left\{Y=\cosh tX+\sinh tv:t\in[0,r],\,v\in T_{X}\mathbb{H}^{n+1},\,\langle v,v\rangle=1\right\}.

For any light-like vector
(z_{0},1)
, there exists a unit space-like vector
v_{0}\in T_{X}\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
such that

\left(z_{0},1\right)=-\langle X,\left(z_{0},1\right)\rangle X+\langle X,\left(z_{0},1\right)\rangle v_{0}.

Note that
-\langle X,(z_{0},1)\rangle=x_{n+1}-\langle x,z_{0}\rangle\geq(|x|^{2}+1)^{\frac{1}{2}}-|x|>0
. Using (
2.6
), we have

\displaystyle\varphi_{\Omega}(z_{0})=

\displaystyle\max_{Y\in\Omega}-\langle Y,(z_{0},1)\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\max_{t\in[0,r]}\,\max_{v\in T_{X}\mathbb{H}^{n+1},\,|v|=1}\left(-\cosh t\langle X,(z_{0},1)\rangle-\sinh t\langle v,(z_{0},1)\rangle\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\max_{t\in[0,r]}\left(-\cosh t\langle X,(z_{0},1)\rangle-\sinh t\min_{v\in T_{X}\mathbb{H}^{n+1},\,|v|=1}\left(\langle X,\left(z_{0},1\right)\rangle\langle v,v_{0}\rangle\right)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\max_{t\in[0,r]}\left(-e^{t}\langle X,(z_{0},1)\rangle\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{r}\left(x_{n+1}-\langle x,z_{0}\rangle\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\langle T,(z_{0},1)\rangle.

Thus we obtain the desired formula (
4.35
). This completes the proof of Lemma
4.4
.
∎

Remark 4.2
.

(1)

Let
T=e^{r}X
,
r\geq 0
and
X\in\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
.
Geometrically, the domain enclosed by the intersection of
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
and the past light cone of
T
is exactly
B(X,r)
. This fact was proved in Proposition
4.2
.

(2)

By treating the geodesic ball
\Omega=B(X,r)
as the outer parallel set of the point
X\in\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
with distance
r
, a more general form of Lemma
4.4
will be proved in Proposition
6.2
below.

Recall that the
Minkowski norm
of a future time-like vector
X\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1,1}
is defined by

N(X)=\sqrt{-\langle X,X\rangle}.

Combining formula (
4.35
) with Definition
1.1
, it is straightforward to see that the hyperbolic
1
-sum of geodesic balls can be identified with the sum of future time-like vectors lying above the hyperboloid model of
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1,1}
, and thus we obtain the following Corollary
4.2
. We note that the case
a=b=1
in Corollary
4.2
was also studied in
[
31
, Proposition 5]
.

Corollary 4.2
.

Let
K=B(X,r_{1})
and
L=B(Y,r_{2})
be geodesic balls in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
, let
T_{K}=e^{r_{1}}X
and
T_{L}=e^{r_{2}}Y
be vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1,1}
, and let
a\geq 0
and
b\geq 0
be real numbers with
a+b\geq 1
. Define
T=aT_{K}+bT_{L}
and
\Omega=a\cdot K+_{1}b\cdot L
. Then

\Omega=B\left(\frac{T}{N(T)},\,\log N(T)\right).

Consequently, the hyperbolic
1
-sum of geodesic balls are geodesic balls.

We next estimate the hyperbolic
p
-sum of geodesic balls for
\frac{1}{2}\leq p\leq 2
. Let
K
and
L
be two geodesic balls centered at the same point in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. We will show that the hyperbolic
p
-sum of
K
and
L
will become larger if we separate their centers.

Theorem 4.5
.

Let
\frac{1}{2}\leq p\leq 2
, let
a>0
and
b>0
such that
a+b\geq 1
, and let
K=B(X,r_{1})
and
L=B(Y,r_{2})
be two geodesic balls in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. Let
\Omega=a\cdot K+_{p}b\cdot L
. Then there is a geodesic ball of radius
\frac{1}{p}\log\left(ae^{pr_{1}+be^{pr_{2}}}\right)
lying inside
\Omega
. Moreover, the above inclusion is proper if and only if
X\neq Y
.

Proof.

Define
t\in(0,1)
by

(4.36)

t=\frac{be^{pr_{2}}}{ae^{pr_{1}}+be^{pr_{2}}}.

Theorem
4.1
asserts that

\widetilde{\Omega}:=(1-t)\cdot X+_{p}t\cdot Y

is well-defined and non-empty. Then we can choose a point
T\in\widetilde{\Omega}
, and hence

\varphi_{T}(z)\leq\varphi_{\widetilde{\Omega}}(z)=\left((1-t)\varphi_{X}(z)^{p}+t\varphi_{Y}(z)^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}},\quad\forall\ z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}.

Substituting (
4.36
) into the above inequality, we arrive at

(4.37)

\left(ae^{pr_{1}}+be^{pr_{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\varphi_{T}\leq\left(a\left(e^{r_{1}}\varphi_{X}\right)^{p}+b\left(e^{r_{2}}\varphi_{Y}\right)^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

Let
\widetilde{B}=B\left(T,\frac{1}{p}\log\left(ae^{pr_{1}}+be^{pr_{2}}\right)\right)
. Then Lemma
4.4
implies

\varphi_{K}=e^{r_{1}}\varphi_{X},\quad\varphi_{L}=e^{r_{2}}\varphi_{Y},\quad\varphi_{\widetilde{B}}=\left(ae^{pr_{1}}+be^{pr_{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\varphi_{T}.

Putting these formulas into (
4.37
), we have

\varphi_{\widetilde{B}}\leq\left(a\varphi_{K}^{p}+b\varphi_{L}^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}=\varphi_{\Omega},

which means that the geodesic ball
\widetilde{B}
lies inside
\Omega
.

Now we explore the case
\widetilde{B}=\Omega
. We first assume that
X\neq Y
, i.e.
K
and
L
are not centered at the same point. If
\widetilde{\Omega}
is not a single point, then the choice of
T
is not unique, and hence
\widetilde{B}
is a proper subset of
\Omega
. Otherwise, if
\widetilde{\Omega}
is a single point, then we infer from Lemma
2.3
that
A[\varphi_{\widetilde{\Omega}}(z)]=0
for all
z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
. However, by the fourth case of Proposition
4.1
and Corollary
4.1
, this can not happen whenever
\frac{1}{2}\leq p\leq 2
and
X\neq Y
. Therefore we conclude that
\widetilde{B}\subset\neq\Omega
when
X\neq Y
.

If
X=Y
, then we will prove that
\Omega
is a geodesic ball of radius
\frac{1}{p}\log\left(ae^{pr_{1}}+be^{pr_{2}}\right)
. Proposition
4.6
implies that we can assume
X=Y=(0,1)
without loss of generality. Consequently,
\varphi_{K}(z)=e^{r_{1}}
,
\varphi_{L}(z)=e^{r_{2}}
, and hence
\varphi_{\Omega}=\left(ae^{pr_{1}}+be^{pr_{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}
. Then Lemma
4.4
implies that
\Omega
is a geodesic ball of radius
\frac{1}{p}\log\left(ae^{pr_{1}}+be^{pr_{2}}\right)
centered at the origin
(0,1)
. This completes the proof of Theorem
4.5
.
∎

5.
Horospherical
p
-surface area measures, Horospherical
p
-Minkowski problem and Horospherical
p
-Christoffel-Minkowski problem

The
quermassintegral

W_{k}(\Omega)
for a (geodesically) convex domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
is defined by (see e.g.,
[
71
]
)

W_{k}(\Omega)=\frac{\omega_{k-1}\cdots\omega_{0}}{\omega_{n-1}\cdots\omega_{n-k}}\int_{\mathcal{L}_{k}}\chi(L_{k}\cap\Omega)dL_{k},\quad k=1,\ldots,n,

where
\mathcal{L}_{k}
is the space of
k
-dimensional totally geodesic subspaces
L_{k}
in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
, and
\omega_{i}
denotes the area of the unit sphere
\mathbb{S}^{i}
in Euclidean space. If
L_{k}\cap\Omega\neq\emptyset
, then
\chi(L_{k})=1
, otherwise
\chi(L_{k})=0
.

This definition of
W_{k}(\Omega)
seems difficult to be used in the calculations, hence we will give an equivalent definition of
W_{k}(\Omega)
for smooth convex
\Omega
by induction. We set

W_{0}(\Omega)=\mathrm{Vol}(\Omega),\quad W_{n+1}(\Omega)=\frac{\omega_{n}}{n+1}.

Furthermore, we let the curvature integrals be defined by

V_{n-k}=\int_{\partial\Omega}p_{k}(\kappa)d\mu,\quad k=0,1,\ldots,n,

where
\kappa=(\kappa_{1},\ldots,\kappa_{n})
are the principal curvatures of
\partial\Omega
. Then we can define the quermassintegral
W_{k}(\Omega)
by the following relations (see
[
47
,
45
]
)

V_{n-k}(\Omega)=(n-k)W_{k+1}(\Omega)+kW_{k-1}(\Omega),\quad k=1,2,\ldots,n.

Since we focus on the geometry of h-convex domains, we would like to consider the following
modified quermassintegrals
of a h-convex domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
, which were introduced by Andrews et al.
[
3
]
,

(5.1)

\widetilde{W}_{k}(\Omega):=\sum_{i=0}^{k}(-1)^{k-i}C_{k}^{i}W_{i}(\Omega),\quad k=0,1,\ldots,n.

Equivalently, that means

{W}_{k}(\Omega)=\sum_{i=0}^{k}C_{k}^{i}\widetilde{W}_{i}(\Omega).

If the domain
\Omega
is smooth, then the modified quermassintegrals can also be defined by induction, i.e.

(5.2)

\displaystyle\widetilde{W}_{0}(\Omega)=

\displaystyle\mathrm{Vol}(\Omega),\quad\widetilde{W}_{1}(\Omega)=\frac{1}{n}|\partial\Omega|-\mathrm{Vol}(\Omega),

\displaystyle\widetilde{W}_{k+1}(\Omega)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n-k}\int_{\partial\Omega}p_{k}(\tilde{\kappa})d\mu-\frac{n-2k}{n-k}\widetilde{W}_{k}(\Omega),\quad k=1,\ldots,n-1,

where
\tilde{\kappa}=\kappa-1=(\kappa_{1}-1,\ldots,\kappa_{n}-1)
are the shifted principal curvatures of
\partial\Omega
.

Andrews et al.
[
3
]
showed that the modified quermassintegral
\widetilde{W}_{k}(\Omega)
is monotone with respect to the inclusion of h-convex domains.

Proposition 5.1

(
[
3
]
)
.

If
\Omega_{0}
and
\Omega_{1}
are h-convex domains with
\Omega_{0}\subset\Omega_{1}
, then
\widetilde{W}_{k}(\Omega_{0})\leq\widetilde{W}_{k}(\Omega_{1})
.

Furthermore, they gave the following evolution equations (see
[
3
, Lemma 2.4]
) of
\widetilde{W}_{k}(\Omega)
along general flows.

Lemma 5.1

(
[
3
]
)
.

Along the flow
\partial_{t}X=\mathscr{F}\nu
, the modified quermassintegral
\widetilde{W}_{k}
of the evolving domain
\Omega_{t}
satisfies

\frac{d}{dt}\widetilde{W}_{k}(\Omega_{t})=\int_{M_{t}}p_{k}(\tilde{\kappa})\mathscr{F}d\mu_{t},\quad k=0,\ldots,n.

Now we introduce the mixed modified quermassintegrals which are defined by the variation of modified quermassintegrals along the hyperbolic
p
-sum in Definition
1.1
.

Definition 5.1
.

Let
0\leq k\leq n
be an integer and
\frac{1}{2}\leq p\leq 2
be a real number. We define the
p
-mixed
k
-th modified quermassintegral of two smooth uniformly h-convex bounded domains
K
,
L\subset\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
by

\widetilde{W}_{p,k}(K,L)=\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\frac{\widetilde{W}_{k}(K+_{p}t\cdot L)-\widetilde{W}_{k}(K)}{t}.

To calculate the integral representation of
\widetilde{W}_{p,k}(K,L)
, we should study the scalar evolution equation of the horospherical support function
u(z)
along a general flow
\partial_{t}X=\mathscr{F}\nu
. Now we assume that, on a small time interval, the solution to the above flow is given by a vector-valued function
X(z,t)
defined on
\mathbb{S}^{n}\times[0,\varepsilon)
, where
X(z,t)
is determined by function
\varphi(z,t)
and expression (
2.9
). By (
2.8
), (
2.10
) and DeTurck’s trick, we have

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}=

\displaystyle\langle\partial_{t}X(z,t),\nu\rangle=\langle\partial_{t}\left(X(z,t)-\nu(z,t)\right),\nu\rangle=\langle\partial_{t}\left(\frac{(z,1)}{\varphi(z,t)}\right),\nu\rangle

(5.3)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\varphi}\partial_{t}u(z,t)\langle(z,1),\nu\rangle=\partial_{t}u(z,t).

Hence, the scalar evolution equation of
u(z,t)
is given by
\partial_{t}u(z,t)=\mathscr{F}
. Equivalently,

\partial_{t}\varphi(z,t)=\varphi\mathscr{F}.

We also refer readers to
[
3
, Proposition 5.3]
for details.

Denote by
\tilde{\lambda}=\tilde{\kappa}^{-1}=(\frac{1}{\kappa_{1}-1},\ldots,\frac{1}{\kappa_{n}-1})
the
shifted principal radii of curvature
of a smooth uniformly h-convex hypersurface
M\subset\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
.

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
k
,
p
,
K
and
L
satisfy the assumptions in Definition
5.1
. Then the integral representation of the
p
-mixed
k
-th modified quermassintegral
\widetilde{W}_{p,k}(K,L)
is given by

\widetilde{W}_{p,k}(K,L)=\frac{1}{p}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}\varphi_{L}^{p}(z)\varphi_{K}^{-p-k}(z)p_{n-k}(A[\varphi_{K}(z)])d\sigma.

Proof.

Let
\Omega_{t}=K+_{p}t\cdot L
. Then the horospherical support function of
\Omega_{t}
is given by

u(z,t)=\frac{1}{p}\log(\varphi_{K}^{p}(z)+t\varphi_{L}^{p}(z)).

By Definition
5.1
, we know

\widetilde{W}_{p,k}(K,L)=\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}\widetilde{W}_{p,k}(\Omega_{t}).

Using (
5.3
), we have that the initial speed of the above variation is given by

\frac{\partial}{\partial t}u(z,0)=\frac{1}{p}\frac{\varphi_{L}^{p}(z)}{\varphi_{K}^{p}(z)}.

Then Lemma
5.1
implies

(5.4)

\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}\widetilde{W}_{k}(\Omega_{t})=\frac{1}{p}\int_{\partial K}\varphi_{L}^{p}\varphi_{K}^{-p}p_{k}(\tilde{\kappa})d\mu.

Using (
2.16
) and (
2.15
), we have

(5.5)

\varphi_{K}^{-p}p_{k}(\tilde{\kappa})d\mu=\varphi_{K}^{-p}\frac{p_{n-k}(\tilde{\lambda})}{p_{n}(\tilde{\lambda})}\det A[\varphi_{K}]d\sigma=\varphi_{K}^{-p-k}p_{n-k}(A[\varphi_{K}])d\sigma.

Then Lemma
5.2
follows by substituting (
5.5
) into (
5.4
).
∎

Definition 5.2
.

Let
0\leq k\leq n
be an integer and
p
be a real number. The
k
-th horospherical
p
-surface area measure
of a smooth uniformly h-convex bounded domain
K\subset\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
is defined by

dS_{p,k}(K,z)=\varphi_{K}^{-p-k}p_{n-k}(A[\varphi_{K}])d\sigma.

Particularly, we call
dS(K,z):=dS_{0,0}(K,z)
the
horospherical surface area measure
of
K
.

By Lemma
5.2
and Definition
5.2
, we have

\widetilde{W}_{p,k}(K,L)=\frac{1}{p}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}\varphi_{L}^{p}dS_{p,k}(K,z).

This motivates us to propose the following two prescribed measure problems by using Definition
5.2
, which are nonlinear elliptic PDEs on
\mathbb{S}^{n}
.

Problem 5.1

(Horospherical
p
-Minkowski problem)
.

Let
n\geq 1
be an integer and
p
be a real number. Given a smooth positive function
f(z)
defined on
\mathbb{S}^{n}
, what are necessary and sufficient conditions for
f(z)
, such that there exists a smooth uniformly h-convex bounded domain
K\subset\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
satisfying

dS_{p,0}\left(K,z\right)=f(z)d\sigma.

That is, finding a smooth positive solution
\varphi(z)
to

(5.6)

\varphi^{-p}(z)p_{n}\left(A[\varphi(z)]\right)=f(z),

such that
A[\varphi(z)]>0
for all
z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
.

Remark 5.1
.

Even though we assumed the smoothness of
f(z)
in Problem
5.1
, the polytopal version of Problem
5.1
is still interesting. Recall that the classical Minkowski problem for polytopes was proposed and completely solved by Minkowski
[
64
]
, and the
L_{p}
Minkowski problem for polytopes was also well studied, see e.g.
[
49
,
11
,
79
,
80
]
. The counterpart of Problem
5.1
for h-convex polytopes (intersections of finite horo-balls) has been called the discrete horospherical
p
-Minkowski problem and has been studied in a recent joint work of the authors with Yao Wan
[
52
]
.

Problem 5.2

(Horospherical
p
-Christoffel-Minkowski problem)
.

Let
n\geq 2
and
1\leq k\leq n-1
be integers, and let
p
be a real number. For a given smooth positive function
f(z)
defined on
\mathbb{S}^{n}
, what are necessary and sufficient conditions for
f(z)
, such that there exists a smooth uniformly h-convex bounded domain
K\subset\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
satisfying

dS_{p,k}\left(K,z\right)=f(z)d\sigma.

That is, finding a smooth positive solution
\varphi(z)
to

(5.7)

\varphi^{-p-k}(z)p_{n-k}\left(A[\varphi(z)]\right)=f(z),

such that
A[\varphi(z)]>0
for all
z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
.

6.
Hyperbolic
p
-dilation and Steiner formulas

In Section
6
, we study the (weighted) Steiner formulas for modified quermassintegrals (weighted volume) of smooth h-convex bounded domains.

6.1.
Hyperbolic
p
-dilation

Definition 6.1
.

Let
p
and
a
be real numbers such that
p>0
and
a\geq 1
, and let
K
be a smooth uniformly h-convex bounded domain in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}

(n\geq 1)
with horospherical support function
u_{K}(z)
. We allow
K
to degenerate to a single point. We define the hyperbolic
p
-dilation
\Omega:=a\cdot_{p}K
of
K
by the smooth uniformly h-convex bounded domain with horospherical support function

u_{\Omega}(z):=u_{K}(z)+\frac{1}{p}\log a.

Equivalently,

\Omega=\bigcap_{z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}}\overline{B}_{z}(u_{\Omega}(z)):=\bigcap_{z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}}\{X\in\mathbb{H}^{n+1}:\log(-\langle X,(z,1)\rangle)\leq u_{\Omega}(z)\}.

By the first case in Proposition
4.1
and Proposition
2.1
, the above Definition
6.1
is well-defined.

Definition 6.2
.

Let
K
and
L
be smooth uniformly h-convex bounded domains in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. Then
K
and
L
are called hyperbolic dilates if there is a constant
c\geq 1
such that either
K=c\cdot_{1}L
or
L=c\cdot_{1}K
.

From Definition
6.1
, it is easy to see that
K
and
L
are hyperbolic dilates if and only if
\varphi_{K}^{-1}(z)\varphi_{L}(z)=e^{u_{L}(z)-u_{K}(z)}
is constant on
\mathbb{S}^{n}
.

In the context, we write
a\cdot K
for
a\cdot_{p}K
if there is no confusion about the choice of
p
. The following Proposition
6.1
follows directly from Case (1) of Proposition
4.1
.

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
n
,
p
,
a
,
K
satisfy the same assumptions as in Definition
6.1
. Then Definition
6.1
is well-defined. Furthermore, except the case that
a=1
and
K
is a single point,
\Omega=a\cdot_{p}K
is a smooth, non-degenerate and uniformly h-convex bounded domain.

Now we give a geometric description of the hyperbolic
p
-dilation.

Proposition 6.2
.

Definition
6.1
is compatible with Definition
1.3
. Let
n\geq 1
be an integer and
p>0
be a real number. Let
K
be a set in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
and
\Omega:=a\cdot_{p}K
. Then
\Omega
is the outer parallel set of
K
with distance
\frac{1}{p}\log a
, i.e.

\Omega=\{X\in\mathbb{H}^{n+1}:d_{\mathbb{H}^{n+1}}\left(X,K\right)\leq\frac{1}{p}\log a\}.

Proof.

In this proof, we use
a\ \widetilde{\cdot}_{p}\ K
to denote the dilation we defined in Definition
1.3
. At first, we prove that
a\ \widetilde{\cdot}_{p}\ K=a^{\frac{1}{p}}\ \widetilde{\cdot}_{1}\ K
. Using (
1.1
) and (
1.2
), we have
P(p,t;a,X,0,\cdot)=X
and
R(p,t;a,X,0,\cdot)=(1-t)^{\frac{1}{q}}a^{\frac{1}{p}}
, where
q
satisfies
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1
. According to Definition
1.2
, it is easy to show that

\displaystyle\bigcup_{t\in[0,1]}B(p,t;a,X,0,\cdot)=

\displaystyle B(a^{\frac{1}{p}},X,0,\cdot),\quad p>1,

\displaystyle\bigcap_{t\in[0,1]}B(p,t;a,X,0,\cdot)=

\displaystyle B(a^{\frac{1}{p}},X,0,\cdot),\quad 0<p<1,

and
B(a^{\frac{1}{p}},X,0,\cdot)=B(X,\frac{1}{p}\log a)
is the geodesic ball of radius
\frac{1}{p}\log a
centered at
X
. Using Definition
1.3
, this implies

(6.1)

\displaystyle a\ \widetilde{\cdot}_{p}\ K=

\displaystyle a^{\frac{1}{p}}\ \widetilde{\cdot}_{1}\ K,

(6.2)

\displaystyle a\ \widetilde{\cdot}_{p}\ K=

\displaystyle\{X\in\mathbb{H}^{n+1}:d_{\mathbb{H}^{n+1}}\left(X,K\right)\leq\frac{1}{p}\log a\}.

Now we assume that
K
is a smooth uniformly h-convex bounded domain.
By Definition
6.1
, it is easy to see that
\Omega=a\cdot_{p}K=a^{\frac{1}{p}}\cdot_{1}K
. By Theorem
4.4
and (
6.1
), we know
\Omega=a\cdot_{p}K=a^{\frac{1}{p}}\ \widetilde{\cdot}_{1}\ K=a\ \widetilde{\cdot}_{p}\ K
. Hence Definition
6.1
is compatible with Definition
1.3
, and
\Omega
can be described by using (
6.2
). We complete the proof of Proposition
6.2
.
∎

6.2.
Steiner formulas for modified quermassintegrals in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}

For a fixed smooth h-convex bounded domain
\Omega=\Omega_{0}
with horospherical support function
u(z)
, we let
\Omega_{t}=e^{t}\cdot_{1}\Omega
. Then
\Omega_{t}
is the h-convex domain with horospherical support function
u_{t}(z)=u(z)+t
. Define
\varphi_{t}(z)=e^{u_{t}(z)}
, then
\varphi_{t}(z)=e^{t}\varphi(z)
. The h-convexity of
\Omega_{t}
follows from Proposition
6.1
. According to Proposition
6.2
