\displaystyle-\frac{\langle D\varphi,D\mathcal{F}\rangle}{\varphi\mathcal{F}^{2}}\delta_{ij}-\frac{\cosh r}{\varphi\mathcal{F}}\delta_{ij}-\left(\frac{n+p}{n-k}-1\right)PA_{ij}[\varphi]

\displaystyle+\frac{n+p}{n-k}\left(\frac{n+p}{n-k}-1\right)\frac{P\varphi_{i}\varphi_{j}}{\varphi}+\frac{n+p}{n-k}P\tilde{u}\delta_{ij}+\frac{P}{\varphi}\delta_{ij},

where we used (
3.22
) and (
3.23
). We complete the proof of Lemma
7.7
.
∎

Lemma 7.8
.

Along the scalar evolution equation (
7.11
) of
\varphi(z,t)
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\log\mathcal{F}(z,t)=

\displaystyle\frac{\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ij}}{\mathcal{F}^{2}}D_{j}D_{i}\log\mathcal{F}-\frac{\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ij}}{\mathcal{F}^{2}}D_{i}\log\mathcal{F}D_{j}\log\mathcal{F}+2\frac{\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ij}}{\mathcal{F}}\Phi D_{i}hD_{j}\left(\varphi^{-\frac{n+p}{n-k}+1}\right)

\displaystyle+\Phi\varphi^{-\frac{n+p}{n-k}+1}\frac{\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ij}}{\mathcal{F}}D_{j}D_{i}h-\Phi\langle D\varphi,Dh\rangle\varphi^{-\frac{n+p}{n-k}}\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ii}}{\mathcal{F}}-\frac{\langle D\varphi,D\mathcal{F}\rangle}{\varphi\mathcal{F}^{3}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ii}

\displaystyle-\frac{\cosh r}{\varphi\mathcal{F}^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ii}-\left(\frac{n+p}{n-k}-1\right)P+\frac{n+p}{n-k}\left(\frac{n+p}{n-k}-1\right)\frac{P}{\varphi\mathcal{F}}\mathcal{F}^{ij}\varphi_{i}\varphi_{j}

(7.86)

\displaystyle+\frac{n+p}{n-k}P\tilde{u}\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ii}}{\mathcal{F}}+\frac{P}{\varphi}\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ii}}{\mathcal{F}},

and

(7.87)

\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\log(\varphi(z,t)-a)=P\frac{\varphi}{\varphi-a}-\frac{1}{(\varphi-a)\mathcal{F}},

where
a
is a constant satisfying
\varphi(z,t)-a>0
.

Proof.

Since
\partial_{t}\mathcal{F}(z,t)=\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ij}\partial_{t}A_{ij}[\varphi(z,t)]
, formulas (
7.81
) and (
7.78
) imply

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\log\mathcal{F}(z,t)=

\displaystyle\frac{\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ij}}{\mathcal{F}}\partial_{t}A_{ij}[\varphi(z,t)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ij}}{\mathcal{F}^{3}}D_{j}D_{i}\mathcal{F}-2\frac{\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ij}}{\mathcal{F}^{4}}D_{i}\mathcal{F}D_{j}\mathcal{F}+2\frac{\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ij}}{\mathcal{F}}\Phi D_{i}hD_{j}\left(\varphi^{-\frac{n+p}{n-k}+1}\right)

\displaystyle+\Phi\varphi^{-\frac{n+p}{n-k}+1}\frac{\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ij}}{\mathcal{F}}D_{j}D_{i}h-\Phi\langle D\varphi,Dh\rangle\varphi^{-\frac{n+p}{n-k}}\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ii}}{\mathcal{F}}-\frac{\langle D\varphi,D\mathcal{F}\rangle}{\varphi\mathcal{F}^{3}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ii}

\displaystyle-\frac{\cosh r}{\varphi\mathcal{F}^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ii}-\left(\frac{n+p}{n-k}-1\right)P+\frac{n+p}{n-k}\left(\frac{n+p}{n-k}-1\right)\frac{P}{\varphi\mathcal{F}}\mathcal{F}^{ij}\varphi_{i}\varphi_{j}

(7.88)

\displaystyle+\frac{n+p}{n-k}P\tilde{u}\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ii}}{\mathcal{F}}+\frac{P}{\varphi}\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ii}}{\mathcal{F}}.

A direct calculation yields

(7.89)

\displaystyle\frac{\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ij}}{\mathcal{F}^{3}}D_{j}D_{i}\mathcal{F}-2\frac{\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ij}}{\mathcal{F}^{4}}D_{i}\mathcal{F}D_{j}\mathcal{F}=\frac{\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ij}}{\mathcal{F}^{2}}D_{j}D_{i}\log\mathcal{F}-\frac{\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ij}}{\mathcal{F}^{2}}D_{i}\log\mathcal{F}D_{j}\log\mathcal{F}.

Inserting (
7.89
) into the right-hand side of (
7.88
), we obtain (
7.86
).

Recall that
P(z,t)=\Phi(t)h(z)\varphi^{-\frac{n+p}{n-k}}(z,t)
.
Then equation (
7.11
) implies

\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\log(\varphi(z,t)-a)=\frac{\partial_{t}\varphi(z,t)}{\varphi(z,t)-a}=P\frac{\varphi}{\varphi-a}-\frac{1}{(\varphi-a)\mathcal{F}}.

This completes the proof of Lemma
7.8
.
∎

Lemma 7.9
.

Suppose that one of the following assumptions of
n
,
k
,
p
,
f(z)
, and
M_{t}
holds:

•

k=0
,
-\infty<p<+\infty
,
n\geq 1
,
f(z)
is a smooth positive even function on
\mathbb{S}^{n}
, and
M_{t}
is a smooth, origin symmetric uniformly h-convex solution to flow
(
7.7
)
;

•

1\leq k\leq n-1
,
p\geq-n
,
n\geq 2
,
f(z)
is a smooth positive even function on
\mathbb{S}^{n}
satisfying Assumption
7.1
, and
M_{t}
is a smooth, origin symmetric uniformly h-convex solution to the flow (
7.7
).

Then there is a positive constant
C
depending only on
n
,
k
,
p
,
\min_{z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}}f(z)
,
||f(z)||_{C^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{n})}
and
M_{0}
such that

(7.90)

\mathcal{F}(z,t)\leq C,\quad\forall(z,t)\in\mathbb{S}^{n}\times[0,T).

Proof.

At first, we explain the assumptions of
n
,
k
,
p
,
f(z)
, and
M_{t}
in Lemma
7.9
. By Lemma
7.2
, Corollary
7.1
and Lemma
7.4
, the origin-symmetry of
M_{t}
implies the
C^{0}
,
C^{1}
estimates of
\varphi(z,t)
and the uniform upper bound of
\Phi(t)
along the flow (
7.7
). If
n=1
and
k=0
,
then Lemma
7.4
implies that
\Phi(t)

stays uniformly away from zero
along the flow (
7.7
). If
n\geq 2
and
0\leq k\leq n-1
, then by Proposition
7.1
and the assumptions in Lemma
7.9
, we can get the pinching estimates along the flow (
7.7
). Then we get the lower bound of
\Phi(t)
by use of Lemma
7.4
. Hence
P(z,t):=\Phi(t)h(z)\varphi^{-\frac{n+p}{n-k}}(z,t)
has a uniform positive lower bound along the flow (
7.7
). By the above arguments, under the assumptions of
n
,
k
,
p
,
f(z)
, and
M_{t}
in Lemma
7.9
, there exists a positive constant
C
such that

(7.91)

\displaystyle 1+\frac{1}{C}\leq

\displaystyle\varphi(z,t)\leq C,

(7.92)

\displaystyle\quad|D\varphi(z,t)|\leq

\displaystyle C,

(7.93)

\displaystyle\quad\frac{1}{C}\leq

\displaystyle\Phi(t)\leq C,

(7.94)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{C}\leq

\displaystyle P(z,t)\leq C,

(7.95)

\displaystyle\quad\frac{1}{C}\leq

\displaystyle\frac{\tilde{\kappa}_{i}}{\tilde{\kappa}_{j}}\leq C,\quad 1\leq i,j\leq n

along the flow (
7.7
), and
C
only depends on
n
,
k
,
p
,
\min_{z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}}f(z)
,
||f(z)||_{C^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{n})}
and
M_{0}
.

Now we define an auxiliary function

(7.96)

Q(z,t)=\log\mathcal{F}(z,t)-b\log(\varphi(z,t)-a),

where
a\in[0,1]
and
b>0
are constants that will be chosen later in this proof. By (
7.91
) and the assumption
a\in[0,1]
, we have
\varphi-a
is bounded between two uniform positive constants along the flow (
7.7
). Hence, in order for (
7.90
), we only need to show
Q(z,t)\leq C
.
Using (
2.15
) and (
7.95
), we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ii}(z,t)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n-k}p_{n-k}^{\frac{-n+k+1}{n-k}}(A[\varphi])\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\partial p_{n-k}(A[\varphi])}{\partial A_{ii}[\varphi]}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\min_{1\leq i\leq n}(A_{ii}[\varphi])^{-n+k+1}\max_{1\leq j\leq n}(A_{jj}[\varphi])^{n-k-1}

(7.97)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq i,j\leq n}\left(\frac{\tilde{\kappa}_{j}}{\tilde{\kappa}_{i}}\right)^{n-k-1}(x,t)\leq C.

Applying (
7.91
), (
7.92
), (
7.93
) and (
7.97
) to the right-hand side of (
7.86
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\log\mathcal{F}(z,t)\leq

\displaystyle\frac{\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ij}}{\mathcal{F}^{2}}D_{j}D_{i}\log\mathcal{F}-\frac{\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ij}}{\mathcal{F}^{2}}D_{i}\log\mathcal{F}D_{j}\log\mathcal{F}-\frac{\langle D\varphi,D\log\mathcal{F}\rangle}{\varphi\mathcal{F}^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ii}

(7.98)

\displaystyle-\left(\frac{n+p}{n-k}-1\right)P+C\frac{1}{\mathcal{F}}+C\frac{1}{\mathcal{F}^{2}}

for some constant
C
.
Inserting (
7.87
) into (
7.96
), we obtain

(7.99)

\frac{\partial}{\partial t}Q(z,t)=\partial_{t}\log\mathcal{F}-bP\frac{\varphi}{\varphi-a}+\frac{b}{(\varphi-a)\mathcal{F}}.

For a fixed time
t_{0}\in[0,T)
, we have the following critical equations at the spacial maximum point
z_{0}
of
Q(\cdot,t_{0})
,

(7.100)

D_{i}\log\mathcal{F}=bD_{i}\log(\varphi-a),\quad D_{j}D_{i}\log\mathcal{F}\leq bD_{j}D_{i}\log(\varphi-a).

Hence, by using (
7.100
), (
7.91
), (
7.92
) and (
7.97
), at
(z_{0},t_{0})
we have

\displaystyle\frac{\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ij}}{\mathcal{F}^{2}}D_{j}D_{i}\log\mathcal{F}-\frac{\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ij}}{\mathcal{F}^{2}}D_{i}\log\mathcal{F}D_{j}\log\mathcal{F}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ij}}{\mathcal{F}^{2}}\left(bD_{j}D_{i}\log(\varphi-a)-b^{2}D_{i}\log(\varphi-a)D_{j}\log(\varphi-a)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ij}}{\mathcal{F}^{2}}\left(\frac{b\varphi_{ij}}{\varphi-a}-\frac{(b^{2}+b)\varphi_{i}\varphi_{j}}{(\varphi-a)^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{b\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ij}}{\mathcal{F}^{2}(\varphi-a)}\left(\varphi_{ij}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{|D\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}\delta_{ij}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\varphi-\frac{1}{\varphi}\right)\delta_{ij}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{b\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ii}}{\mathcal{F}^{2}(\varphi-a)}\left(\frac{1}{2}\frac{|D\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}-\frac{1}{2}\left(\varphi-\frac{1}{\varphi}\right)\right)-\frac{(b^{2}+b)\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ij}\varphi_{i}\varphi_{j}}{\mathcal{F}^{2}(\varphi-a)^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{b}{\mathcal{F}(\varphi-a)}+\frac{b\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ii}}{\mathcal{F}^{2}(\varphi-a)}\left(\frac{1}{2}\frac{|D\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}-\frac{1}{2}\left(\varphi-\frac{1}{\varphi}\right)\right)-\frac{(b^{2}+b)\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ij}\varphi_{i}\varphi_{j}}{\mathcal{F}^{2}(\varphi-a)^{2}}

(7.101)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\frac{b}{\mathcal{F}}+C\frac{b^{2}+b}{\mathcal{F}^{2}},

where
C
is a large positive constant depending only on
n
,
k
,
p
,
||f(z)||_{C^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{n})}
and
M_{0}
.
Using (
7.100
) and (
7.79
), at
(z_{0},t_{0})
we have

(7.102)

\displaystyle-\frac{\langle D\varphi,D\log\mathcal{F}\rangle}{\varphi\mathcal{F}^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ii}=-\frac{b|D\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi(\varphi-a)\mathcal{F}^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ii}\leq 0.

Inserting (
7.101
) and (
7.102
) into (
7.98
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\log\mathcal{F}(z,t)\leq

\displaystyle C\frac{b}{\mathcal{F}}+C\frac{b^{2}+b}{\mathcal{F}^{2}}-\left(\frac{n+p}{n-k}-1\right)P+C\frac{1}{\mathcal{F}}+C\frac{1}{\mathcal{F}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle-\left(\frac{n+p}{n-k}-1\right)P+C\frac{b+1}{\mathcal{F}}+C\frac{b^{2}+b+1}{\mathcal{F}^{2}}

at
(z_{0},t_{0})
,
where
C
is a uniform positive constant.
This together with (
7.99
) gives

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}Q(z,t)\leq-\left(\frac{n+p}{n-k}-1+b\frac{\varphi}{\varphi-a}\right)P+C\frac{b}{\mathcal{F}}+C\frac{b^{2}+b+1}{\mathcal{F}^{2}}

at
(z_{0},t_{0})
.
By (
7.91
), we can choose
a=1
, then
\left(\varphi-1\right)^{-1}\varphi>c>0
for some constant
c
depending only on
n
,
k
and
M_{0}
;
hence, we can choose a large positive constant
b
such that
\frac{n+p}{n-k}-1+bc\geq 1
.
By (
7.94
), it is easy to see that
\partial_{t}Q<0
at
(z_{0},t_{0})
for sufficiently large
\mathcal{F}
. By using the maximum principle and (
7.91
), we have
Q(z,t)\leq C
for some uniform positive constant
C
. Then we obtain (
7.90
). This completes the proof of Lemma
7.9
.
∎

Lemma 7.10
.

Let
n\geq 1
,
0\leq k\leq n-1
, and
-\infty<p<+\infty
.
Let
f(z)
be a smooth positive even function on
\mathbb{S}^{n}
.
Suppose that
M_{t}=\partial\Omega_{t}
is a smooth, origin symmetric uniformly h-convex solution to the flow (
7.7
).
Then there is a positive constant
C
depending only on
n
,
k
,
p
,
||f(z)||_{C^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{n})}
and
M_{0}
such that

(7.103)

\mathcal{F}(z,t)\geq C^{-1},\quad\forall(z,t)\in\mathbb{S}^{n}\times[0,T).

Proof.

Since
M_{t}=\partial\Omega_{t}
is origin symmetric, we have the
C^{0}
,
C^{1}
estimates of
\varphi(z,t)=e^{u(z,t)}
in (
7.17
) and (
7.22
). Moreover, we know from Lemma
7.4
that the global term
\Phi(t)
is bounded from above by a uniform positive constant along the flow (
7.7
). Therefore, there exists a large constant
C
depending only on
n
,
k
,
p
,
||f||_{C^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{n})}
such that

(7.104)

\displaystyle 1+\frac{1}{C}\leq\varphi(z,t)\leq C,

(7.105)

\displaystyle|D\varphi|\leq C,

(7.106)

\displaystyle 0<\Phi(t)\leq C,

(7.107)

\displaystyle P(z,t)\leq C.

Now we define an auxiliary function

(7.108)

Q(z,t)=\log\mathcal{F}(z,t)+\log(\varphi(z,t)-a),

where
a\in[0,1]
is a positive constant that will be chosen later in this proof.
By (
7.104
), in order for (
7.103
), we only need to show
Q(z,t)\geq-C
for some uniform positive constant
C
.
For a fixed time
t_{0}\in[0,T)
, at the spacial minimum point
z_{0}
of
Q(\cdot,t_{0})
, we have the critical equations

(7.109)

D_{i}\log\mathcal{F}=-D_{i}\log(\varphi-a),\quad D_{j}D_{i}\log\mathcal{F}\geq-D_{j}D_{i}\log(\varphi-a).

Hence, at
(z_{0},t_{0})
we have

\displaystyle\frac{\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ij}}{\mathcal{F}^{2}}D_{j}D_{i}\log\mathcal{F}-\frac{\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ij}}{\mathcal{F}^{2}}D_{i}\log\mathcal{F}D_{j}\log\mathcal{F}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ij}}{\mathcal{F}^{2}}\left(-D_{j}D_{i}\log(\varphi-a)-D_{i}\log(\varphi-a)D_{j}\log(\varphi-a)\right)=-\frac{\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ij}}{\mathcal{F}^{2}(\varphi-a)}\varphi_{ij}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ij}}{\mathcal{F}^{2}(\varphi-a)}\left(\varphi_{ij}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{|D\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}\delta_{ij}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\varphi-\frac{1}{\varphi}\right)\delta_{ij}\right)-\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ii}}{\mathcal{F}^{2}(\varphi-a)}\left(\frac{1}{2}\frac{|D\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}-\frac{1}{2}\left(\varphi-\frac{1}{\varphi}\right)\right)

(7.110)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\mathcal{F}(\varphi-a)}-\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ii}}{\mathcal{F}^{2}(\varphi-a)}\left(\frac{1}{2}\frac{|D\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}-\frac{1}{2}\left(\varphi-\frac{1}{\varphi}\right)\right).

Inserting (
7.110
) into (
7.86
) and using (
7.87
), at
(z_{0},t_{0})
we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}Q(z,t)=

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\log\mathcal{F}(z,t)+\partial_{t}\log(\varphi(z,t)-a)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle-\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ii}}{\mathcal{F}^{2}(\varphi-a)}\left(\frac{1}{2}\frac{|D\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}-\frac{1}{2}\left(\varphi-\frac{1}{\varphi}\right)\right)-\frac{1}{\mathcal{F}(\varphi-a)}+2\frac{\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ij}}{\mathcal{F}}\Phi D_{i}hD_{j}\left(\varphi^{-\frac{n+p}{n-k}+1}\right)

\displaystyle+\Phi\varphi^{-\frac{n+p}{n-k}+1}\frac{\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ij}}{\mathcal{F}}D_{j}D_{i}h-\Phi\langle D\varphi,Dh\rangle\varphi^{-\frac{n+p}{n-k}}\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ii}}{\mathcal{F}}-\frac{\langle D\varphi,D\mathcal{F}\rangle}{\varphi\mathcal{F}^{3}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ii}

\displaystyle-\frac{\cosh r}{\varphi\mathcal{F}^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ii}-\left(\frac{n+p}{n-k}-1\right)P+\frac{n+p}{n-k}\left(\frac{n+p}{n-k}-1\right)\frac{P}{\varphi\mathcal{F}}\mathcal{F}^{ij}\varphi_{i}\varphi_{j}

\displaystyle+\frac{n+p}{n-k}P\tilde{u}\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ii}}{\mathcal{F}}+\frac{P}{\varphi}\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ii}}{\mathcal{F}}

(7.111)

\displaystyle+P\frac{\varphi}{\varphi-a}-\frac{1}{(\varphi-a)\mathcal{F}}

Using the estimates (
7.104
)–(
7.107
) in the right-hand side of (
7.111
), at
(z_{0},t_{0})
we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}Q(z,t)\geq

\displaystyle-\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ii}}{\mathcal{F}^{2}(\varphi-a)}\left(\frac{1}{2}\frac{|D\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}-\frac{1}{2}\left(\varphi-\frac{1}{\varphi}\right)\right)-\frac{\langle D\varphi,D\log\mathcal{F}\rangle}{\varphi\mathcal{F}^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ii}

(7.112)

\displaystyle-\frac{\cosh r}{\varphi\mathcal{F}^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ii}-C\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ii}}{\mathcal{F}}-\frac{C}{\mathcal{F}}-C.

Using the critical equation (
7.109
) and (
3.16
), we know

(7.113)

-\frac{\langle D\varphi,D\log\mathcal{F}\rangle}{\varphi\mathcal{F}^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ii}=\frac{|D\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi(\varphi-a)}\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ii}}{\mathcal{F}^{2}}\geq 0.

By (
7.104
), we can choose
a=1
.
Plugging (
7.113
) into (
7.112
), and using (
3.22
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}Q(z,t)\geq

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ii}}{\mathcal{F}^{2}\varphi(\varphi-1)}\left(-\frac{1}{2}|D\varphi|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\varphi^{2}-\frac{1}{2}+|D\varphi|^{2}-\cosh r\left(\varphi-1\right)\right)

\displaystyle-C\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ii}}{\mathcal{F}}-\frac{C}{\mathcal{F}}-C

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ii}}{\mathcal{F}^{2}\varphi(\varphi-1)}\left(\cosh r\varphi-1-\cosh r\left(\varphi-1\right)\right)-C\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ii}}{\mathcal{F}}-\frac{C}{\mathcal{F}}-C

(7.114)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ii}}{\mathcal{F}^{2}\varphi(\varphi-1)}(\cosh r-1)-C\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ii}}{\mathcal{F}}-\frac{C}{\mathcal{F}}-C

at
(z_{0},t_{0})
.
Note that (
3.22
) and (
7.105
) imply

(7.115)

\cosh r-1\geq\frac{1}{2}\left(\varphi+\frac{1}{\varphi}\right)-1>c>0

for some constant
c
depending only on
n
,
k
and
M_{0}
. Using (
7.115
) in the right-hand side of (
7.114
), we arrive at

(7.116)

\frac{\partial}{\partial t}Q(z,t)\geq c\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ii}}{\mathcal{F}^{2}}-C\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ii}}{\mathcal{F}}-\frac{C}{\mathcal{F}}-C

at
(z_{0},t_{0})
,
where
C>0
only depends on
n
,
k
,
p
,
||f(z)||_{C^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{n})}
and
M_{0}
.
Besides, we have
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ii}\geq 1
by (
7.79
).
Hence, the estimate (
7.116
) implies
\partial_{t}Q(z,t)\geq 0
for sufficiently small
\mathcal{F}>0
.
We therefore prove that
Q(z,t)\geq-C
for some constant
C
along the flow (
7.7
) by using the maximum principle and (
7.104
). Consequently, we obtain the desired estimate (
7.103
). This completes the proof of Lemma
7.10
.
∎

7.3.
Long time existence and convergence

In this subsection, we give the proofs of Theorem
7.2
–
7.4
.

Proof of Theorem
7.4
.

If
A_{ij}[\varphi]>0
, then (
7.80
) implies

\frac{\partial}{\partial\varphi_{pq}}\left(-\mathcal{F}^{-1}(A[\varphi])\right)=\mathcal{F}^{-2}\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{ij}>0.

Hence, equation (
7.11
) is parabolic at
t=0
provided that
A_{ij}[\varphi(z,0)]>0
on
\mathbb{S}^{n}
.
Consequently, as long as the initial smooth hypersurface
M_{0}
is uniformly h-convex, the flow (
7.7
) exits for a short time.
Note that
M_{t}
is origin symmetric along the flow (
7.7
), which follows from
the evenness of
f(z)
and equation (
7.11
).

In order to prove the long time existence of the flow (
7.7
), let us summarize the a priori estimates in Subsection
7.2
.
In the case
n\geq 2
, by the pinching estimates in Proposition
7.1
and the bounds of
\mathcal{F}
proved in Lemma
7.9
and Lemma
7.10
, we know that the shifted principal curvatures
\tilde{\kappa}_{i}=\kappa_{i}-1
are bounded between two positive constants along the flow (
7.7
). Here we remark that in the case
n=1
and
k=0
, we have
C^{-1}\leq\tilde{\kappa}\leq C
by Lemma
7.9
and Lemma
7.10
directly. Combining this fact with the
C^{0}
estimates in Lemma
7.2
and the
C^{1}
estimates in Corollary
7.1
, we have

||\varphi(z,t)||_{C_{z,t}^{2,1}(\mathbb{S}^{n}\times[0,T))}\leq C_{2,1}

for some constant
C_{2,1}
. Hence equation (
7.11
) is uniformly parabolic on
\mathbb{S}^{n}\times[0,T)
.
By (
3.12
),
\mathcal{F}(A[\varphi])=p_{n-k}^{\frac{1}{n-k}}(A[\varphi])
is concave for
A[\varphi]>0
, then

\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\varphi_{pq}\partial\varphi_{rs}}\left(-\mathcal{F}^{-1}(A[\varphi])\right)B_{pq}B_{rs}=\mathcal{F}^{-2}\ddot{\mathcal{F}}^{pq,rs}B_{pq}B_{rs}-2\mathcal{F}^{-3}\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{pq}B_{pq}\dot{\mathcal{F}}^{rs}B_{rs}\leq 0

for
B_{pq}\in{\rm Sym}(n)
.
We conclude that
-\mathcal{F}^{-1}
is concave with respect to
\varphi_{pq}
.
In Lemma
7.4
, we proved that
\Phi(t)
is bounded between two uniform positive constants.

By the above estimates, we can apply the a priori estimates in
[
74
]
to equation (
7.11
) and get the
C^{2+\alpha,1+\frac{\alpha}{2}}
estimate of
\varphi(z,t)
, see also
[
7
,
19
]
. Then by the parabolic Schauder theory, for each nonnegative integers
i
,
j
, there exists a uniform constant
C_{i,j}
(independent of
T
) such that

(7.117)

||\varphi(z,t)||_{C_{z,t}^{i,j}(\mathbb{S}^{n}\times[0,T))}\leq C_{i,j}.

Consequently, we obtain the long time existence of the flow (
7.7
) by a standard continuation argument.
∎

Proofs of Theorem
7.2
–
7.3
.

Given any smooth even and uniformly h-convex initial hypersurface
M_{0}
, we have
J_{p}^{\prime}(\Omega_{t})\leq 0
along the flow (
7.7
) by the estimate in (
7.14
). Besides, the
C^{0}
estimate in Lemma
7.2
yields that
|J_{p}(\Omega_{t})|
is uniformly bounded from above along the flow (
7.7
).
From the estimates of higher order derivatives (
7.117
), we have that the both
|\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\varphi(z,t)|
and
|\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}\varphi(z,t)|
are bounded from above by some constant
C
along the flow. Hence
J_{p}^{\prime}(\Omega_{t})
is uniformly continuous and

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to+\infty}J_{p}^{\prime}(\Omega_{t})=0.

By the Arzelà-Ascoli theorem and a diagonal argument, there exists a time sequence
\{t_{i}\}\subset(0,+\infty)
such that
\{M_{t_{i}}\}
converge smoothly to a smooth, origin symmetric uniformly h-convex hypersurface
M=\partial\Omega
as
i\to+\infty
. Let
u(z)=\log\varphi(z)
be the horospherical support function of
\Omega
.
As

\displaystyle\lim_{i\to+\infty}J_{p}^{\prime}(\Omega_{t_{i}})=0,

we have

J_{p}^{\prime}(\Omega)=0.

Then by Lemma
7.1
, there exists a positive constant
\gamma
such that
\varphi(z)
satisfies

(7.118)

\displaystyle\gamma=f^{-1}(z)\varphi^{-p-n}(z)p_{n-k}(\tilde{\lambda}(x)).

Then we complete the proofs of Theorem
7.2
and Theorem
7.3
by using (
2.15
).
∎

8.
Uniqueness of solutions to Horospherical
p
-Minkowski problem and Horospherical
p
-Christoffel-Minkowski problem with constant function

8.1.
Results in Section
8

The main result in Section
8
is the following Proposition
8.1
.

Proposition 8.1
.

Let
n\geq 1
and
0\leq k<l\leq n
be integers. Assume that
\chi(s)
is a smooth positive and monotone non-decreasing function defined on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
.
Let
\Omega
be a smooth uniformly h-convex bounded domain in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. If
n\geq 2
,
k=0
, and
1\leq l\leq n
, then we assume in addition that
\Omega
contains the origin in its interior. If
M=\partial\Omega
satisfies the following equation

(8.1)

\frac{p_{l}(\tilde{\kappa})}{p_{k}(\tilde{\kappa})}=\chi(\psi(x)),

where
\psi(x)=\cosh r-\tilde{u}
for
x\in M
,
then
M
must be a geodesic sphere. In particular, if the function
\chi
is strictly increasing, then
M
is a geodesic sphere centered at the origin.

As an application of Proposition
8.1
, we consider the case that
n\geq 1
,
p\geq-n
and
f(z)
is a positive constant function in equations (
7.5
) and (
7.6
). In particular, we completely classify the solutions to these equations that satisfy
\varphi(z)>1
and
A_{ij}[\varphi(z)]>0
.

Theorem 8.1
.

Let
n\geq 1
and
0\leq k\leq n-1
be integers, let
p\geq-n
be a real number, and let
\gamma
be a positive constant. For
p>n-2k
, we let

\gamma_{0}=\frac{(2k+p-n)^{\frac{2k+p-n}{2}}(n-k)^{n-k}}{(n+p)^{\frac{n+p}{2}}}.

Consider equation

(8.2)

\varphi(z)^{-p-k}p_{n-k}\left(A[\varphi(z)]\right)=\gamma,

where
\varphi(z)
is required to satisfy
A_{ij}[\varphi(z)]>0
for all
z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
. Geometrically, the requirement of
\varphi(z)
means that
\log\varphi(z)
is the horospherical support function of a smooth uniformly h-convex bounded domain
\Omega
. If
n\geq 2
,
k=0
, and
p>-n
, then we require in addition that
\varphi(z)>1
for any
z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
.

(1)

If
p>n-2k
and
\gamma\in(0,\gamma_{0})
,
then the solutions to equation (
8.2
) are
\varphi_{1}(z)=c_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}(z)=c_{2}
, where
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
are solutions to equation

(8.3)

c^{-p-k}\left(\frac{1}{2}(c-c^{-1})\right)^{n-k}=\gamma.

Geometrically, the domains
\Omega_{1}
and
\Omega_{2}
are geodesic balls of radius
\log c_{1}
and
\log c_{2}
centered at the origin, respectively.

(2)

If
p>n-2k
and
\gamma=\gamma_{0}
,
then

\varphi(z)=\left(\frac{n+p}{2k+p-n}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

is the unique solution to equation (
8.2
), which means that
\Omega
is a geodesic ball of radius
\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{n+p}{2k+p-n}
centered at the origin.

(3)

If
p>n-2k
and
\gamma>\gamma_{0}
, then equation (
8.2
) admits no solution.

(4)

If
p=n-2k
and
\gamma\in(0,2^{k-n})
, then there exists a unique solution
\varphi(z)=c
to equation (
8.2
), where
c
solves equation (
8.3
). Equivalently,
\Omega
is a geodesic ball of radius
\log c
centered at the origin.

(5)

If
p=n-2k
and
\gamma\geq 2^{k-n}
, then equation (
8.2
) admits no solution.

(6)

If
-n<p<n-2k
, then there exists a unique solution
\varphi(z)=c
to (
8.2
) for each
\gamma\in(0,+\infty)
, where
c
solves equation (
8.3
). Equivalently,
\Omega
is a geodesic ball of radius
\log c
centered at the origin.

(7)

If
p=-n
, then the solutions to (
8.2
) are given by

\varphi(z)=\left(1+2\gamma^{\frac{1}{n-k}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left((|x|^{2}+1)^{\frac{1}{2}}-\langle x,z\rangle\right),

where
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. Geometrically,
\Omega
is a geodesic ball of radius
\frac{1}{2}\log\left(1+2\gamma^{\frac{1}{n-k}}\right)
centered at
(x,(|x|^{2}+1))\in\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
.

By using Theorem
8.1
, we can prove the convergence of the flow (
7.7
) for constant
f(z)
.

Theorem 8.2
.

Let
n\geq 1
and
0\leq k\leq n-1
be integers, let
p\geq-n
be a real number, let
f(z)
be a positive constant function defined on
\mathbb{S}^{n}
, and let
M_{0}=\partial\Omega_{0}
be a smooth, origin symmetric and uniformly h-convex hypersurface in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. Then the flow (
7.7
) converges smoothly to a
geodesic sphere
\partial B(r)
centered at the origin satisfying
\widetilde{W}_{k}(B(r))=\widetilde{W}_{k}(\Omega_{0})
.

Remark 8.1
.

In the case that
n\geq 2
,
0\leq k\leq n-1
,
p=-n
, and
f(z)
is a positive constant, the long time existence and the exponential convergence of the flow (
7.7
) was studied by using Alexandrov reflection argument in
[
3
, Theorem 1.7]
.

8.2.
Heintze-Karcher inequality and integral formulas

Proposition 8.2
.

Let
\Omega
be a smooth uniformly h-convex bounded domain in
\mathbb{H}^{2}
. Then

(8.4)

\displaystyle\int_{\partial\Omega}\left(\frac{\cosh r-\tilde{u}}{\tilde{\kappa}}-\tilde{u}\right)d\mu\geq 0.

Equality holds if and only if
\Omega
is a geodesic ball.

Proof.

Using (
3.24
), (
2.15
), (
3.23
) and (
2.16
), we have

(8.5)

\displaystyle\frac{\cosh r-\tilde{u}}{\tilde{\kappa}}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varphi\tilde{\kappa}}=A[\varphi]=\varphi^{\prime\prime}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\left(\varphi^{\prime}\right)^{2}}{\varphi}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\varphi-\frac{1}{\varphi}\right),

\displaystyle\tilde{u}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\left(\varphi^{\prime}\right)^{2}}{\varphi}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\varphi-\frac{1}{\varphi}\right),\quad d\mu=A[\varphi]d\sigma.

Then

\displaystyle\int_{\partial\Omega}\left(\frac{\cosh r-\tilde{u}}{\tilde{\kappa}}-\tilde{u}\right)d\mu=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\left(A[\varphi]-\tilde{u}\right)A[\varphi]d\sigma

(8.6)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\left(\varphi^{\prime\prime}-\frac{\left(\varphi^{\prime}\right)^{2}}{\varphi}\right)\left(\varphi^{\prime\prime}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\left(\varphi^{\prime}\right)^{2}}{\varphi}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\varphi-\frac{1}{\varphi}\right)\right)d\sigma.

By integration by parts, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\frac{\varphi^{\prime\prime}\left(\varphi^{\prime}\right)^{2}}{\varphi}d\sigma=

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\left(\frac{\left(\varphi^{\prime}\right)^{2}}{\varphi}\right)^{\prime}\varphi^{\prime}d\sigma=-\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\left(2\frac{\varphi^{\prime\prime}\varphi^{\prime}}{\varphi}-\frac{\left(\varphi^{\prime}\right)^{3}}{\varphi^{2}}\right)\varphi^{\prime}d\sigma

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\frac{\varphi^{\prime\prime}\left(\varphi^{\prime}\right)^{2}}{\varphi}d\sigma+\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\frac{\left(\varphi^{\prime}\right)^{4}}{\varphi^{2}}d\sigma,

which implies

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\left(-\frac{3}{2}\frac{\varphi^{\prime\prime}\left(\varphi^{\prime}\right)^{2}}{\varphi}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\left(\varphi^{\prime}\right)^{4}}{\varphi^{2}}\right)d\sigma=0.

Then we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\left(\varphi^{\prime\prime}-\frac{\left(\varphi^{\prime}\right)^{2}}{\varphi}\right)\left(\varphi^{\prime\prime}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\left(\varphi^{\prime}\right)^{2}}{\varphi}\right)d\sigma

(8.7)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\left(\left(\varphi^{\prime\prime}\right)^{2}-\frac{3}{2}\frac{\varphi^{\prime\prime}\left(\varphi^{\prime}\right)^{2}}{\varphi}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\left(\varphi^{\prime}\right)^{4}}{\varphi^{2}}\right)d\sigma=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\left(\varphi^{\prime\prime}\right)^{2}d\sigma.

Using integration by parts, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\left(\varphi^{\prime\prime}-\frac{\left(\varphi^{\prime}\right)^{2}}{\varphi}\right)\left(\frac{1}{2}\left(\varphi-\frac{1}{\varphi}\right)\right)d\sigma=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\left(\log\varphi\right)^{\prime\prime}\left(\frac{1}{2}\left(\varphi^{2}-1\right)\right)d\sigma

(8.8)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\left(\log\varphi\right)^{\prime}\varphi^{\prime}\varphi d\sigma=-\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\left(\varphi^{\prime}\right)^{2}d\sigma.

Inserting (
8.7
) and (
8.8
) into (
8.6
) and using the Wirtinger inequality, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\partial\Omega}\left(\frac{\cosh r-\tilde{u}}{\tilde{\kappa}}-\tilde{u}\right)d\mu=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}(\varphi^{\prime\prime})^{2}d\sigma-\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\left(\varphi^{\prime}\right)^{2}d\sigma\geq 0.

Hence we obtain(
8.4
).
Besides, we have that equality holds in (
8.4
) only if
\varphi(z)=\alpha\sin t+\beta\cos t+\gamma
for some constants
\alpha
,
\beta
and
\gamma
, where we set
z=(\cos t,\sin t)
and
t\in[0,2\pi]
. In this case, we know

\left(\varphi^{\prime}\right)^{2}+\left(\varphi-\gamma\right)^{2}=\alpha^{2}+\beta^{2}
and
\varphi^{\prime\prime}=-\varphi+\gamma
. Combining these facts with (
8.5
) yields

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\tilde{\kappa}}=

\displaystyle\varphi A[\varphi]=\varphi^{\prime\prime}\varphi-\frac{1}{2}\left(\varphi^{\prime}\right)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\varphi^{2}-1\right)=-\varphi^{2}+\gamma\varphi-\frac{1}{2}\left(\varphi^{\prime}\right)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\varphi^{2}-\frac{1}{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\left(\left(\varphi^{\prime}\right)^{2}+\left(\varphi-\gamma\right)^{2}-\gamma^{2}+1\right)=\frac{1}{2}\left(\gamma^{2}-\alpha^{2}-\beta^{2}-1\right).

Hence equality holds in (
8.4
) only if
\Omega
is a geodesic ball. Conversely, if
\Omega
is a geodesic ball, then
\tilde{\kappa}
is constant on
\partial\Omega
, and hence

\displaystyle\int_{\partial\Omega}\left(\frac{\cosh r-\tilde{u}}{\tilde{\kappa}}-\tilde{u}\right)d\mu=\frac{1}{\tilde{\kappa}}\int_{M}\left(\left(\cosh r-\tilde{u}\right)-\tilde{u}\tilde{\kappa}\right)d\mu=0,

where we used (
3.20
). Therefore, equality holds in (
8.4
) if and only if
\Omega
is a geodesic ball in
\mathbb{H}^{2}
. We complete the proof of Proposition
8.2
.
∎

Lemma 8.1
.

Let
M
be an
n
-dimensional
(n\geq 1)
hypersurface in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
and
\tilde{f}
be a smooth function defined on
M
. For
0\leq k\leq n-1
, we have

(8.9)

\int_{M}\left(\left(\cosh r-\tilde{u}\right)p_{k}(\tilde{\kappa})-\tilde{u}p_{k+1}(\tilde{\kappa})\right)\tilde{f}d\mu=-\frac{1}{k+1}\int_{M}\dot{p}_{k+1}^{ij}(\tilde{\kappa})\nabla_{i}\cosh r\nabla_{j}\tilde{f}d\mu.

For
1\leq k,\ l\leq n
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{M}\left(\cosh r-\tilde{u}\right)\tilde{f}(p_{k}(\tilde{\kappa})p_{l-1}(\tilde{\kappa})-p_{k-1}(\tilde{\kappa})p_{l}(\tilde{\kappa}))d\mu

(8.10)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{l}\int_{M}\dot{p}_{l}^{ij}(\tilde{\kappa})\nabla_{i}\cosh r\nabla_{j}\left(\tilde{f}p_{k}(\tilde{\kappa})\right)d\mu+\frac{1}{k}\int_{M}\dot{p}_{k}^{ij}(\tilde{\kappa})\nabla_{i}\cosh r\nabla_{j}\left(\tilde{f}p_{l}(\tilde{\kappa})\right)d\mu.

Proof.

In the case
0\leq k\leq n-1
, by using (
3.21
) and integration by parts, we have

\displaystyle\int_{M}\left(\left(\cosh r-\tilde{u}\right)p_{k}(\tilde{\kappa})-\tilde{u}p_{k+1}(\tilde{\kappa})\right)\tilde{f}d\mu

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{k+1}\int_{M}\left(\dot{p}^{ij}_{k+1}(\tilde{\kappa})\nabla_{j}\nabla_{i}\cosh r\right)\tilde{f}d\mu=-\frac{1}{k+1}\int_{M}\dot{p}^{ij}_{k+1}(\tilde{\kappa})\nabla_{i}\cosh r\nabla_{j}\tilde{f}d\mu,

where we also used the fact that
\dot{p}^{ij}_{k+1}(\tilde{\kappa})
is divergence-free.
Hence we obtain the integral formula (
8.9
).

Similarly, for
1\leq k,\ l\leq n
, we have

\displaystyle-\int_{M}\left(\left(\cosh r-\tilde{u}\right)p_{k-1}(\tilde{\kappa})-\tilde{u}p_{k}(\tilde{\kappa})\right)\tilde{f}p_{l}(\tilde{\kappa})d\mu

(8.11)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{k}\int_{M}\left(\dot{p}_{k}^{ij}(\tilde{\kappa})\nabla_{j}\nabla_{i}\cosh r\right)\tilde{f}p_{l}(\tilde{\kappa})d\mu=\frac{1}{k}\int_{M}\dot{p}^{ij}_{k}(\tilde{\kappa})\nabla_{i}\cosh r\nabla_{j}\left(\tilde{f}p_{l}(\tilde{\kappa})\right)d\mu,

and

\displaystyle\int_{M}\left(\left(\cosh r-\tilde{u}\right)p_{l-1}(\tilde{\kappa})-\tilde{u}p_{l}(\tilde{\kappa})\right)\tilde{f}p_{k}(\tilde{\kappa})d\mu

(8.12)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{l}\int_{M}\left(\dot{p}^{ij}_{l}(\tilde{\kappa})\nabla_{j}\nabla_{i}\cosh r\right)\tilde{f}p_{k}(\tilde{\kappa})d\mu=-\frac{1}{l}\int_{M}\dot{p}^{ij}_{l}(\tilde{\kappa})\nabla_{i}\cosh r\nabla_{j}\left(\tilde{f}p_{k}(\tilde{\kappa})\right)d\mu.

Then formula (
8.10
) follows from the sum of (
8.11
) and (
8.12
). We complete the proof of Lemma
8.1
.
∎

8.3.
Proofs of the results

Proof of Proposition
8.1
.

The following proof is divided into four cases.

Case 1.

n=1
,
k=0
, and
l=1
. From Proposition
8.2
and (
8.1
), we have

(8.13)

\displaystyle 0\leq\int_{M}\frac{\left(\cosh r-\tilde{u}\right)-\tilde{u}\tilde{\kappa}}{\tilde{\kappa}}d\mu=\int_{M}\frac{\left(\cosh r-\tilde{u}\right)-\tilde{u}\tilde{\kappa}}{\chi}d\mu,

with equality if and only if
M
is a geodesic circle.
Taking
\tilde{f}=\frac{1}{\chi}
in (
8.9
) and using (
3.40
), we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{M}\frac{\left(\cosh r-\tilde{u}\right)-\tilde{u}\tilde{\kappa}}{\chi}d\mu=\frac{1}{n}\int_{M}\frac{\chi^{\prime}}{\chi^{2}}g^{ij}\nabla_{i}\cosh r\nabla_{j}\left(\cosh r-\tilde{u}\right)

(8.14)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{n}\int_{M}\frac{\chi^{\prime}}{\chi^{2}}\tilde{h}^{ij}\langle V,\partial_{i}X\rangle\langle V,\partial_{j}X\rangle\leq 0.

Combining (
8.13
) with (
8.14
), we have
M
is a geodesic circle, and
\chi
is constant on
M
. If
\chi^{\prime}>0
on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
, then (
3.24
) and (
8.1
) imply that
\cosh r-\tilde{u}=\frac{1}{\varphi}
is constant, which means the horospherical support function of
M
is constant. Hence
M
is a geodesic circle centered at the origin.

By Corollary
3.2
, if
\Omega
contains the origin in its interior, then
M=\partial\Omega
is star-shaped and hence
\tilde{u}>0
on
M
. This assumption will be used in the following Case 2 and Case 3.

Case 2.

n\geq 2
,
k=0
, and
l=1
. By (
3.6
), (
3.20
) and the assumption
\tilde{u}>0
, we have

\int_{M}\left((\cosh r-\tilde{u})-\tilde{u}p_{1}(\tilde{\kappa})\right)p_{1}(\tilde{\kappa})d\mu\leq\int_{M}\left((\cosh r-\tilde{u})p_{1}(\tilde{\kappa})-\tilde{u}p_{2}(\tilde{\kappa})\right)d\mu=0.

Equality holds if and only if
M
is totally umbilical, which means
M
is a geodesic sphere, and then
\chi
is constant on
M
by (
8.1
).
Taking
\tilde{f}=p_{1}(\tilde{\kappa})=\chi
in (
8.9
) and using (
3.40
), we have

\displaystyle\int_{M}\left((\cosh r-\tilde{u})-\tilde{u}p_{1}(\tilde{\kappa})\right)p_{1}(\tilde{\kappa})d\mu=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{n}\int_{M}g^{ij}\nabla_{i}\cosh r\nabla_{j}\chi d\mu

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\int_{M}\chi^{\prime}\tilde{h}^{ij}\langle V,\partial_{i}X\rangle\langle V,\partial_{j}X\rangle d\mu\geq 0.

Hence we have that
M
is a geodesic sphere in Case 2.
Particularly, if
\chi^{\prime}>0
on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
, then
\cosh r-\tilde{u}=\frac{1}{\varphi}
is constant, and hence
M
is a geodesic sphere centered at the origin.

Case 3.

n\geq 2
,
k=0
, and
2\leq l\leq n
. Using (
3.6
), (
3.20
) and the assumption
\tilde{u}>0
, we have

(8.15)

\int_{M}\left(\left(\cosh r-\tilde{u}\right)-\tilde{u}p_{1}(\tilde{\kappa})\right)p_{l}^{\frac{l-1}{l}}(\tilde{\kappa})d\mu\leq\int_{M}\left(\cosh r-\tilde{u}\right)p_{l-1}(\tilde{\kappa})-\tilde{u}p_{l}(\tilde{\kappa})d\mu=0.

Equality holds if and only if
M
is a geodesic sphere.
Taking
\tilde{f}=p_{l}^{\frac{l-1}{l}}(\tilde{\kappa})=\chi^{\frac{l-1}{l}}
in (
8.9
) and using Lemma
3.11
, we have

\displaystyle-\int_{M}\left(\left(\cosh r-\tilde{u}\right)-\tilde{u}p_{1}(\tilde{\kappa})\right)\chi^{\frac{l-1}{l}}d\mu

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\int_{M}g^{ij}\nabla_{i}\cosh r\nabla_{j}\chi^{\frac{l-1}{l}}d\mu=\frac{l-1}{ln}\int_{M}\chi^{-\frac{1}{l}}\chi^{\prime}\nabla_{i}\cosh r\nabla_{j}\left(\cosh r-\tilde{u}\right)d\mu

(8.16)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{l-1}{ln}\int_{M}\chi^{-\frac{1}{l}}\chi^{\prime}\tilde{h}^{ij}\langle V,\partial_{i}X\rangle\langle V,\partial_{j}X\rangle d\mu\leq 0.

Combining (
8.15
) with (
8.16
), we have that
M
is a geodesic sphere. Similar to Case 1, if
\chi^{\prime}>0
, then
M
is a geodesic sphere centered at the origin.

Case 4.

n\geq 2
and
1\leq k<l\leq n
. Again, we have by use of (
3.5
)

p_{k}(\tilde{\kappa})p_{l-1}(\tilde{\kappa})-p_{k-1}(\tilde{\kappa})p_{l}(\tilde{\kappa})\geq 0,

with equality if and only if
\tilde{\kappa}_{1}=\tilde{\kappa}_{2}=\cdots=\tilde{\kappa}_{n}
.
Taking
\tilde{f}=p_{k}^{-1}(\tilde{\kappa})=p_{l}^{-1}(\tilde{\kappa})\chi
in (
8.10
) and using Lemma
3.11
, we have

\displaystyle 0\leq

\displaystyle\int_{M}\left(\cosh r-\tilde{u}\right)p_{k}^{-1}(\tilde{\kappa})\left((p_{k}(\tilde{\kappa})p_{l-1}(\tilde{\kappa})-p_{k-1}(\tilde{\kappa})p_{l}(\tilde{\kappa}))\right)d\mu

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{k}\int_{M}\dot{p}_{k}^{ij}(\tilde{\kappa})\nabla_{i}\cosh r\nabla_{j}\left(\frac{p_{l}(\tilde{\kappa})}{p_{k}(\tilde{\kappa})}\right)d\mu=\frac{1}{k}\int_{M}\dot{p}_{k}^{ij}(\tilde{\kappa})\nabla_{i}\cosh r\nabla_{j}\chi d\mu

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{k}\int_{M}\chi^{\prime}\dot{p}_{k}^{ij}(\tilde{\kappa})\tilde{h}_{j}{}^{s}\langle V,\partial_{i}X\rangle\langle V,\partial_{s}X\rangle d\mu\leq 0.

Thus,
M
is a geodesic sphere. In the case
\chi^{\prime}>0
, we have that
M
is centered at the origin.
We complete the proof of Proposition
8.1
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
8.1
.

Assume that
\varphi(z)
is a solution to equation (
8.2
) that satisfies the requirements in Theorem
8.1
. By Corollary
2.2
, there exists a smooth uniformly h-convex bounded domain
\Omega
with horospherical support function
\log\varphi(z)
.
By (
2.15
) and (
3.24
), we have

(8.17)

\frac{p_{n}(\tilde{\kappa})}{p_{k}(\tilde{\kappa})}=\frac{1}{\gamma}(\cosh r-\tilde{u})^{n+p}

on
\partial\Omega
.

Case 1.

p>-n
.
Taking
l=n
and
\chi(s)=\frac{1}{\gamma}s^{n+p}
in Proposition
8.1
, we have
that
\Omega
must be a geodesic ball centered at the origin. Then we can assume that
\varphi(z)=c>1
for all
z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
, and hence the radius of
\Omega
is
\log c
. By the definition of
A[\varphi]
in (
2.12
), we have that
c
is the solution to equation
(
8.3
). Hence we only need to count the number of solutions to equation (
8.3
).

Define a function
\zeta(t)
on
(1,+\infty)
by

\zeta(t)=t^{-p-k}\left(\frac{1}{2}(t-t^{-1})\right)^{n-k}.

Then

\displaystyle\zeta^{\prime}(t)=

\displaystyle-(p+k)t^{-p-k-1}\cdot 2^{k-n}(t-t^{-1})^{n-k}+t^{-p-k}\cdot(n-k)2^{k-n}(t-t^{-1})^{n-k-1}(1+t^{-2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2^{k-n}t^{-p-k-2}(t-t^{-1})\left(-(2k+p-n)t^{2}+n+p\right).

If
p>n-2k
, then

\zeta(t)
attains its maximum at
t_{0}=\left(\frac{n+p}{2k+p-n}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}>1
, and
\zeta(t_{0})=\gamma_{0}
.
Furthermore, it is easy to see that
\zeta(1)=\zeta(+\infty)=0
provided that
p>n-2k
. Therefore, when
p>n-2k
, if
\gamma>\zeta(t_{0})
, then (
8.3
) has no solution; if
\gamma=\zeta(t_{0})
, then
c=t_{0}
is the unique solution to (
8.3
); if
0<\gamma<\zeta(t_{0})
, then (
8.3
) has exactly two different solutions. Thus the Cases (1)–(3) of Theorem
8.1
are proved.

If
p=n-2k
, then
\zeta(t)
is strictly increasing on
(1,+\infty)
. Furthermore, it is easy to see that
\zeta(1)=0
and
\zeta(+\infty)=2^{k-n}
. Then Case (4) and Case (5) of Theorem
8.1
follows directly from the above argument.

If
-n<p<n-2k
, then
\zeta(t)
is strictly increasing on
(1,+\infty)
and
\zeta(+\infty)=+\infty
. Then Case (6) of Theorem
8.1
follows in the same manner as above.

Case 2.

p=-n
. By equation (
8.17
) and Proposition
8.1
, we can assume that
\Omega=B(X,r)
, which is the geodesic ball of radius
r
centered at
X
in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. It is direct to see that the shifted principal radii of curvature are all equal to
\sinh re^{r}
on
\partial\Omega
. Hence

\sinh re^{r}=\gamma^{\frac{1}{n-k}}.

Consequently, we have
e^{r}=\left(1+2\gamma^{\frac{1}{n-k}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}
. This together with Lemma
4.4
implies the Case (7) of Theorem
8.1
.

Then we complete the proof of Theorem
8.1
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
8.2
.

With the assumptions of
n
,
k
and
p
in Theorem
8.2
, the long time existence of the flow (
7.7
) was proved in Theorem
7.4
. The subsequential convergence of the flow (
7.7
) follows from the proofs of Theorem
7.2
and Theorem
7.3
.
By (
7.118
), each limiting hypersurface
M
satisfies
\varphi^{-p-k}p_{n-k}(A[\varphi])=\gamma f(z)
, which is equivalent to

\frac{p_{n}(\tilde{\kappa})}{p_{k}(\tilde{\kappa})}=\frac{1}{\gamma f(z)}\left(\cosh r-\tilde{u}\right)^{p+n}.

Since
f(z)
is constant and
p\geq-n
, we obtain that
M
must be a geodesic sphere
\partial B(r)
by Theorem
8.1
. Besides, formula (
7.13
) shows that
\widetilde{W}_{k}(B(r))=\widetilde{W}_{k}(\Omega_{0})
, which implies the uniqueness of
M
. Hence we get the smooth convergence of the flow (
7.7
).
∎

9.
Kazdan-Warner type obstructions for Horospherical Minkowski problem and Horospherical Christoffel-Minkowski problem

Letting
p=-n
, equation (
7.1
) becomes

(9.1)

\varphi^{n-k}p_{n-k}\left(A[\varphi]\right)=f(z).

By (
2.15
), if
A[\varphi(z)]>0
on
\mathbb{S}^{n}
, then equation (
9.1
) is equivalent to

(9.2)

p_{n-k}\left(\tilde{\lambda}\right)=f(z),

where
\tilde{\lambda}
are the shifted principal radii of curvature.
We call Problem
5.2
the
horospherical Christoffel problem
in the case
p=-n
and
k=n-1
.
By taking
k=n-1
in both equation (
9.1
) and equation (
9.2
), we have that the equation for the horospherical Christoffel problem is

(9.3)

\displaystyle\varphi p_{1}(A[\varphi])=

\displaystyle f(z),

that is

(9.4)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{\kappa_{i}-1}=

\displaystyle f(z).

Similarly, we call Problem
5.1
the
horospherical Minkowski problem
in the case
p=-n
and
k=0
.
By taking
k=0
in both equation (
9.1
) and equation (
9.2
), the equation for the horospherical Minkowski problem is

(9.5)

\varphi^{n}p_{n}\left(A[\varphi]\right)=f(z),

that is

(9.6)

\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(\kappa_{i}-1\right)=f^{-1}(z).

We call Problem
5.2
the
horospherical Christoffel-Minkowski problem
in the case
p=-n
and
1\leq k\leq n-1
.
Then (
9.1
) (or (
9.2
)) is the equation for this problem.

In the following theorem we prove a Kazdan-Warner type obstruction for the horospherical Minkowski problem and the horospherical Christoffel-Minkowski problem.

Theorem 9.1
.

Let
n\geq 2
and
0\leq k\leq n-1
be integers. Assume that
\varphi(z)
is a smooth uniformly h-convex solution to (
9.1
). Then

(9.7)

\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}\varphi^{-n}\langle Df(z),Dx_{i}\rangle d\sigma=0,\quad i=0,1,\ldots,n,

where
x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
are the coordinate functions of
\mathbb{S}^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. More generally,
f(z)
satisfies

(9.8)

\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}\varphi^{-n}\overline{V}(f(z))d\sigma=0

for any conformal vector field
\overline{V}
on the unit sphere
\mathbb{S}^{n}
.

Remark 9.1
.

•

If
f(z)
is monotone with respect to a coordinate
x_{i}
, then (
9.7
) shows that there is no smooth uniformly h-convex solution to equation (
9.1
).

•

If
f(z)
and
\varphi(z)
are even functions on
\mathbb{S}^{n}
, then they satisfy condition (
9.7
). Hence, there is no contradiction between the existence result in Theorem
7.2
and the necessary condition in Theorem
9.1
.

The remainder of Section
9
is organized as follows.

(1)

In Subsection
9.1
, we will give the proof of Theorem
9.1
. Let
\widetilde{g}=\varphi(z)^{-2}g_{0}
be a conformal metric on
\mathbb{S}^{n}
and
M
be a smooth uniformly h-convex hypersurface in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
with horospherical support function
u(z)=\log\varphi(z)
.
Motivated by the results in
[
26
]
, we show the relationship between the Schouten tensor of metric
\widetilde{g}
and the shifted principal radii of curvature on
M
. We remark that another proof of this relationship will be given in Section
13
.
The
\sigma_{k}
-Nirenberg problem studies prescribed
k
-th elementary symmetric polynomial of the eigenvalues of the Schouten tensor.
Then we prove Theorem
9.1
by using several Kazdan-Warner type obstructions proved in
[
44
,
56
,
75
]
for the
\sigma_{k}
-Nirenberg problem.

(2)

In Subsection
9.2
, we will give a direct proof of (
9.7
). The proof is self-contained and is also valid for the case
n=1
. In Lemma
9.3
, we will prove a sequence of identities for smooth closed hypersurfaces in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. Then we prove (
9.7
) by integration by parts.

9.1.
Proof of Theorem
9.1

Given a Riemannian manifold
(M^{n},g)
with
n\geq 3
, the Schouten tensor of metric
g
is defined by

(9.9)

S_{g}:=\frac{1}{n-2}\left(\mathrm{Ric}_{g}-\frac{R_{g}}{2(n-1)}g\right),

where
\mathrm{Ric}_{g}
is the Ricci curvature tensor and
R_{g}
is the scalar curvature of metric
g
.

Lemma 9.1
.

Let
M
be a smooth closed uniformly h-convex hypersurface in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}

(n\geq 1)
.
Let
Y(z)=(X-\nu)(z)
,
\widetilde{M}=Y(\mathbb{S}^{n})
. Fix a local normal coordinate system around
z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
such that
A_{ij}[\varphi(z)]
is diagonal at
z
. Let
II(U,V):=(\widetilde{\nabla}_{U}V)^{\perp}
denote the projection of
\widetilde{\nabla}_{U}V
on the normal bundle of
\widetilde{M}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1,1}
.
Then

(9.10)

II(\partial_{i}Y,\partial_{j}Y)=\frac{1}{\varphi^{2}}(-\tilde{\lambda}_{i}X+(1+\tilde{\lambda}_{i})\nu)\delta_{ij}.

Proof.

Since

\langle X,X\rangle=-1,\langle X,\nu\rangle=0,\langle\nu,\nu\rangle=1,

we have

\displaystyle\langle\partial_{i}Y,X\rangle=

\displaystyle\langle\partial_{i}(X-\nu),X\rangle=\langle\nu,\partial_{i}X\rangle=0,

\displaystyle\langle\partial_{i}Y,\nu\rangle=

\displaystyle\langle\partial_{i}(X-\nu),\nu\rangle=\langle\partial_{i}X,\nu\rangle=0.

Then we know that vectors
X
and
\nu
span the normal bundle of
\widetilde{M}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1,1}
.
From the definition of
II(\partial_{i}Y,\partial_{j}Y)
and (
2.8
), we have

\displaystyle II(\partial_{i}Y,\partial_{j}Y)=

\displaystyle-\langle\partial_{j}\partial_{i}(X-\nu),X\rangle X+\langle\partial_{j}\partial_{i}(X-\nu),\nu\rangle\nu

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\partial_{i}(X-\nu),\partial_{j}X\rangle X-\langle\partial_{i}(X-\nu),\partial_{j}\nu\rangle\nu

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varphi}\langle(e_{i},0),\partial_{j}X\rangle X+\langle\partial_{i}(X-\nu),\partial_{j}(X-\nu)-\partial_{j}X\rangle\nu

(9.11)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\varphi}A_{ij}[\varphi]X+\left(\frac{1}{\varphi^{2}}\delta_{ij}+\frac{1}{\varphi}A_{ij}[\varphi]\right)\nu,

where we used (
2.11
) and (
3.44
) in the last equality. Since
A_{ij}[\varphi]
is diagonal at
z
, by using (
2.15
) and (
2.17
), we have

(9.12)

A_{ij}[\varphi]=\frac{1}{\varphi}\tilde{\lambda}_{i}\delta_{ij}.

Therefore, we obtain Lemma
9.1
by substituting (
9.12
) into (
9.11
).
∎

Lemma 9.2
.

The induced Riemannian metric on
\widetilde{M}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1,1}
is
\tilde{g}=\frac{1}{\varphi^{2}}g_{0}
, where
g_{0}
is the canonical metric on the unit sphere
\mathbb{S}^{n}
. Then we have the eigenvalues of
\tilde{g}^{-1}\circ\widetilde{S}
are
\{\tilde{\lambda}_{i}+\frac{1}{2}\}
,
i=1,\ldots,n
, where
\widetilde{S}
is the Schouten tensor of
\tilde{g}
and
\tilde{\lambda}_{i}
are the shifted principal radii of curvature of
M
at
X(z)
.

Proof.

By (
3.44
), we have
\langle\partial_{i}Y,\partial_{j}Y\rangle=\frac{1}{\varphi^{2}}\delta_{ij}
, which means
\tilde{g}=\frac{1}{\varphi^{2}}g_{0}
is the induced metric on
\widetilde{M}
.
Now we set a local orthonormal frame
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\}
for
\widetilde{M}
such that
v_{i}:=\varphi\partial_{i}(X-\nu)
,
i=1,\ldots,n
. By the Gauss equation of
\widetilde{M}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1,1}
and (
9.10
), we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{{\rm Riem}}(v_{i},v_{j},v_{k},v_{l})=

\displaystyle\langle II(v_{i},v_{k}),II(v_{j},v_{l})\rangle-\langle II(v_{i},v_{l}),II(v_{j},v_{k})\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\delta_{ik}\delta_{jl}-\delta_{il}\delta_{jk})\langle-\tilde{\lambda}_{i}X+(1+\tilde{\lambda}_{i})\nu,-\tilde{\lambda}_{j}X+(1+\tilde{\lambda}_{j})\nu\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\delta_{ik}\delta_{jl}-\delta_{il}\delta_{jk})(1+\tilde{\lambda}_{i}+\tilde{\lambda}_{j}),

where
\widetilde{{\rm Riem}}
is the Riemannian curvature tensor of
\widetilde{M}
.
Then the Ricci curvature of metric
\tilde{g}
is

(9.13)

\widetilde{\mathrm{Ric}}(v_{i},v_{k})=\left((n-1)+(n-2)\tilde{\lambda}_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\tilde{\lambda}_{j}\right)\delta_{ik},

and the scalar curvature of
\tilde{g}
is

(9.14)

\widetilde{R}=n(n-1)+2(n-1)\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{\lambda}_{i}.

Therefore the matrix
\left(\widetilde{S}(v_{i},v_{j})\right)
is diagonal. Substituting (
9.13
) and (
9.14
) into the definition of
\widetilde{S}
in (
9.9
), we know

\displaystyle\widetilde{S}(v_{i},v_{i})=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n-2}\left(\widetilde{\mathrm{Ric}}(v_{i},v_{i})-\frac{\widetilde{R}}{2(n-1)}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n-2}\left(n-1+(n-2)\tilde{\lambda}_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\tilde{\lambda}_{j}-\frac{n(n-1)+2(n-1)\sum_{j=1}^{n}\tilde{\lambda}_{j}}{2(n-1)}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tilde{\lambda}_{i}+\frac{1}{2}.

This completes the proof of Lemma
9.2
.
∎

Using Lemma
9.2
and (
9.14
), we can get the following Corollary
9.1
.

Corollary 9.1
.

When
n\geq 3
,
0\leq k\leq n-1
, we have

(9.15)

\displaystyle p_{n-k}(\tilde{\lambda})=p_{n-k}\left(\tilde{g}^{-1}\circ\widetilde{S}-\frac{1}{2}I\right)=\sum_{i=0}^{n-k}(-1)^{n-k-i}\frac{C_{n-k}^{i}}{2^{n-k-i}}p_{i}\left(\tilde{g}^{-1}\circ\widetilde{S}\right).

When
n=2
, we have

(9.16)

\displaystyle p_{1}(\tilde{\lambda})=\frac{1}{2}(\tilde{\lambda}_{1}+\tilde{\lambda}_{2})=\frac{1}{4}\widetilde{R}-\frac{1}{2}.

In
[
44
,
75
]
, Han, Viaclovsky proved the following Theorem A.

Theorem A

(
[
44
,
75
]
)
.

Let
(M^{n},g)
be an
n
-dimensional
(n\geq 3)
compact Riemannian manifold,
\sigma_{k}(g^{-1}\circ S_{g})
be the
k
-th
(1\leq k\leq n)
elementary symmetric function of the eigenvalues of the Schouten tensor with respect to metric
g
. Let
\overline{V}
be a conformal Killing vector field on
(M^{n},g)
. When
k\geq 3
, also assume that
(M^{n},g)
is locally conformally flat. Then

\int_{M}\langle\overline{V},\nabla\sigma_{k}(g^{-1}\circ S_{g})\rangle d\mathrm{vol}_{g}=0.

Remark 9.2
.

When
n\geq 3
and
k=1
, Theorem A degenerates to the classical Kazdan-Warner identity, i.e.

\int_{M}\langle\overline{V},\nabla R_{g}\rangle d\mathrm{vol}_{g}=0.

Recently, Li, Lu, and Lu
[
56
]
considered the
\sigma_{2}
-Nirenberg problem and obtained the following Kazdan-Warner type identity on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
. Here, we state their result by using the notations in this paper.

Theorem B

(
[
56
]
)
.

Let
\overline{V}
be a conformal Killing vector field on
(\mathbb{S}^{2},g_{0})
, and let
\tilde{g}=\frac{1}{\varphi^{2}}g_{0}
be a conformal metric to
g_{0}
on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
, where
\varphi(z)
is a smooth, positive function on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
. Then

\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\varphi^{-2}\overline{V}\left(\sigma_{2}\left(\varphi A[\varphi]+\frac{1}{2}I\right)\right)d\sigma=0.

By using (
2.15
), we remark that Theorem B is equivalent to

(9.17)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\varphi^{-2}\overline{V}\left(\sigma_{2}\left(\tilde{\lambda}+\frac{1}{2}\right)\right)d\sigma=0.

Now we can give the proof of Theorem
9.1
.

Proof of Theorem
9.1
.

Assume that
\tilde{g}=\frac{1}{\varphi^{2}(z)}g_{0}
, where
g_{0}
is the canonical metric on the unit sphere
\mathbb{S}^{n}
.
Then we have
d\mathrm{vol}_{\tilde{g}}=\varphi^{-n}d\sigma
, where
d\sigma
is the volume element of
\mathbb{S}^{n}
.
Taking
p=-n
in (
7.2
), we have

(9.18)

f(z)=p_{n-k}(\tilde{\lambda}).

The proof of (
9.8
) is divided into three cases.

Case 1.

n\geq 3
and
0\leq k\leq n-1
.
Using (
9.15
) in (
9.18
), we have by Theorem A

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}\varphi^{-n}\overline{V}(f(z))d\sigma=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}\varphi^{-n}\overline{V}(p_{n-k}(\tilde{\lambda}))d\sigma

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{n-k}(-1)^{n-k-i}\frac{C_{n-k}^{i}}{2^{n-k-i}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}\varphi^{-n}\overline{V}\left(p_{i}(\tilde{g}^{-1}\circ\widetilde{S})\right)d\sigma=0

for all
0\leq k\leq n
, where
\overline{V}
is any conformal Killing vector field on
\mathbb{S}^{n}
.

Case 2.

n=2
and
k=1
.
Using (
9.16
) in (
9.18
), we have by the Kazdan-Warner identity

\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\varphi^{-2}\overline{V}(f(z))d\sigma=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\varphi^{-2}\overline{V}\left(p_{1}(\tilde{\lambda})\right)d\sigma=\frac{1}{4}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\varphi^{-2}\overline{V}(\widetilde{R})d\sigma=0.

Case 3.

n=2
and
k=0
.
By using (
9.18
), (
9.17
) and the Kazdan-Warner identity, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\varphi^{-2}\overline{V}(f(z))d\sigma=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\varphi^{-2}\overline{V}\left(p_{2}(\tilde{\lambda})\right)d\sigma

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\varphi^{-2}\overline{V}\left(p_{2}\left(\tilde{\lambda}+\frac{1}{2}\right)-p_{1}(\tilde{\lambda})-\frac{1}{4}\right)d\sigma

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\varphi^{-2}\left(\overline{V}\left(p_{2}\left(\tilde{\lambda}+\frac{1}{2}\right)\right)-\frac{1}{4}\overline{V}(\widetilde{R})\right)d\sigma=0.

Thus we obtain (
9.8
).

In particular, we can choose the conformal Killing vector field
\overline{V}
in (
9.8
) as
Dx_{i}
,
0\leq i\leq n
, where
x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
are the coordinate functions of
\mathbb{S}^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. Therefore we obtain (
9.7
). This completes the proof of Theorem
9.1
.
∎

9.2.
Direct proof of (
9.7
)

In Theorem
9.1
, we gave Kazdan-Warner type obstructions to existence of solutions to equation (
9.1
). The proof in Subsection
9.1
is intrinsic, and it is based on several theorems in
[
44
,
56
,
75
]
. In this subsection, we will give a geometric proof of the necessary condition (
9.7
). The following Lemma
9.3
is crucial.

Lemma 9.3
.

Let
n\geq 1
and
M=\partial\Omega
be a smooth closed hypersurface in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1,1}
. Then

(9.19)

\displaystyle\int_{M}(\cosh r\nu-\tilde{u}X)p_{k}(\kappa)d\mu=0,\quad\forall\ 0\leq k\leq n.

Proof.

Let
\vec{a}
be any fixed constant vector in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1,1}
. We divide the proof of Lemma
9.3
into two cases.

Case 1.

n\geq 1
and
1\leq k\leq n
. By using (
3.36
), we have

(9.20)

\displaystyle\nabla_{j}\nabla_{i}\langle X,\vec{a}\rangle=\nabla_{j}\langle\partial_{i}X,\vec{a}\rangle=\langle-{h}_{ij}\nu+g_{ij}X,\vec{a}\rangle=-{h}_{ij}\langle\nu,\vec{a}\rangle+g_{ij}\langle X,\vec{a}\rangle.

By (
9.20
) and (
3.14
), we have

\displaystyle\int_{M}\cosh r\dot{p}_{k}^{ij}(\kappa)\nabla_{j}\nabla_{i}\langle X,\vec{a}\rangle d\mu=

\displaystyle\int_{M}\cosh r\dot{p}_{k}^{ij}(\kappa)\left(-{h}_{ij}\langle\nu,\vec{a}\rangle+g_{ij}\langle X,\vec{a}\rangle\right)d\mu

(9.21)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{M}\left(-k\cosh r\langle\nu,\vec{a}\rangle p_{k}(\kappa)+\cosh r\langle X,\vec{a}\rangle\dot{p}_{k}^{ij}(\kappa)g_{ij}\right)d\mu.

Since
\dot{p}_{k}^{ij}
is divergence-free, by using integration by parts, (
3.39
) and (
3.17
), we also have

\displaystyle\int_{M}\cosh r\dot{p}_{k}^{ij}(\kappa)\nabla_{j}\nabla_{i}\langle X,\vec{a}\rangle d\mu=

\displaystyle\int_{M}\langle X,\vec{a}\rangle\dot{p}_{k}^{ij}(\kappa)\nabla_{j}\nabla_{i}\cosh rd\mu

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{M}\langle X,\vec{a}\rangle\dot{p}_{k}^{ij}(\kappa)\nabla_{j}\langle V,\partial_{i}X\rangle d\mu

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{M}\langle X,\vec{a}\rangle\dot{p}_{k}^{ij}(\kappa)\left(-h_{ij}\tilde{u}+\cosh rg_{ij}\right)d\mu

(9.22)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{M}\left(-k\tilde{u}\langle X,\vec{a}\rangle p_{k}(\kappa)+\cosh r\langle X,\vec{a}\rangle\dot{p}_{k}^{ij}(\kappa)g_{ij}\right)d\mu.

Comparing (
9.21
) with (
9.22
), we have

(9.23)

\displaystyle\int_{M}\langle\cosh r\nu-\tilde{u}X,\vec{a}\rangle p_{k}(\kappa)d\mu=0,

for all
1\leq k\leq n
and
\vec{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1,1}
.

Case 2.

n\geq 1
and
k=0
. By using (
3.17
) and (
3.18
), it is easy to get

(9.24)

\displaystyle\mathrm{div}_{\mathbb{H}^{n+1}}(\cosh r\vec{a})=

\displaystyle\langle V,\vec{a}\rangle,\quad\mathrm{div}_{\mathbb{H}^{n+1}}\left(\cosh r\langle X,\vec{a}\rangle X\right)=n\cosh r\langle X,\vec{a}\rangle,

\displaystyle\mathrm{div}_{\mathbb{H}^{n+1}}\left(\langle X,\vec{a}\rangle V\right)=

\displaystyle n\cosh r\langle X,\vec{a}\rangle+\langle V,\vec{a}\rangle.

Since
X
is the normal vector field of the inclusion
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1,1}
, we know that the projection of
\vec{a}
on the tangent bundle
T\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
is
\vec{a}+\langle X,\vec{a}\rangle X
, which implies that
\cosh r\vec{a}+\cosh r\langle X,\vec{a}\rangle X
is a tangential vector field on
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
.
Hence, by using
\tilde{u}:=\langle V,\nu\rangle
,
\langle X,\nu\rangle=0
, (
9.24
) and the divergence theorem, we have

\displaystyle\int_{M}\langle\cosh r\nu-\tilde{u}X,\vec{a}\rangle d\mu=

\displaystyle\int_{M}\langle\cosh r\vec{a}-\langle X,\vec{a}\rangle V,\nu\rangle d\mu

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{M}\langle\cosh r\vec{a}+\cosh r\langle X,\vec{a}\rangle X-\langle X,\vec{a}\rangle V,\nu\rangle d\mu

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\mathrm{div}_{\mathbb{H}^{n+1}}(\cosh r\vec{a})+\mathrm{div}_{\mathbb{H}^{n+1}}\left(\cosh r\langle X,\vec{a}\rangle X\right)-\mathrm{div}_{\mathbb{H}^{n+1}}\left(\langle X,\vec{a}\rangle V\right)dv

(9.25)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\left(\langle V,\vec{a}\rangle+n\cosh r\langle X,\vec{a}\rangle-\left(n\cosh r\langle X,\vec{a}\rangle+\langle V,\vec{a}\rangle\right)\right)dv=0.

Since the vector
\vec{a}
is arbitrary, Lemma
9.3
follows from (
9.23
) and (
9.25
).
∎

Corollary 9.2
.

Let
M
be a smooth uniformly h-convex hypersurface in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}

(n\geq 1)
and
u(z)
be the horospherical support function of
M
. We denote
\varphi(z)=e^{u(z)}
. Then

(9.26)

\displaystyle\int_{M}(\cosh r\nu-\tilde{u}X)p_{k}(\tilde{\kappa})d\mu=0,\quad\forall\ 0\leq k\leq n,

and

(9.27)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}\left(\frac{D\varphi}{\varphi}(z)+z\right)\varphi^{-k}(z)p_{n-k}(A[\varphi(z)]))d\sigma=0,\quad\forall\ 0\leq k\leq n.

Proof.

By choosing suitable linear combinations, it is easy to see that (
9.26
) follows from (
9.19
). Formula (
3.27
) states

(9.28)

\displaystyle\cosh r\nu-\tilde{u}X=\left(-\frac{D\varphi}{\varphi}-z,0\right).

Formulas (
2.16
) and (
2.15
) imply

(9.29)

\displaystyle p_{k}(\tilde{\kappa})d\mu=\frac{p_{n-k}}{p_{n}}(\tilde{\lambda})\varphi^{-n}p_{n}(\tilde{\lambda})d\sigma=\varphi^{-n}p_{n-k}(\tilde{\lambda})d\sigma=\varphi^{-k}p_{n-k}(A[\varphi])d\sigma.

Then we get (
9.27
) by substituting (
9.28
) and (
9.29
) into (
9.26
).
∎

Now we can give a direct proof of the obstruction (
9.7
).

Direct proof of (
9.7
).

Let
\vec{a}
be any constant vector in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. We denote its projection on the tangent bundle of
\mathbb{S}^{n}
as
\vec{a}^{T}=\vec{a}-\langle z,\vec{a}\rangle z
. Recall the equation (
9.1
),

\displaystyle\varphi^{n-k}(z)p_{n-k}(A[\varphi(z)])=f(z).

By integration by parts, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}\varphi^{-n}\langle Df,\vec{a}\rangle d\sigma=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}\varphi^{-n}\langle D\left(\varphi^{n-k}p_{n-k}(A[\varphi])\right),\vec{a}\rangle d\sigma

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}\varphi^{-n}\langle D\left(\varphi^{n-k}\right),\vec{a}\rangle p_{n-k}(A[\varphi])d\sigma+\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}\varphi^{-k}\langle Dp_{n-k}(A[\varphi]),\vec{a}\rangle d\sigma

(9.30)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}\left((n-k)\varphi^{-k-1}\langle D\varphi,\vec{a}\rangle-\mathrm{div}_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}(\varphi^{-k}\vec{a}^{T})\right)p_{n-k}(A[\varphi])d\sigma.

A direct calculation yields

\displaystyle\mathrm{div}_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}(\varphi^{-k}\vec{a}^{T})=

\displaystyle\langle D\left(\varphi^{-k}\right),\vec{a}\rangle+\varphi^{-k}\mathrm{div}_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}(\vec{a}-\langle z,\vec{a}\rangle z)

(9.31)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-k\varphi^{-k-1}\langle D\varphi,\vec{a}\rangle-n\varphi^{-k}\langle z,\vec{a}\rangle.

Inserting (
9.31
) into the right-hand side of (
9.30
) and using (
9.27
), we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}\varphi^{-n}\langle Df,\vec{a}\rangle d\sigma=n\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}\langle\frac{D\varphi}{\varphi}+z,\vec{a}\rangle\varphi^{-k}p_{n-k}(A[\varphi])d\sigma=0.

Since
\vec{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
is arbitrary, we complete the proof of (
9.7
).
∎

Remark 9.3
.

From the above proof, we know that (
9.7
) holds for all
n\geq 1
in Theorem
9.1
.

10.
Horospherical
p
-Brunn-Minkowski inequality and Horospherical
p
-Minkowski inequalities

In this section, we propose a conjecture of horospherical
p
-Brunn-Minkowski inequalities in Conjecture
10.3
and two conjectures of horospherical
p
-Minkowski inequalities in Conjecture
10.5
and Conjecture
10.6
. We unify some well-known geometric inequalities from a horospherically convex geometric point of view. Furthermore, we prove some new inequalities which are special cases of these conjectures.

10.1.
An introduction of horospherical
p
-Brunn-Minkowski inequality and horospherical
p
-Minkowski inequalities

Let
\omega_{n}
denote the volume of the unit n-sphere
\mathbb{S}^{n}
and
B(r)
denote a geodesic ball of radius
r>0
centered at the origin in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
.
At first, we calculate the
k
-th modified quermassintegrals of
B(r)
, i.e.
\widetilde{W}_{k}(B(r))
. Since the shifted principal curvatures
\{\tilde{\kappa}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{\kappa}_{n}\}
on
\partial B(r)
are all equal to
\frac{e^{-r}}{\sinh r}
and the area of
\partial B(r)
is
\omega_{n}\sinh^{n}r
. By taking
\mathscr{F}\equiv 1
in Lemma
5.1
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dr}\widetilde{W}_{k}(B(r))=\omega_{n}\left(\frac{e^{-r}}{\sinh r}\right)^{k}\sinh^{n}r=\omega_{n}\sinh^{n-k}re^{-kr}.

Hence we can define a monotonically increasing function
I_{k}:[0,+\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
by

(10.1)

\displaystyle I_{k}(r):=\widetilde{W}_{k}\left(B(r)\right)=\omega_{n}\int_{0}^{r}\sinh^{n-k}te^{-kt}dt.

Particularly, we have

(10.2)

\displaystyle I_{n}(r)=\frac{\omega_{n}}{n}(1-e^{-nr}).

Definition 10.1
.

The modified
k
-mean radius of a domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
is defined by

(10.3)

r^{k}_{\Omega}:=I_{k}^{-1}(\widetilde{W}_{k}(\Omega)).

Based on the observation of hyperbolic
p
-sum of geodesic balls in Theorem
4.5
, we can prove the following geometric inequality.

Theorem 10.2
.

Let
n\geq 1
and
0\leq k\leq n
be integers, and let
\frac{1}{2}\leq p\leq 2
be a real number. Assume that
a
and
b
are positive numbers such that
a+b\geq 1
.
Let
K=B(X,r_{1})
and
L=B(Y,r_{2})
be two geodesic balls in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
, and let
\Omega=a\cdot K+_{p}b\cdot L
. Then

(10.4)

\exp\left(pI_{k}^{-1}(\widetilde{W}_{k}(\Omega))\right)\geq a\exp\left(pI_{k}^{-1}(\widetilde{W}_{k}(K))\right)+b\exp\left(pI_{k}^{-1}(\widetilde{W}_{k}(L))\right),\quad k=0,1,\ldots,n.

Equality holds if and only if
X=Y
, i.e.
K
and
L
are centered at the same point.

Proof.

By the definition of the increasing function
I_{k}
in (
10.1
), we have

(10.5)

\exp\left(pI_{k}^{-1}(\widetilde{W}_{k}(K))\right)=e^{pr_{1}},\quad\exp\left(pI_{k}^{-1}(\widetilde{W}_{k}(L))\right)=e^{pr_{2}}.

Theorem
4.5
asserts that we can find a geodesic ball
\widetilde{B}
of radius
\frac{1}{p}\log\left(ae^{pr_{1}}+be^{pr_{2}}\right)
lying inside
\Omega
, and
\widetilde{B}\subset\neq\Omega
unless
X=Y
. Combining this with Proposition
5.1
, we have

(10.6)

\exp\left(pI_{k}^{-1}(\widetilde{W}_{k}(\Omega))\right)\geq\exp\left(pI_{k}^{-1}(\widetilde{W}_{k}(\widetilde{B}))\right)=ae^{pr_{1}}+be^{pr_{2}},

with equality if and only if
X=Y
. Then Theorem
10.2
follows by substituting (
10.5
) and (
10.6
) into (
10.4
).
∎

Question arises whether inequality (
10.4
) still holds by replacing
K
and
L
by general h-convex domains.
For this purpose, we propose the following Conjecture
10.3
, which is named as the horospherical
p
-Brunn-Minkowski inequalities for modified quermassintegrals. Recall the definition of hyperbolic dilates in Definition
6.2
.

Conjecture 10.3

(Horospherical
p
-Brunn-Minkowski inequalities)
.

Let
n\geq 1
and
0\leq k\leq n
be integers, and let
\frac{1}{2}\leq p\leq 2
be a real number. Assume that
a\geq 0
and
b\geq 0
are real numbers such that
a+b\geq 1
.
Let
K
and
L
be two smooth uniformly h-convex bounded domains in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
, and let
\Omega=a\cdot K+_{p}b\cdot L
. Then

(10.7)

\exp\left(pI_{k}^{-1}(\widetilde{W}_{k}(\Omega))\right)\geq a\exp\left(pI_{k}^{-1}(\widetilde{W}_{k}(K))\right)+b\exp\left(pI_{k}^{-1}(\widetilde{W}_{k}(L))\right),\quad k=0,1,\ldots,n.

(1)

When
0\leq k\leq n-1
, equality holds in (
10.7
) if and only if one of the following conditions holds,

(a)

a=1
and
b=0
,

(b)

a=0
and
b=1
,

(c)

K=L
and
a+b=1
,

(d)

K
and
L
are geodesic balls centered at the same point.

(2)

When
k=n
, equality holds in (
10.7
) if and only if one of the following conditions holds,

(a)

a\geq 1
and
b=0
,

(b)

a=0
and
b\geq 1
,

(c)

K
and
L
are hyperbolic dilates.

When
k=n
, we will prove Conjecture
10.3
by showing that it is equivalent to a Minkowski inequality for functions on the unit sphere
\mathbb{S}^{n}
.

Lemma 10.1
.

For a smooth uniformly h-convex bounded domain
\Omega
in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}

(n\geq 1)
, we have

(10.8)

\displaystyle\int_{\partial\Omega}p_{n}(\tilde{\kappa})d\mu=

\displaystyle\omega_{n}-n\widetilde{W}_{n}(\Omega),

(10.9)

\displaystyle\widetilde{W}_{n}(\Omega)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}(1-\varphi^{-n}_{\Omega})d\sigma.

Consequently, it holds that

(10.10)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}\varphi_{\Omega}^{-n}d\sigma=\omega_{n}e^{-nr^{n}_{\Omega}},

where
r^{n}_{\Omega}:=I_{n}^{-1}\left(\widetilde{W}_{n}(\Omega)\right)
.

Proof.

Along a general flow
\partial_{t}X=\mathscr{F}\nu
, by Lemma
5.1
and (
5.3
), we have

\frac{d}{dt}\widetilde{W}_{n}(\Omega_{t})=\int_{\Omega_{t}}p_{n}(\tilde{\kappa})\mathscr{F}d\mu_{t},\quad\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\varphi(z,t)=\varphi(z,t)\mathscr{F}.

Using (
2.16
) and (
2.15
), we have

(10.11)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\widetilde{W}_{n}(\Omega_{t})=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}p_{n}(\tilde{\kappa})\mathscr{F}\varphi^{-n}p_{n}(\tilde{\lambda})d\sigma=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}\varphi^{-n}\mathscr{F}d\sigma=\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}\left(-\frac{1}{n}\varphi^{-n}(z,t)\right)d\sigma.

Let
N=(0,1)
be the origin of
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1,1}
. Then
\widetilde{W}_{n}(N)=0
, and
\varphi_{N}(z)=1
for all
z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
. By Proposition
4.1
, we can define
\Omega_{t}
by
\varphi_{\Omega_{t}}(z)=(1-t)+t\varphi_{\Omega}(z)
such that
\Omega_{0}=N
,
\Omega_{1}=\Omega
, and
\partial\Omega_{t}
is smooth and uniformly h-convex for
t\in(0,1]
.
Integrating the both sides of (
10.11
) from
0
to
1
then yields

\displaystyle\widetilde{W}_{n}(\Omega)=\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}(1-\varphi^{-n}_{\Omega})d\sigma=\frac{1}{n}\omega_{n}-\frac{1}{n}\int_{\partial\Omega}p_{n}(\tilde{\kappa})d\mu

for any smooth uniformly h-convex bounded domain
\Omega
. Thus we obtain (
10.8
) and (
10.9
).
By (
10.3
) and (
10.2
), we have

(10.12)

\displaystyle\widetilde{W}_{n}(\Omega)=I_{n}(r^{n}_{\Omega})=\frac{\omega_{n}}{n}(1-e^{-nr^{n}_{\Omega}}).

Comparing (
10.9
) with (
10.12
), we obtain the desired formula (
10.10
).
We complete the proof of Lemma
10.1
.
∎

Lemma 10.2
.

Let
n\geq 1
be an integer and
q\in(-1,0)\cup(0,+\infty)
be a real number. Assume that
f
and
g
are two positive continuous functions on
\mathbb{S}^{n}
, then

(10.13)

\left(\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}(f+g)^{-q}d\sigma\right)^{-\frac{1}{q}}\geq\left(\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}f^{-q}d\sigma\right)^{-\frac{1}{q}}+\left(\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}g^{-q}d\sigma\right)^{-\frac{1}{q}}.

Equality holds if and only if
f^{-1}g
is constant.

Proof.

Case 1.

q\in(-1,0)
.
By the Hölder inequality, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}f^{-q}d\sigma\leq

\displaystyle\left(\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}f(f+g)^{-q-1}d\sigma\right)^{-q}\left(\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}(f+g)^{-q}d\sigma\right)^{q+1},

with equality if and only if
f(f+g)^{-1}
is constant on
\mathbb{S}^{n}
.
Then,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}(f+g)^{-q}d\sigma=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}(f+g)(f+g)^{-q-1}d\sigma

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}f(f+g)^{-q-1}d\sigma+\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}g(f+g)^{-q-1}d\sigma

(10.14)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\left(\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}(f+g)^{-q}d\sigma\right)^{\frac{q+1}{q}}\left(\left(\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}f^{-q}d\sigma\right)^{-\frac{1}{q}}+\left(\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}g^{-q}d\sigma\right)^{-\frac{1}{q}}\right),

with equality if and only if both
f(f+g)^{-1}
and
g(f+g)^{-1}
are constants, equivalently,
f^{-1}g
is constant. Thus (
10.13
) is proved for
q\in(-1,0)
.

Case 2.

q\in(0,+\infty)
.
By the Hölder inequality, we have

\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}(f+g)^{-q}d\sigma\leq\left(\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}f(f+g)^{-q-1}d\sigma\right)^{\frac{q}{q+1}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}f^{-q}d\sigma\right)^{\frac{1}{q+1}},

with equality if and only if
f(f+g)^{-1}
is constant on
\mathbb{S}^{n}
.
Hence inequality (
10.14
) is also valid for positive
q
. In this case, equality holds in (
10.14
) if and only if
f^{-1}g
is constant on
\mathbb{S}^{n}
.
This completes the proof of Lemma
10.2
.
∎

Now we are able to prove the following Theorem
10.4
, which is a special case of Conjecture
10.3
.

Theorem 10.4
.

Conjecture
10.3
holds in the case
n\geq 1
and
k=n
.

Proof.

Case 1.

a>0
,
b>0
and
a+b\geq 1
. By the assumption in Conjecture
10.3
and Definition
1.1
, we have
\varphi_{\Omega}^{p}(z)=a\varphi_{K}^{p}(z)+b\varphi_{L}^{p}(z)
.
Taking
q=\frac{n}{p}>0
,
f(z)=a\varphi_{K}^{p}(z)
and
g(z)=b\varphi_{L}^{p}(z)
in (
10.13
), we have

(10.15)

\displaystyle\left(\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}\varphi^{-n}_{\Omega}d\sigma\right)^{-\frac{p}{n}}\geq a\left(\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}\varphi^{-n}_{K}d\sigma\right)^{-\frac{p}{n}}+b\left(\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}\varphi^{-n}_{L}d\sigma\right)^{-\frac{p}{n}}.

Equality holds in (
10.15
) if and only if
\varphi_{K}^{-1}\varphi_{L}
is constant on
\mathbb{S}^{n}
, which means
K
and
L
are hyperbolic dilates, see Definition
6.2
.
Using (
10.10
), we have

(10.16)

\displaystyle\exp\left(pI_{n}^{-1}(\widetilde{W}_{n}(\Omega))\right)=e^{pr^{n}_{\Omega}}=\omega_{n}^{\frac{p}{n}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}\varphi^{-n}_{\Omega}d\sigma\right)^{-\frac{p}{n}}.

Substituting (
10.16
) into (
10.15
), we obtain

(10.17)

\displaystyle\exp\left(pI_{n}^{-1}(\widetilde{W}_{n}(\Omega))\right)\geq a\exp\left(pI_{n}^{-1}(\widetilde{W}_{n}(K))\right)+b\exp\left(pI_{n}^{-1}(\widetilde{W}_{n}(L))\right),

with equality if and only if
K
and
L
are hyperbolic dilates.

Case 2.
Either
a\geq 1
and
b=0
, or
a=0
and
b\geq 1
. It is easy to see that equality holds in (
10.15
). Hence, in this case, equality holds in (
10.17
) for all smooth uniformly h-convex bounded domains
K
and
L
.

We complete the proof of Theorem
10.4
.
∎

Remark 10.1
.

If we omit the geometric meaning of
\varphi_{\Omega}
, then Lemma
10.2
implies that inequality (
10.15
) holds for
p\in[-n,0)\cup(0,+\infty)
.

Next, we will show that Conjecture
10.3
holds in the case
K=L
. The following Theorem C was proved by Andrews et al.
[
3
, Corollary 1.9]
, see also an alternative proof in
[
45
, Remark 1.7]
.

Theorem C

(
[
3
]
)
.

Let
M=\partial\Omega
be a smooth, closed and uniformly h-convex hypersurface in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}

(n\geq 1)
. Then for any
0\leq l<k\leq n
, there holds

\widetilde{W}_{k}(\Omega)\geq I_{k}\circ I_{l}^{-1}(\widetilde{W}_{l}(\Omega)),

with equality holding if and only if
\Omega
is a geodesic ball.

From Theorem C, we can obtain the following Alexandrov-Fenchel type inequalities.

Corollary 10.1
.

Let
\Omega
be a smooth uniformly h-convex bounded domain in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}

(n\geq 1)
. Then

(10.18)

\int_{\partial\Omega}p_{k}(\tilde{\kappa})d\mu\geq\omega_{n}\sinh^{n-k}r^{k}_{\Omega}e^{-kr^{k}_{\Omega}},\quad k=0,1,\ldots,n-1.

Equality holds if and only if
\Omega
is a geodesic ball.

Proof.

By Theorem C, we have

\widetilde{W}_{k+1}(\Omega)\geq I_{k+1}\circ I_{k}^{-1}(\widetilde{W}_{k}(\Omega))=I_{k+1}(r^{k}_{\Omega}).

Hence, by using (
10.1
),
e^{-t}=\cosh t-\sinh t
and integration by parts, we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{W}_{k+1}(\Omega)\geq

\displaystyle\omega_{n}\int_{0}^{r^{k}_{\Omega}}\sinh^{n-k-1}te^{-(k+1)t}dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega_{n}\int_{0}^{r^{k}_{\Omega}}\sinh^{n-k-1}t\cosh te^{-kt}dt-\omega_{n}\int_{0}^{r^{k}_{\Omega}}\sinh^{n-k}te^{-kt}dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\omega_{n}}{n-k}\sinh^{n-k}te^{-kt}\right|_{t=0}^{t=r^{k}_{\Omega}}+\frac{k\omega_{n}}{n-k}\int_{0}^{r^{k}_{\Omega}}\sinh^{n-k}te^{-kt}dt-\widetilde{W}_{k}(\Omega)

(10.19)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\omega_{n}}{n-k}\sinh^{n-k}r^{k}_{\Omega}e^{-kr^{k}_{\Omega}}-\frac{n-2k}{n-k}\widetilde{W}_{k}(\Omega).

Equality holds if and only if
\Omega
is a geodesic ball.
Then inequality (
10.18
) follows directly from (
10.19
) and (
5.2
). We complete the proof of Corollary
10.1
.
∎

Proposition 10.1
.

Conjecture
10.3
holds in the case
K=L
. More generally, for any
n\geq 1
,
0\leq k\leq n-1
,
p>0
and
t\geq 1
, we have

(10.20)

\exp\left(pI_{k}^{-1}(\widetilde{W}_{k}(t\cdot\Omega))\right)\geq t\exp\left(pI_{k}^{-1}(\widetilde{W}_{k}(\Omega))\right)

holds for any smooth uniformly h-convex bounded domain
\Omega
. If
t>1
, then equality holds in (
10.20
) if and only if
\Omega
is a geodesic ball.

Proof.

Let
\Omega_{t}=t\cdot\Omega
,
r^{k}_{t}:=I_{k}^{-1}(\widetilde{W}_{k}(\Omega_{t}))
and
\chi(t)=e^{pr^{k}_{t}}
.
Then the desired inequality (
10.20
) can be written shortly as

(10.21)

\chi(t)\geq t\chi(1),\quad\forall\ t\geq 1.

Definition
6.1
gives

u_{\Omega_{t}}(z):=u(z,t)=u(z,1)+\frac{1}{p}\log t.

Hence, at
t=1
we have

\frac{\partial}{\partial t}u(z,t)=\frac{1}{p}.

By Lemma
5.1
, (
5.3
) and (
10.1
), at
t=1
we have

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\widetilde{W}_{k}(t\cdot\Omega)=\frac{1}{p}\int_{\partial\Omega}p_{k}(\tilde{\kappa})d\mu,\quad I_{k}^{\prime}(r^{k}_{\Omega})=\omega_{n}\sinh^{n-k}r^{k}_{\Omega}e^{-kr^{k}_{\Omega}}.

Hence, by using (
10.18
), we have

\displaystyle\chi^{\prime}(1)=

\displaystyle pe^{pr^{k}_{\Omega}}\frac{d}{dt}r^{k}_{t}=pe^{pr^{k}_{\Omega}}\left(I^{\prime}_{k}(r^{k}_{\Omega})\right)^{-1}\frac{d}{dt}\widetilde{W}_{k}(t\cdot\Omega)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\omega_{n}\sinh^{n-k}r^{k}_{\Omega}e^{-kr^{k}_{\Omega}}\right)^{-1}e^{pr^{k}_{\Omega}}\int_{\partial\Omega}p_{k}(\tilde{\kappa})d\mu

(10.22)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle e^{pr^{k}_{\Omega}}=\chi(1),

with equality if and only if
\Omega
is a geodesic ball.
It is easy to see that (
10.22
) is necessary for (
10.21
); while, we claim that it is also sufficient.

Instead of
\Omega
, if we set
\overline{\Omega}={t_{0}}\cdot\Omega
be the initial domain for
t_{0}\geq 1
, and
\zeta(s)=\chi(t_{0}s)
, then inequality (
10.22
) implies
\zeta^{\prime}(1)\geq\zeta(1)
. Hence

\chi^{\prime}(t_{0})t_{0}=\zeta^{\prime}(1)\geq\zeta(1)=\chi(t_{0}).

Letting
t_{0}
runs from
1
to infinity, we have
\frac{d}{dt}\log\left(\frac{\chi(t)}{t}\right)\geq 0
for all
t\geq 1
. Then we get (
10.21
).
By the equality case of (
10.22
), if
t>1
, then equality holds in (
10.21
) if and only if
\Omega
is a geodesic ball. We complete the proof of Proposition
10.1
.
∎

By using Theorem
10.4
and Proposition
10.1
, we can simplify Conjecture
10.3
.

Proposition 10.2
.

Assume that Conjecture
10.3
holds in the case that
a\geq 0
and
b\geq 0
with
a+b=1
. Then Conjecture
10.3
holds in the case that
a\geq 0
and
b\geq 0
with
a+b>1
.

Proof.

We have proved in Theorem
10.4
and in Proposition
10.1
that Conjecture
10.3
holds when either
n\geq 1
and
k=n
, or
ab=0
.
Then we can suppose that
n\geq 1
,
0\leq k\leq n-1
,
a>0
,
b>0
and
a+b>1
without loss of generality.
For a smooth h-convex bounded domain
\Omega
, we set

\chi(\Omega)=\exp\left(pI_{k}^{-1}(\widetilde{W}_{k}(\Omega))\right),

where
0\leq k\leq n-1
and
\frac{1}{2}\leq p\leq 2
.
By (
10.7
) and the assumption in Proposition
10.2
, we have that inequality

(10.23)

\chi\left((1-t)\cdot K+_{p}t\cdot L\right)\geq(1-t)\chi(K)+t\chi(L)

holds for any smooth uniformly h-convex bounded domains
K
and
L
, and
t\in[0,1]
. If
t\in(0,1)
, then equality holds in (
10.23
) if and only if either
K=L
, or
K
and
L
are geodesic balls centered at the same point.
We set

\overline{\Omega}=\frac{a}{a+b}\cdot K+_{p}\frac{b}{a+b}\cdot L,

which is well-defined by Theorem
4.1
.
Using Proposition
10.1
and taking
t=\frac{b}{a+b}\in(0,1)
in (
10.23
), we have

(10.24)

\chi\left(a\cdot K+b\cdot L\right)=\chi\left((a+b)\cdot\overline{\Omega}\right)\geq(a+b)\chi(\overline{\Omega})\geq a\chi(K)+b\chi(L),

thus we obtain (
10.7
). Furthermore, if
a+b>1
, then Proposition
10.1
asserts that equality holds only if
\overline{\Omega}
is a geodesic ball.

Then equality holds in (
10.24
)
if and only if equality holds in (
10.23
) and
\overline{\Omega}
is a geodesic ball. Consequently, equality holds in (
10.24
) if and only if
K
and
L
are geodesic balls centered at the same point.
We complete the proof of Proposition
10.2
.
∎

In the classical convex geometry in Euclidean space, we know that the Minkowski inequality can be obtained by calculating the variation of the Brunn-Minkowski inequality. Furthermore, these two inequalities can deduce each other. Similarly, we propose the following conjectures of Minkowski type inequalities in hyperbolic space. Since the statement does not rely on the definition of hyperbolic
p
-sum in Definition
1.1
, we can relax the restriction on
p
.
Since Conjecture
10.3
has been solved in the case
k=n
in Theorem
10.4
, we mainly focus on the case
0\leq k\leq n-1
.

Conjecture 10.5

(Horospherical
p
-Minkowski inequalities of type I)
.

Let
n\geq 1
and
0\leq k\leq n
be integers, and let
p
be a real number.
Suppose that
K
and
L
are smooth uniformly h-convex bounded domains in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. If
p>0
, then

(10.25)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}\varphi_{L}^{p}\varphi_{K}^{-p-n}p_{n-k}(\tilde{\lambda}_{K})d\sigma-\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}\varphi_{K}^{-n}p_{n-k}(\tilde{\lambda}_{K})d\sigma\geq\omega_{n}\sinh^{n-k}r^{k}_{K}e^{-kr^{k}_{K}}\left(e^{p(r^{k}_{L}-r^{k}_{K})}-1\right),

where
r^{k}_{\Omega}
is defined by
r^{k}_{\Omega}=I_{k}^{-1}(\widetilde{W}_{k}(\Omega))
for a h-convex bounded domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
.

(1)

When
0\leq k\leq n-1
, equality holds in (
10.25
) if and only if one of the following conditions holds,

(a)

K=L
,

(b)

K
and
L
are geodesic balls centered at the same point.

(2)

When
k=n
, equality holds in (
10.25
) if and only if
K=c\cdot L
or
L=c\cdot K
for some constant
c\geq 1
.

If
-n\leq p<0
, then

(10.26)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}\varphi_{L}^{p}\varphi_{K}^{-p-n}p_{n-k}(\tilde{\lambda}_{K})d\sigma-\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}\varphi_{K}^{-n}p_{n-k}(\tilde{\lambda}_{K})d\sigma\leq\omega_{n}\sinh^{n-k}r^{k}_{K}e^{-kr^{k}_{K}}\left(e^{p(r^{k}_{L}-r^{k}_{K})}-1\right).

(1)

When
0\leq k\leq n-1
and
-n<p<0
, equality holds in (
10.26
) if and only if one of the following conditions holds,

(a)

K=L
,

(b)

K
and
L
