11.
Weighted horospherical
p
-Brunn-Minkowski theory

Different from Theorem C, there are some geometric inequalities for hypersurfaces in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
which contain the quantity
\cosh r
, see
[
47
,
45
,
72
,
78
]
. Here
r
denotes the geodesic distance from points to a fixed origin in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
.
Various assumptions of convexity of hypersurfaces were considered in these papers. Nevertheless, h-convex hypersurfaces satisfy most of them. Then we can ask:
Whether there exists a horospherical Brunn-Minkowski theory with weight
\cosh r
?

In Section
11
, we approach this problem with similar works as in the previous sections. We recall that
\mathrm{Vol}_{w}(\Omega):=\int_{\Omega}\cosh rdv
, where
\Omega
is a bounded domain in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
.

11.1.
Weighted horospherical
p
-Minkowski problem

The following definitions are similar to Definition
5.1
–
5.2
.

Definition 11.1
.

Let
n\geq 1
be an integer and
\frac{1}{2}\leq p\leq 2
be a real number.
Assume that
K
and
L
are two smooth uniformly h-convex bounded domains in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
.
The
p
-mixed weighted volume of
K
,
L
is defined by

\displaystyle V_{\omega,p}(K,L)=\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\frac{\mathrm{Vol}_{w}(K+_{p}t\cdot L)-\mathrm{Vol}_{w}(K)}{t}.

Definition 11.2
.

Let
n\geq 1
be an integer and
p
be a real number.
Assume that
K
is a smooth uniformly h-convex bounded domain in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
.
The weighted horospherical
p
-surface area measure of
K
is defined by

\displaystyle dS_{\omega,p}(K,z)=\cosh r_{K}\varphi_{K}^{-p}\det(A[\varphi_{K}])d\sigma,

where

\displaystyle\cosh r_{K}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{|D\varphi_{K}|^{2}}{\varphi_{K}}(z)+\frac{1}{2}\left(\varphi_{K}(z)+\frac{1}{\varphi_{K}(z)}\right).

Then, by a weighted version of Lemma
5.2
, we see that

\displaystyle V_{\omega,p}(K,L)=\frac{1}{p}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}\varphi_{L}^{p}dS_{\omega,p}(K,z).

Similar to Problem
5.1
, we can propose the following prescribed measure problem for h-convex bounded domains.

Problem 11.1

(Weighted horospherical
p
-Minkowski problem)
.

Let
n\geq 1
be an integer and
p
be a real number.
Given a positive function
f(z)
defined on
\mathbb{S}^{n}
, what are necessary and sufficient conditions for
f
, such that there exists a smooth uniformly h-convex bounded domain
K\subset\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
satisfying

\displaystyle dS_{\omega,p}(K,z)=f(z)d\sigma.

That is, finding a smooth positive solution to

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\varphi^{-p}(z)\left(\frac{|D\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}(z)+\varphi(z)+\frac{1}{\varphi(z)}\right)p_{n}(A[\varphi(z)])=f(z),

such that
A[\varphi(z)]>0
for all
z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
.

11.2.
Weighted horospherical
p
-Brunn-Minkowski inequality and Weighted horospherical
p
-Minkowski inequalities

Let
\Omega
be a bounded domain in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. Define

(11.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(\Omega)=(n+1)^{\frac{1}{n+1}}\omega_{n}^{-\frac{1}{n+1}}\mathrm{Vol}_{\omega}^{\frac{1}{n+1}}(\Omega).

Geometrically, we have
\mathcal{S}(B(r))=\sinh r
. Similar to Conjecture
10.3
, we propose the following Brunn-Minkowski type inequality.

Conjecture 11.3

(Weighted horospherical
p
-Brunn-Minkowski inequality)
.

Let
n\geq 1
be an integer and
\frac{1}{2}\leq p\leq 2
be a real number.
Assume that
K
and
L
are two smooth origin symmetric, uniformly h-convex bounded domains in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. Let
\Omega=a\cdot K+_{p}b\cdot L
, where
a\geq 0
,
b\geq 0
and
a+b\geq 1
. Then

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{S}(\Omega)+\sqrt{\mathcal{S}^{2}(\Omega)+1}\right)^{p}\geq a\left(\mathcal{S}(K)+\sqrt{\mathcal{S}^{2}(K)+1}\right)^{p}+b\left(\mathcal{S}(L)+\sqrt{\mathcal{S}^{2}(L)+1}\right)^{p}.

Equality holds if and only if one of the following conditions holds,

(1)

a=1
and
b=0
,

(2)

a=0
and
b=1
,

(3)

K=L
and
a+b=1
,

(4)

K
and
L
are geodesic balls centered at the origin.

Remark 11.1
.

By calculating the weighted volume of geodesic balls not centered at the origin, we find that
the origin-symmetric assumption in Conjecture
11.3
can not be removed. Furthermore,
it can’t be replaced by the assumption that
K
and
L
both contain the origin in their interiors.
We will discuss it in Subsection
13.3
.

Here we recall Theorem F.

Theorem F

(
[
72
]
)
.

Let
\Omega
be a smooth uniformly h-convex bounded domain in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}

(n\geq 1)
which contains the origin in its interior. Then

(11.2)

\displaystyle\int_{\partial\Omega}\cosh rd\mu\geq\left(\left((n+1)\mathrm{Vol}_{w}(\Omega)\right)^{2}+\omega_{n}^{\frac{2}{n+1}}\left((n+1)\mathrm{Vol}_{w}(\Omega)\right)^{\frac{2n}{n+1}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Equality holds if and only if
\Omega
is a geodesic ball centered at the origin.

By a similar proof as in Proposition
10.1
, one can prove the following special case of Conjecture
11.3
.

Proposition 11.1
.

Conjecture
11.3
holds in the case
K=L
and
K
is a smooth uniformly h-convex bounded domain which contains the origin in its interior. Precisely, if we assume that
p>0
,
t\geq 1
, and
\Omega
is a smooth uniformly h-convex bounded domain that contains the origin in its interior, then

(11.3)

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{S}(t\cdot\Omega)+\sqrt{\mathcal{S}^{2}(t\cdot\Omega)+1}\right)^{p}\geq t\left(\mathcal{S}(\Omega)+\sqrt{\mathcal{S}^{2}(\Omega)+1}\right)^{p}.

If
t>1
,
then equality holds in (
11.3
) if and only if
\Omega
is a geodesic ball centered at the origin.

Proof.

Let
t\geq 1
,
\Omega_{t}:=t\cdot\Omega
, and
u(z,t)
be the horospherical support function of
\Omega_{t}
.
Denote
u(z):=u(z,1)
. From Definition
6.1
, we have
\varphi^{p}(z,t)=t\varphi^{p}(z)
and
u(z,t)=u(z,1)+\frac{1}{p}\log t
, which implies
\partial_{t}u(z,t)=\frac{1}{pt}
.
By using (
11.1
), (
5.3
) and the co-area formula, we have

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{S}(\Omega_{t})=

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\left((n+1)^{\frac{1}{n+1}}\omega_{n}^{-\frac{1}{n+1}}\mathrm{Vol}_{\omega}^{\frac{1}{n+1}}(\Omega_{t})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(n+1)^{-\frac{n}{n+1}}\omega_{n}^{-\frac{1}{n+1}}\mathrm{Vol}_{\omega}^{-\frac{n}{n+1}}(\Omega_{t})\int_{\partial\Omega_{t}}\cosh r\frac{1}{pt}d\mu_{t}

(11.4)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{pt}\omega_{n}^{-1}\mathcal{S}^{-n}(\Omega_{t})\int_{\partial\Omega_{t}}\cosh rd\mu_{t}.

By (
11.1
), Theorem F asserts that

\displaystyle\int_{\partial\Omega_{t}}\cosh rd\mu_{t}\geq

\displaystyle\left(\left(\omega_{n}\mathcal{S}^{n+1}(\Omega_{t})\right)^{2}+\omega_{n}^{\frac{2}{n+1}}\left(\omega_{n}\mathcal{S}^{n+1}(\Omega_{t})\right)^{\frac{2n}{n+1}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\omega_{n}^{2}\mathcal{S}^{2n+2}(\Omega_{t})+\omega_{n}^{2}\mathcal{S}^{2n}(\Omega_{t})\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

(11.5)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega_{n}\mathcal{S}^{n}(\Omega_{t})\sqrt{\mathcal{S}^{2}(\Omega_{t})+1}.

Substituting (
11.5
) into (
11.4
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{S}(\Omega_{t})\geq\frac{1}{pt}\sqrt{\mathcal{S}^{2}(\Omega_{t})+1}.

Hence,

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\log\left(\mathcal{S}(\Omega_{t})+\sqrt{\mathcal{S}^{2}(\Omega_{t})+1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{S}(\Omega_{t})+\sqrt{\mathcal{S}^{2}(\Omega_{t})+1}\right)^{-1}\left(\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{S}(\Omega_{t})+\frac{1}{2}\frac{2\mathcal{S}(\Omega_{t})}{\sqrt{\mathcal{S}^{2}(\Omega_{t})+1}}\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{S}(\Omega_{t})\right)

(11.6)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{S}(\Omega_{t})+\sqrt{\mathcal{S}^{2}(\Omega_{t})+1}\right)^{-1}\frac{\mathcal{S}(\Omega_{t})+\sqrt{\mathcal{S}^{2}(\Omega_{t})+1}}{\sqrt{\mathcal{S}^{2}(\Omega_{t})+1}}\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{S}(\Omega_{t})\geq\frac{1}{pt}.

Integrating the both sides of (
11.6
), we obtain

\displaystyle\log\left(\mathcal{S}(\Omega_{t})+\sqrt{\mathcal{S}^{2}(\Omega_{t})+1}\right)\geq\frac{1}{p}\log t+\log\left(\mathcal{S}(\Omega)+\sqrt{\mathcal{S}^{2}(\Omega)+1}\right),

thus we obtain (
11.3
).

By Theorem F, equality holds in (
11.5
) if and only if
\Omega_{t}
is a geodesic ball centered at the origin, which means that
\varphi(z,t)=t^{\frac{1}{p}}\varphi(z)
is constant on
\mathbb{S}^{n}
. Hence, equality holds in (
11.5
) for some
t\geq 1
if and only if
\Omega
is a geodesic ball centered at the origin. Then we have that equality holds in (
11.3
) if and only if either
t=1
, or
\Omega
is a geodesic ball centered at the origin. This completes the proof of Proposition
11.1
.
∎

Letting
a=1-t
and
b=t
in Conjecture
11.3
and using a similar calculation as in Proposition
10.3
, we can propose the following Conjecture
11.4
, which is the weighted version of Conjecture
10.5
.

Conjecture 11.4

(Weighted horospherical
p
-Minkowski inequality of type I)
.

Let
K
and
L
be two smooth origin-symmetric, uniformly h-convex bounded domains in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}

(n\geq 1)
. If
p>0
, then

\displaystyle\int_{\partial K}\varphi_{L}^{p}\cosh r(\cosh r-\tilde{u})^{p}d\mu-\int_{\partial K}\cosh rd\mu

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\omega_{n}\mathcal{S}^{n}(K)\sqrt{\mathcal{S}^{2}(K)+1}\left(\left(\frac{\mathcal{S}(L)+\sqrt{\mathcal{S}^{2}(L)+1}}{\mathcal{S}(K)+\sqrt{\mathcal{S}^{2}(K)+1}}\right)^{p}-1\right).

If
p<0
, then

\displaystyle\int_{\partial K}\varphi_{L}^{p}\cosh r(\cosh r-\tilde{u})^{p}d\mu-\int_{\partial K}\cosh rd\mu

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\omega_{n}\mathcal{S}^{n}(K)\sqrt{\mathcal{S}^{2}(K)+1}\left(\left(\frac{\mathcal{S}(L)+\sqrt{\mathcal{S}^{2}(L)+1}}{\mathcal{S}(K)+\sqrt{\mathcal{S}^{2}(K)+1}}\right)^{p}-1\right).

In the both inequalities, equality holds if and only if either

(1)

K=L
, or

(2)

K
and
L
are geodesic balls centered at the origin.

Proposition 11.2
.

Assume that
\frac{1}{2}\leq p\leq 2
and
\Omega_{t}:=(1-t)\cdot K+_{p}t\cdot L
, then Conjecture
11.4
is equivalent to

(11.7)

\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}\left(\mathcal{S}(\Omega_{t})+\sqrt{\mathcal{S}^{2}(\Omega_{t})+1}\right)^{p}\geq\left(\mathcal{S}(L)+\sqrt{\mathcal{S}^{2}(L)+1}\right)^{p}-\left(\mathcal{S}(K)+\sqrt{\mathcal{S}^{2}(K)+1}\right)^{p}.

Equality holds if and only if either
K=L
, or
K
and
L
are geodesic balls centered at the origin.
Thus Conjecture
11.4
can be obtained from Conjecture
11.3
in this case.

Proof.

At first, we show the equivalence between (
11.7
) and Conjecture
11.4
.
Let
u(z,t)
denote the horospherical support function of
\Omega_{t}
. Then we have
\varphi^{p}(z,t)=(1-t)\varphi_{K}^{p}(z)+t\varphi_{L}^{p}(z)
by the definition of
\Omega_{t}
. Thus

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u(z,t)=\frac{\partial_{t}\varphi^{p}(z,t)}{p\varphi^{p}(z,t)}=\frac{1}{p}\frac{\varphi_{L}^{p}(z)-\varphi_{K}^{p}(z)}{(1-t)\varphi_{K}^{p}(z)+t\varphi_{L}^{p}(z)}.

Using (
5.3
) and a similar calculation as in (
11.4
), we have

(11.8)

\displaystyle\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}\mathcal{S}(\Omega_{t})=\omega_{n}^{-1}\mathcal{S}^{-n}(K)\int_{\partial K}\cosh r\left(\frac{1}{p}\frac{\varphi_{L}^{p}(z)-\varphi_{K}^{p}(z)}{\varphi_{K}^{p}(z)}\right)d\mu.

A direct calculation gives

(11.9)

\displaystyle\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}\log\left(\mathcal{S}(\Omega_{t})+\sqrt{\mathcal{S}^{2}(\Omega_{t})+1}\right)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{S}^{2}(K)+1}}\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}\mathcal{S}(\Omega_{t}).

Inserting (
11.8
) into (
11.9
), we have

\displaystyle\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}\left(\mathcal{S}(\Omega_{t})+\sqrt{\mathcal{S}^{2}(\Omega_{t})+1}\right)^{p}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p\left(\mathcal{S}(K)+\sqrt{\mathcal{S}^{2}(K)+1}\right)^{p}\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}\log\left(\mathcal{S}(\Omega_{t})+\sqrt{\mathcal{S}^{2}(\Omega_{t})+1}\right)

(11.10)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{S}(K)+\sqrt{\mathcal{S}^{2}(K)+1}\right)^{p}\frac{\int_{\partial K}\varphi_{L}^{p}(z)\cosh r\left(\cosh r-\tilde{u}\right)^{p}d\mu-\int_{\partial K}\cosh rd\mu}{\omega_{n}\mathcal{S}^{n}(K)\sqrt{\mathcal{S}^{2}(K)+1}},

where we used (
3.24
) in the second equality.
Substituting (
11.10
) into the left-hand side of (
11.7
), we have

\displaystyle\int_{\partial K}\varphi_{L}^{p}(z)\cosh r\left(\cosh r-\tilde{u}\right)^{p}d\mu-\int_{\partial K}\cosh rd\mu

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\omega_{n}\mathcal{S}^{n}(K)\sqrt{\mathcal{S}^{2}(K)+1}\left(\left(\frac{\mathcal{S}(L)+\sqrt{\mathcal{S}^{2}(L)+1}}{\mathcal{S}(K)+\sqrt{\mathcal{S}^{2}(K)+1}}\right)^{p}-1\right),

thus we have that (
11.7
) is equivalent to Conjecture
11.4
in the case
\frac{1}{2}\leq p\leq 2
.

If we admit Conjecture
11.3
, then we have

(11.11)

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{S}(\Omega_{t})+\sqrt{\mathcal{S}^{2}(\Omega_{t})+1}\right)^{p}\geq(1-t)\left(\mathcal{S}(K)+\sqrt{\mathcal{S}^{2}(K)+1}\right)^{p}+t\left(\mathcal{S}(L)+\sqrt{\mathcal{S}^{2}(L)+1}\right)^{p}

for all
t\in[0,1]
.
At this time, (
11.7
) can be achieved by differentiating the both sides of (
11.11
) at
t=0
.
Thus we have that Conjecture
11.4
can be obtained from Conjecture
11.3
.
We complete the proof of Proposition
11.2
.
∎

When
p>0
, the following Conjecture
11.5
can be considered as either a weighted version of Conjecture
10.6
, or a corollary of Conjecture
11.4
by Theorem F. At the same time, we can also get it by letting
a=1
and
b=t
in Conjecture
11.3
and doing a similar calculation as in Proposition
10.3
.

Conjecture 11.5

(Weighted horospherical
p
-Minkowski inequality of type II)
.

Let
n\geq 1
be an integer and
p>0
be a real number.
Assume that
K
and
L
are smooth uniformly h-convex bounded domains in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. Then

\displaystyle\int_{\partial K}\varphi_{L}^{p}(z)\cosh r(\cosh r-\tilde{u})^{p}d\mu

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\omega_{n}\mathcal{S}^{n}(K)\sqrt{\mathcal{S}^{2}(K)+1}\left(\frac{\mathcal{S}(L)+\sqrt{\mathcal{S}^{2}(L)+1}}{\mathcal{S}(K)+\sqrt{\mathcal{S}^{2}(K)+1}}\right)^{p}.

Equality holds if and only if
K
and
L
are geodesic balls centered at the origin.

In the study of Conjecture
10.6
, we proved a special case in Theorem
10.10
. Hence we can propose the following Conjecture
11.6
, which is the corresponding weighted version of Theorem
10.10
.

Conjecture 11.6
.

Let
\Omega
be a smooth uniformly h-convex bounded domain in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. If
0\leq p<1
, then

(11.12)

\displaystyle\int_{\partial\Omega}\cosh r(\cosh r-\tilde{u})^{p}d\mu\geq\omega_{n}\mathcal{S}^{n}(\Omega)\sqrt{\mathcal{S}^{2}(\Omega)+1}\left(\mathcal{S}(\Omega)+\sqrt{\mathcal{S}^{2}(\Omega)+1}\right)^{-p}.

Equality holds if and only if
\Omega
is a geodesic ball centered at the origin.

Remark 11.2
.

It is natural to propose Conjecture
11.6
for
p\geq 0
.
However, if
p\geq 1
, then we will prove (
11.12
) in the follow-up Subsection
11.3
.

11.3.
Proof of (
11.12
) in the case
p\geq 1

The main result in Subsection
11.3
is the following Theorem
11.7
.

Theorem 11.7
.

Let
\Omega
be a smooth bounded domain in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. If
p\geq 1
, then

(11.13)

\int_{\partial\Omega}\cosh r(\cosh r-\tilde{u})^{p}d\mu\geq\omega_{n}\mathcal{S}^{n}(\Omega)\sqrt{\mathcal{S}^{2}(\Omega)+1}\left(\mathcal{S}(\Omega)+\sqrt{\mathcal{S}^{2}(\Omega)+1}\right)^{-p}.

Equality holds if and only if
\Omega
is a geodesic ball centered at the origin.

The authors
[
54
]
have gave a new proof of Theorem F and have shown that
\Omega
need not contain the origin, and the h-convex assumption of
\Omega
can be removed. That means that the
p=0
case of Conjecture
11.6
has been completely solved in
[
54
]
.
Fortunately, the method used in
[
54
]
can also be used to prove Theorem
11.7
.

In the first step, we define the vertical projection map
\Pi
from
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1,1}
to
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}:=\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\times\{0\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1,1}
by
\Pi(x,x_{n+1})=x
. Denote by
\rho:=|x|
the radial function in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. Here we remark that Brendle and Wang
[
17
]
used the map
\Pi
to prove the Penrose inequality for surface lying in a convex static timelike hypersurface in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1,1}
, see also
[
62
]
. For a bounded domain
\Omega
in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
, let
\overline{\Omega}
denote
\Pi(\Omega)
in this subsection. The support function and the outward unit normal of
\overline{\Omega}
are denoted by
\overline{u}
and
\overline{\nu}
, respectively.

If we write
dv:=\sinh^{n}rdrd\sigma
and
d\overline{v}:=\rho^{n}d\rho d\sigma
for the volume elements of
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
and
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
respectively, then

(11.14)

\pi^{*}(d\overline{v})=\pi^{*}\left(\rho^{n}d\rho d\sigma\right)=\sinh^{n}r\cosh rdrd\sigma=\cosh rdv.

Therefore,
\mathrm{Vol}_{w}\left(\Omega\right)
is exactly the classical volume of
\overline{\Omega}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. Consequently, the right-hand side of (
11.13
) is a function of
\mathrm{Vol}\left(\overline{\Omega}\right)
. In the second step, we will rewrite the left-hand side of (
11.13
) by using the geometric quantities of
\overline{\Omega}
. The outward unit normal at
X
of
\partial\Omega
is given by (see
[
54
]
)

(11.15)

\nu\left(X\right)=\frac{\left(\overline{\nu}+\overline{u}x,x_{n+1}\overline{u}\right)}{\left(\overline{u}^{2}+1\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}},

where
\overline{\nu}
and
\overline{u}
are evaluated at
x\in\partial\overline{\Omega}
.
For the conformal Killing vector field
V=\sinh r\partial_{r}
, we know

V=\sinh r\partial_{r}\left(\sinh r\theta,\cosh r\right)=\left(\cosh rx,\sinh^{2}r\right)=\left(x_{n+1}x,|x|^{2}\right).

This together with (
11.15
) gives

\widetilde{u}\left(X\right):=\langle V,\nu\rangle=\frac{x_{n+1}\overline{u}}{\left(\overline{u}^{2}+1\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}}.

Therefore

(11.16)

\left(\cosh r-\widetilde{u}\right)\left(X\right)=x_{n+1}\left(1-\frac{\overline{u}}{\left(\overline{u}^{2}+1\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\right).

Letting
d\overline{\mu}
denote the area element of
\partial\overline{\Omega}
, we have by (
11.15
)

(11.17)

\Pi_{*}\left(\cosh rd\mu\right)=\Pi_{*}\nu\lrcorner d\overline{v}=\langle\Pi_{*}\nu,\overline{\nu}\rangle d\overline{\mu}=\left(\overline{u}^{2}+1\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}d\overline{\mu}.

Using (
11.16
) and (
11.17
), we can write the left-hand side of (
11.13
) as

\int_{\partial\Omega}\cosh r\left(\cosh r-\widetilde{u}\right)^{p}d\mu=\int_{\partial\overline{\Omega}}x_{n+1}^{p}\left(1-\frac{\overline{u}}{\left(\overline{u}^{2}+1\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\right)^{p}\left(\overline{u}^{2}+1\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}d\overline{\mu}.

Since
x_{n+1}=\left(|x|^{2}+1\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\geq\left(\overline{u}^{2}+1\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}
, we have

(11.18)

\int_{\partial\Omega}\cosh r\left(\cosh r-\widetilde{u}\right)^{p}d\mu\geq\int_{\partial\overline{\Omega}}\left(\overline{u}^{2}+1\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\left(\overline{u}^{2}+1\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}-\overline{u}\right)^{p}d\overline{\mu},\quad\forall p\geq 0.

Finally, we are ready to prove Theorem
11.7
.

Proof of Theorem
11.7
.

At first, we define a positive function
\chi(t)
on
\mathbb{R}
by

\chi(t):=\left(t^{2}+1\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\left(t^{2}+1\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}-t\right)^{p}.

Then (
11.18
) can be written shortly as

(11.19)

\int_{\partial\Omega}\cosh r\left(\cosh r-\widetilde{u}\right)^{p}d\mu\geq\int_{\partial\overline{\Omega}}\chi(\overline{u})d\overline{\mu}.

Now we study the properties of
\chi(t)
.
A direct calculation gives

(11.20)

\left(\log\chi(t)\right)^{\prime}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\log\left(t^{2}+1\right)\right)^{\prime}+p\left(\log\left(\left(t^{2}+1\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}-t\right)\right)^{\prime}=\frac{t-p\left(t^{2}+1\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}}{t^{2}+1}.

As we assumed
p\geq 1
,
\chi(t)
is strictly decreasing on
\mathbb{R}
. For the second derivative of
\chi(t)
, we have by use of (
11.20
)

\displaystyle\frac{\chi^{\prime\prime}(t)}{\chi(t)}=

\displaystyle\left(\log\chi(t)\right)^{\prime\prime}+\left(\log\chi(t)\right)^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{t-p\left(t^{2}+1\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}}{t^{2}+1}\right)^{\prime}+\left(\frac{t-p\left(t^{2}+1\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}}{t^{2}+1}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(t^{2}+1\right)^{-2}\left(\left(t^{2}+1\right)\left(t-p\left(t^{2}+1\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)^{\prime}-2t\left(t-p\left(t^{2}+1\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)+\left(t-p\left(t^{2}+1\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(t^{2}+1\right)^{-2}\left(\left(t^{2}+1\right)\left(1-pt\left(t^{2}+1\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right)-2t^{2}+t^{2}+p^{2}\left(t^{2}+1\right)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(t^{2}+1\right)^{-2}\left(1-pt\left(t^{2}+1\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+p^{2}\left(t^{2}+1\right)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(t^{2}+1\right)^{-2}\left(1+p\left(t^{2}+1\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(p\left(t^{2}+1\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}-t\right)\right)>0,

where we used
p\geq 1
in the last inequality. Since
\chi^{\prime\prime}(t)>0
on
\mathbb{R}
, Jensen’s inequality yields that

(11.21)

\frac{\int_{\partial\overline{\Omega}}\chi\left(\overline{u}\right)d\overline{\mu}}{\int_{\partial\overline{\Omega}}d\overline{\mu}}\geq\chi\left(\frac{\int_{\partial\overline{\Omega}}\overline{u}d\overline{\mu}}{\int_{\partial\overline{\Omega}}d\overline{\mu}}\right),

with equality if and only if
\overline{u}
is constant on
\partial\overline{\Omega}
.
By the divergence theorem and the classical isoperimetric inequality, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\partial\overline{\Omega}}\overline{u}d\overline{\mu}=

\displaystyle(n+1)\mathrm{Vol}\left(\overline{\Omega}\right)>0,

(11.22)

\displaystyle\frac{\int_{\partial\overline{\Omega}}\overline{u}d\overline{\mu}}{\int_{\partial\overline{\Omega}}d\overline{\mu}}=

\displaystyle\frac{(n+1)\mathrm{Vol}\left(\overline{\Omega}\right)}{{\rm Area}\left(\partial\overline{\Omega}\right)}\leq\left(n+1\right)^{\frac{1}{n+1}}\omega_{n}^{-\frac{1}{n+1}}\mathrm{Vol}\left(\overline{\Omega}\right)^{\frac{1}{n+1}}.

Equality holds in (
11.22
) if and only if
\overline{\Omega}
is a geodesic ball in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
.
Now we let
\overline{\chi}(t):=t^{-1}\chi(t)
, which is strictly decreasing on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
.
Using (
11.21
) and (
11.22
), we arrive at

(11.23)

\int_{\partial\overline{\Omega}}\chi\left(\overline{u}\right)d\overline{\mu}\geq\int_{\partial\overline{\Omega}}\overline{u}d\overline{\mu}\cdot\overline{\chi}\left(\frac{\int_{\partial\overline{\Omega}}\overline{u}d\overline{\mu}}{\int_{\partial\overline{\Omega}}d\overline{\mu}}\right)\geq\left(n+1\right)\mathrm{Vol}\left(\overline{\Omega}\right)\overline{\chi}\left(\left(n+1\right)^{\frac{1}{n+1}}\mathrm{Vol}\left(\overline{\Omega}\right)^{\frac{1}{n+1}}\right).

Recall that (
11.14
) implies
\mathrm{Vol}\left(\overline{\Omega}\right)=\mathrm{Vol}_{w}\left(\Omega\right)
. Combining (
11.23
) with (
11.19
) and (
11.1
), we have

\displaystyle\int_{\partial\Omega}\cosh r\left(\cosh r-\widetilde{u}\right)^{p}d\mu\geq

\displaystyle\int_{\partial\overline{\Omega}}\chi(\overline{u})d\overline{\mu}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\left(n+1\right)\mathrm{Vol}\left(\overline{\Omega}\right)\overline{\chi}\left(\left(n+1\right)^{\frac{1}{n+1}}\omega_{n}^{-\frac{1}{n+1}}\mathrm{Vol}\left(\overline{\Omega}\right)^{\frac{1}{n+1}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(n+1\right)\mathrm{Vol}_{w}\left(\Omega\right)\overline{\chi}\left(\left(n+1\right)^{\frac{1}{n+1}}\omega_{n}^{-\frac{1}{n+1}}\mathrm{Vol}_{w}\left(\Omega\right)^{\frac{1}{n+1}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega_{n}\mathcal{S}^{n+1}\left(\Omega\right)\overline{\chi}\left(\mathcal{S}\left(\Omega\right)\right)

(11.24)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega_{n}\mathcal{S}^{n}(\Omega)\sqrt{\mathcal{S}^{2}(\Omega)+1}\left(\mathcal{S}(\Omega)+\sqrt{\mathcal{S}^{2}(\Omega)+1}\right)^{-p}.

Hence we obtain (
11.13
).

By (
11.21
) and (
11.22
), if equality holds in (
11.24
), then
\overline{\Omega}
is a geodesic ball, and
\overline{u}
is constant on
\overline{\Omega}
. Consequently, equality holds in (
11.13
) if and only if
\Omega
is a geodesic ball centered at the origin. This completes the proof of Theorem
11.7
.
∎

Remark 11.3
.

Letting
\chi(t):=\left(t^{2}+1\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}
, we can check directly that
t^{-1}\chi(t)
is strictly deceasing on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
, and
\chi(t)
is strictly convex on
\mathbb{R}
.
Therefore the above proof can be used to prove Theorem F without major changes, just as we have done in
[
54
]
.

12.
Relationship between the hyperbolic
p
-sum in hyperbolic space and the Minkowski-Firey
L_{p}
-addition in Euclidean space

The arguments in Section
12
are motivated by formula (
3.28
). For a convex body
\widehat{K}
with smooth support function
\varphi(z)
in Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, the position vectors of
\partial K
are exactly given by
D\varphi(z)+\varphi(z)z
,
z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
, see e.g.
[
73
, p. 47]
. Here, we identify the
(n+1)
-dimensional Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
with the hyperplane
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\times\{0\}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1,1}
. We will show that formula (
3.28
) actually gives a map
\pi
from h-convex bounded domains in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
to convex bounded domains in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
.
Subsections
12.1
–
12.2
are devoted to figure out the detailed properties of the map
\pi
.
Among those properties, Proposition
12.1
plays a key role.
Here we remark that map
\pi
closely relates to the Penrose inequality in general relativity, see
[
62
]
.
In the next step, we study the counterpart of
p
-Brunn-Minkowski inequality,
p
-Minkowski inequality and
p
-isoperimetric inequality in hyperbolic space. These works will be done in Subsections
12.3
–
12.4
.

In order to compare the geometry of domains in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
and
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, we make some conventions on the notations. Different from the horospherical Gauss map
G
, we use
\widehat{G}_{\widehat{\Omega}}
to denote the Euclidean Gauss map of a convex body
\widehat{\Omega}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. We denote the support function of
\widehat{\Omega}
as
\widehat{u}_{\widehat{\Omega}}(z)
, i.e.

\widehat{u}_{\widehat{\Omega}}(z)=\max_{x\in\widehat{\Omega}}\langle x,z\rangle,\quad z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}.

Now we recall the definition of Minkowski-Firey
L_{p}
-addition
[
30
]
(Firey’s
p
-sum for short) for convex bodies in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
.
Through Section
12
, we use
+_{p}^{\mathcal{R}}
to denote the Firey’s
p
-sums for convex bodies in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
and use
+_{p}^{\mathcal{H}}
to denote the hyperbolic
p
-sums for h-convex bounded domains in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
.

Let
p>0
be a positive number. Assume that
\widehat{K}
and
\widehat{L}
are convex bodies in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
that contain the origin in their interiors. Let
a\geq 0
and
b\geq 0
be two real numbers.
Let
\widehat{\Omega}:=a\cdot\widehat{K}+^{\mathcal{R}}_{p}b\cdot\widehat{L}
denote the
p
-linear combination of
\widehat{K}
and
\widehat{L}
.
If
p\geq 1
, then
\widehat{\Omega}
is defined by

\widehat{u}_{\widehat{\Omega}}^{p}(z)=a\widehat{u}_{\widehat{K}}^{p}(z)+b\widehat{u}_{\widehat{L}}^{p}(z).

If
p\in(0,1)
, then the definition of
\widehat{\Omega}
was proposed by Böröczky, Lutwak, Yang, and Zhang
[
12
]
, i.e.

\widehat{\Omega}:=\bigcap_{z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}}\left\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\left|\langle x,z\rangle\leq a\widehat{u}_{\widehat{K}}^{p}(z)+b\widehat{u}_{\widehat{L}}^{p}(z)\right.\right\}.

The above definitions can also be found in
[
73
, Chapter 9]
.
In the case
p\in(0,1)
, we should remark the following fact. If
\widehat{u}(z):=\left(a\widehat{u}_{\widehat{K}}^{p}(z)+b\widehat{u}_{\widehat{L}}^{p}(z)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}
satisfies
D^{2}\widehat{u}(z)+\widehat{u}(z)I>0
on
\mathbb{S}^{n}
, then
\widehat{u}_{\widehat{\Omega}}(z)=\widehat{u}(z)
.

For a positive number
p\geq 1
, the
p
-mixed volume

\mathrm{Vol}_{p}\left(\widehat{K},\widehat{L}\right)
of convex bodies
\widehat{K}
and
\widehat{L}
is defined by

(12.1)

\mathrm{Vol}_{p}\left(\widehat{K},\widehat{L}\right):=\frac{1}{n+1}\int_{\partial\widehat{K}}\widehat{u}_{\widehat{L}}^{p}(z)\widehat{u}^{1-p}_{\widehat{K}}(z)d\widehat{\mu},

where
z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
denoted the outward unit normal of
\partial\widehat{K}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
.
To avoid confusion, we may write
d\widehat{\mu}_{K}
for
d\widehat{\mu}
in the following text.

The following
L_{p}
Brunn-Minkowski inequality was proved by Firey
[
30
]
, see also an independent proof by Lutwak
[
59
]
.

Theorem G

(
L_{p}
Brunn-Minkowski inequality)
.

Let
p>1
be a real number. Assume that
\widehat{K}
and
\widehat{L}
are convex bodies in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
that contain the origin in their interiors. Then

(12.2)

\mathrm{Vol}\left(\widehat{K}+_{p}^{\mathcal{R}}\widehat{L}\right)^{\frac{p}{n+1}}\geq\mathrm{Vol}\left(\widehat{K}\right)^{\frac{p}{n+1}}+\mathrm{Vol}\left(\widehat{L}\right)^{\frac{p}{n+1}},

with equality if and only if
\widehat{K}
and
\widehat{L}
are dilates.

The following
p
-Minkowski inequality was proved by Lutwak
[
59
]
.

Theorem H

(
p
-Minkowski inequality)
.

Let
p>1
be a real number. Assume that
\widehat{K}
and
\widehat{L}
are convex bodies in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
that contain the origin in their interiors. Then

(12.3)

\mathrm{Vol}_{p}\left(\widehat{K},\widehat{L}\right)^{n+1}\geq\mathrm{Vol}\left(\widehat{K}\right)^{n+1-p}\mathrm{Vol}\left(\widehat{L}\right)^{p},

with equality if and only if
\widehat{K}
and
\widehat{L}
are dilates.

In Theorem H, by taking
\widehat{L}
as the geodesic balls centered at the origin in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, one will obtain an isoperimetric type inequality for each
p>1
, which is known as the
p
-isoperimetric inequality.

Theorem I

(
p
-isoperimetric inequality)
.

Let
p>1
be a real number. Assume that
\widehat{\Omega}
is a convex body in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
that contains the origin in its interior. Then

(12.4)

\int_{\partial\widehat{\Omega}}\widehat{u}_{\widehat{\Omega}}^{1-p}(z)d\widehat{\mu}\geq(n+1)^{\frac{n+1-p}{n+1}}\omega_{n}^{\frac{p}{n+1}}\mathrm{Vol}\left(\widehat{\Omega}\right)^{\frac{n+1-p}{n+1}},

with equality if and only if
\widehat{\Omega}
is a geodesic ball centered at the origin.

Remark 12.1
.

(1)

If
p=1
, then inequalities (
12.2
) and (
12.3
) also hold, which are the classical Brunn-Minkowski inequality and the Minkowski inequality. Each of the inequalities holds equality if and only if
\widehat{K}
and
\widehat{L}
are homothetic.

(2)

In the case
0\leq p<1
, the studies of
L_{p}
Brunn-Minkowski inequality and
p
-Minkowski inequality become more difficult. Böröczky et al.
[
12
]
considered the
p=0
case for origin symmetric convex bodies in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. For origin symmetric convex bodies in higher dimensional Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
(
n\geq 2
), the inequalities were proved for
p
close to
1^{-}
, see
[
21
,
50
]
.

12.1.
A projection map from
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
to
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}

Denote by
\widehat{B}(x,r)
the geodesic ball of radius
r
centered at
x
in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
.

Definition 12.1
.

Define a map
\pi
by

\pi\left(\Omega\right):=\bigcup_{X=(x,x_{n+1})\in\Omega}\widehat{B}\left(-x,x_{n+1}\right),

where
\Omega
is any set in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1,1}
.

Geometrically,
-\pi(X)=\widehat{B}\left(x,x_{n+1}\right)
is the domain enclosed by the intersection of the past light cone of
X=(x,x_{n+1})
with
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\times\{0\}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1,1}
. Here, we provide a concrete example of Definition
12.1
in the following Figure
12.1
.

Figure 12.1.
In this figure, the green surface denotes the hyperboloid model of
\mathbb{H}^{2}
in
\mathbb{R}^{2,1}
. Let
X=(\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2},\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2},\sqrt{2})
be a point on
\mathbb{H}^{2}
. The blue domain
\Omega
denotes the geodesic ball of radius
\frac{3}{2}\log 2
centered at
X
on
\mathbb{H}^{2}
, i.e.
\Omega=B(X,\frac{3}{2}\log r)
. Then the purple domain lying on
\{x_{2}=0\}
denotes
-\pi(\Omega)
. Later, Proposition
12.2
will imply that
-\pi(\Omega)=\widehat{B}((2,2),4)\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

We will show that
\pi
maps h-convex domains in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
to convex domains in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
in the following proposition.

Proposition 12.1
.

Assume that
\Omega
is a smooth uniformly h-convex bounded domain in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
with horospherical support function
u_{\Omega}(z)
. Then
\widehat{\Omega}:=\pi\left(\Omega\right)
is a smooth uniformly convex bounded domain in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
with support function

(12.5)

\widehat{u}_{\widehat{\Omega}}\left(z\right)=\varphi_{\Omega}(z)=e^{u_{\Omega}(z)}>0.

Consequently,
\widehat{\Omega}
contains the origin in its interior.

Before proving Proposition
12.1
, we state its applications in the following Theorem
12.2
, Corollary
12.1
and Proposition
12.2
.

Theorem 12.2
.

Let
p
,
a
,
b
,
K
and
L
satisfy the same assumptions as in Definition
1.1
. Let
\Omega=a\cdot K+_{p}^{\mathcal{H}}b\cdot L
. Then

(12.6)

\pi(\Omega)=a\cdot\pi(K)+_{p}^{\mathcal{R}}b\cdot\pi(L).

Proof.

Let
\widehat{K}=\pi(K)
,
\widehat{L}=\pi(L)
,
\widehat{\Omega}=\pi(\Omega)
, and
\varphi_{\Omega}^{p}(z)=\alpha\varphi_{K}^{p}(z)+\beta\varphi_{L}^{p}(z)
. By (
2.12
) and Proposition
2.1
, we have

D_{j}D_{i}\varphi_{\Omega}+\varphi_{\Omega}\delta_{ij}=A_{ij}[\varphi_{\Omega}]+\frac{1}{2}\frac{|D\varphi_{\Omega}|^{2}}{\varphi_{\Omega}}\delta_{ij}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\varphi_{\Omega}+\varphi_{\Omega}^{-1}\right)\delta_{ij}\geq 1>0.

This together with (
12.5
) gives

\displaystyle D^{2}\widehat{u}_{\widehat{\Omega}}+\widehat{u}_{\widehat{\Omega}}I=D^{2}\varphi_{\Omega}+\varphi_{\Omega}I>0,

\displaystyle\widehat{u}^{p}_{\widehat{\Omega}}(z)=\varphi^{p}_{\Omega}(z)=a\varphi^{p}_{K}(z)+b\varphi^{p}_{L}(z)=a\widehat{u}_{\widehat{K}}^{p}(z)+b\widehat{u}_{\widehat{L}}^{p}(z).

Therefore
\widehat{\Omega}=a\cdot\widehat{K}+_{p}^{\mathcal{R}}b\cdot\widehat{L}
. This completes the proof of Theorem
12.2
.
∎

Corollary 12.1
.

Let
\Omega
be a smooth uniformly h-convex bounded domain in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
and
\widehat{\Omega}=\pi(\Omega)
. Define
\xi:\partial\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
by

\xi(X)=\frac{\widetilde{u}X-\cosh r\nu}{\cosh r-\widetilde{u}}=-X+x_{n+1}(z,1),

where
X=(x,x_{n+1})\in\partial\Omega
and
z=G_{\Omega}(X)
.
Then
\xi
gives a diffeomorphism from
\partial\Omega
to
\partial\widehat{\Omega}
. Moreover,
G_{\Omega}(X)=\widehat{G}_{\widehat{\Omega}}\left(\xi(X)\right)
for all
X\in\partial\Omega
.

Proof.

For any
z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
, it is well-known that
\widehat{G}_{\widehat{\Omega}}^{-1}(z)=\left(D\widehat{u}_{\widehat{\Omega}}(z)+\widehat{u}_{\widehat{\Omega}}(z)z,0\right)
.
Using (
12.5
) and (
3.28
), we obtain

\widehat{G}_{\widehat{\Omega}}^{-1}(z)=\left(D\varphi_{\Omega}(z)+\varphi_{\Omega}(z)z,0\right)=\xi(X(z)).

We complete the proof of Corollary
12.1
.
∎

Proposition 12.2
.

Let
X=(x,x_{n+1})\in\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
and
\Omega:=B(X,r)
be a ball of radius
r
centered at
X
in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
.
Then
\widehat{\Omega}=\pi\left(\Omega\right)
is given by
\widehat{B}(-e^{r}x,e^{r}x_{n+1})
.

Proof.

It is well-known that the classical support function of the geodesic ball
\widehat{B}(y,\rho)
is given by

\widehat{u}_{\widehat{B}(y,\rho)}(z)=\rho+\langle y,z\rangle,\quad z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}.

On the other hand, formulas (
12.5
) and (
4.35
) yield that

\widehat{u}_{\widehat{\Omega}}(z)=\varphi_{\Omega}(z)=e^{r}\left(x_{n+1}-\langle x,z\rangle\right).

Then Proposition
12.2
follows from the above formulas.
∎

12.2.
Proof of Proposition
12.1

Lemma 12.1
.

Proposition
12.1
holds when
\Omega
degenerates to a single point
X=\left(x,x_{n+1}\right)
in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
.

Proof.

From Definition
12.1
, we have
\widehat{B}\left(-x,x_{n+1}\right)=\pi(X)
. Then we have

\widehat{u}_{\widehat{B}\left(-x,x_{n+1}\right)}\left(z\right)=\sup_{y\in\widehat{B}\left(-x,x_{n+1}\right)}\langle y,z\rangle=\langle-x,z\rangle+x_{n+1}.

At the same time, we have

e^{u_{X}(z)}=\varphi_{X}\left(z\right)=-\langle X,\left(z,1\right)\rangle=-\langle x,z\rangle+x_{n+1}.

The above two formulas imply
\widehat{u}_{\widehat{B}\left(-x,x_{n+1}\right)}(z)=e^{u_{X}\left(z\right)}
. We complete the proof of Lemma
12.1
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
12.1
.

In this proof, we will use Lemma
12.1
frequently without mention.
By the strict horo-convexity of
\Omega
, we have
A[\varphi_{\Omega}]>0
. Using (
2.12
) and (
3.22
), we know

(12.7)

D^{2}\varphi_{\Omega}(z)+\varphi_{\Omega}(z)I=A[\varphi_{\Omega}]+\cosh r\left(X(z)\right)>I,\quad\forall z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}.

Then we can assume that
\widetilde{\Omega}
is the convex body in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
with support function
\widehat{u}_{\widetilde{\Omega}}(z)=\varphi_{\Omega}(z)
. For any
y\in\widehat{\Omega}
, there exists
X\in\Omega
such that
y\in\pi\left(X\right)
. Consequently,
\langle y,z\rangle\leq\widehat{u}_{\pi(X)}(z)=-\langle x,z\rangle+x_{n+1}
for all
z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
.
Since
\varphi_{X}\left(z\right)\leq\varphi_{\Omega}(z)
for all
z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
,
we have

\langle y,z\rangle\leq-\langle x,z\rangle+x_{n+1}=\varphi_{X}\left(z\right)\leq\varphi_{\Omega}\left(z\right)=e^{u(z)},\quad\forall z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}.

Hence
y\in\widetilde{\Omega}
and then
\widehat{\Omega}\subset\widetilde{\Omega}
.

For any
y_{0}\in\widetilde{\Omega}
, we claim that there exists
X_{0}=\left(x^{0},x_{n+1}^{0}\right)\in\Omega
satisfying

y_{0}\in\pi(X_{0})=\widehat{B}\left(-x^{0},x^{0}_{n+1}\right)\subset\widehat{\Omega}
. This will imply
\widetilde{\Omega}\subset\widehat{\Omega}
and then
\widetilde{\Omega}=\widehat{\Omega}
.

Since
y_{0}\in\widetilde{\Omega}
, we have

(12.8)

\langle y_{0},z\rangle-\varphi_{\Omega}\left(z\right)\leq 0,\quad\forall z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}.

Let
X_{1}=\left(x^{1},x^{1}_{n+1}\right)
be an interior point of
\Omega
. Obviously,
\varphi_{X_{1}}(z)<\varphi_{\Omega}(z)
. If
y_{0}\in\widehat{B}\left(-x^{1},x^{1}_{n+1}\right)=\pi\left(X_{1}\right)
, then we can choose
X_{0}:=X_{1}
. Hence we can assume that
y_{0}
lies outside
\pi\left(X_{1}\right)
without loss of generality, which implies

(12.9)

\max_{z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}}\left(\langle y_{0},z\rangle-\varphi_{X_{1}}\left(z\right)\right)>0.

We define an auxiliary function

(12.10)

\varphi_{t}(z):=t\varphi_{\Omega}(z)+\left(1-t\right)\varphi_{X_{1}}(z),\quad t\in(0,1].

From (
12.8
) and (
12.9
), there exists
t_{0}\in(0,1]
such that

\displaystyle\max_{z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}}\left(\langle y_{0},z\rangle-\varphi_{t_{0}}\left(z\right)\right)=0,

and we assume that the maximum is attained at
z_{0}\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
, which implies
\langle y_{0},z_{0}\rangle=\varphi_{t_{0}}\left(z_{0}\right)
. At
z_{0}
, we have

\langle y_{0},e\rangle-\langle D\varphi_{t_{0}}\left(z_{0}\right),e\rangle=0,\quad\forall e\in T_{z_{0}}\mathbb{S}^{n}.

Then we can assume
y_{0}-D\varphi_{t_{0}}\left(z_{0}\right)=\lambda z_{0}
for some
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
. Taking the inner product with
z_{0}
on both sides of this formula, we get
\lambda=\langle y_{0},z_{0}\rangle=\varphi_{t_{0}}\left(z_{0}\right)
. Hence

y_{0}=D\varphi_{t_{0}}\left(z_{0}\right)+\varphi_{t_{0}}(z_{0})z_{0}.

By Definition
1.1
and (
12.10
), we know that
\log\varphi_{t_{0}}(z)
is the horospherical support function of a uniformly h-convex bounded domain
\Omega_{t_{0}}
, see also
[
3
, Proposition 5.1]
. Since
\varphi_{t_{0}}(z)\leq\varphi_{\Omega}(z)
, we know
\Omega_{t_{0}}\subset\Omega
.
By using (
2.9
), we can find a point
X_{0}:=X(z_{0})\in\partial\Omega_{t_{0}}\subset\Omega
. By (
3.30
), we obtain

X_{0}-x_{n+1}^{0}(z_{0},1)=-\left(D\varphi_{t_{0}}(z_{0})+\varphi_{t_{0}}(z_{0})z_{0},0\right)=-y_{0}.

It is easy to see that
\left(X_{0}-x_{n+1}^{0}\left(z_{0},1\right)\right)\in\widehat{B}(x_{0},x_{n+1}^{0})
, which implies

y_{0}\in\widehat{B}\left(-x_{0},x^{0}_{n+1}\right)=\pi(X_{0})\subset\widehat{\Omega}.

Then
\widetilde{\Omega}\subset\widehat{\Omega}
and hence
\widetilde{\Omega}=\widehat{\Omega}
.
This shows that
\widehat{\Omega}=\pi(\Omega)
is a convex body with support function
\varphi_{\Omega}(z)
in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. As
\varphi_{\Omega}(z)=e^{u_{\Omega}(z)}>0
, we know that
\pi(\Omega)
contains the origin in its interior.
We complete the proof of Proposition
12.1
.
∎

12.3.
Weighted
p
-Brunn-Minkowski type inequality and weighted
p
-Minkowski type inequality for h-convex domains

The
p
-Brunn-Minkowski inequality (Theorem G) and
p
-Minkowski inequality (Theorem H) play important roles in the convex geometric analysis.
Proposition
12.1
and Theorem
12.2
suggest us to interpret them to geometric inequalities for h-convex domains in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
by use of map
\pi
. The results in this subsection will be proved in Subsection
12.4
.

To state our results, we should define two geometric functionals.

Definition 12.3
.

Let
p
be a real number.
Let
K
and
L
be two smooth uniformly h-convex bounded domain in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. We define

(12.11)

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(K):=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n+1}\int_{\partial K}\frac{p_{n}\left(\cosh r\kappa-\widetilde{u}\right)}{\left(\cosh r-\widetilde{u}\right)^{n+1}}d\mu,

(12.12)

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{p}\left(K,L\right):=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n+1}\int_{\partial K}\varphi_{L}^{p}(z)\frac{p_{n}\left(\cosh r\kappa-\widetilde{u}\right)}{\left(\cosh r-\widetilde{u}\right)^{n+1-p}}d\mu.

Proposition 12.3
.

The geometric functional
\mathcal{V}
is monotone increasing with respect to inclusions for h-convex domains.

Theorem G can be used to get a weighted horospherical
p
-Brunn-Minkowski type inequality for h-convex domains in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
.

Theorem 12.4
.

Let
1\leq p\leq 2
,
a\geq 0
,
b\geq 0
and
a+b\geq 1
. Assume that
K
and
L
are two smooth uniformly h-convex bounded domains in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
.
Let
\Omega=a\cdot K+_{p}^{\mathcal{H}}b\cdot L
. Then

(12.13)

\mathcal{V}^{\frac{p}{n+1}}\left(\Omega\right)\geq a^{\frac{1}{p}}\mathcal{V}^{\frac{p}{n+1}}\left(K\right)+b^{\frac{1}{p}}\mathcal{V}^{\frac{p}{n+1}}\left(L\right).

When
p=1
, equality holds in (
12.13
) if
K
and
L
are hyperbolic dilates.
When
1<p\leq 2
and
ab\neq 0
, equality holds in (
12.13
) if and only if
K
and
L
are hyperbolic dilates.

Theorem H can be used to get a weighted horospherical
p
-Minkowski type inequality for h-convex domains in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
.

Theorem 12.5
.

Let
p\geq 1
. Assume that
K
and
L
are two smooth uniformly h-convex bounded domains in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
.
Then

(12.14)

\mathcal{V}_{p}\left(K,L\right)\geq\mathcal{V}^{\frac{n+1-p}{n+1}}\left(K\right)\mathcal{V}^{\frac{p}{n+1}}\left(L\right).

When
p=1
, equality holds in (
12.14
) if
K
and
L
are hyperbolic dilates.
When
p>1
, equality holds in (
12.14
) if and only if
K
and
L
are hyperbolic dilates.

The following Theorem
12.6
is the counterpart of Theorem I in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
.

Theorem 12.6
.

Let
p\geq 1
. Assume that
\Omega
is a smooth uniformly h-convex bounded domain in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. Then

(12.15)

\int_{\partial\Omega}\frac{p_{n}\left(\cosh r\kappa-\widetilde{u}\right)}{\left(\cosh r-\widetilde{u}\right)^{n+1-p}}d\mu\geq\omega_{n}^{\frac{p}{n+1}}\left(\int_{\partial\Omega}\frac{p_{n}\left(\cosh r\kappa-\widetilde{u}\right)}{\left(\cosh r-\widetilde{u}\right)^{n+1}}d\mu\right)^{\frac{n+1-p}{n+1}}.

When
p>1
, equality holds in (
12.15
) if and only if
\Omega
is a geodesic ball centered at the origin. When
p=1
, equality holds in (
12.15
) if and only if
\Omega
is a geodesic ball.

12.4.
Proof of results in Subsection
12.3

Lemma 12.2
.

Let
p
be a real number.
Assume that
K
and
L
are smooth uniformly h-convex bounded domains in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. Let
\widehat{K}=\pi(K)
and
\widehat{L}=\pi(L)
. Then

(12.16)

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(K)=

\displaystyle\mathrm{Vol}\left(\widehat{K}\right),

(12.17)

\displaystyle\quad\mathcal{V}_{p}(K,L)=

\displaystyle\mathrm{Vol}_{p}\left(\widehat{K},\widehat{L}\right).

Proof.

The following formula is well-known in convex geometry (see e.g.,
[
73
, p. 121]
),

(12.18)

d\widehat{\mu}_{\widehat{K}}\left(\widehat{G}^{-1}_{\widehat{K}}(z)\right)=p_{n}\left(D^{2}\widehat{u}_{\widehat{K}}(z)+\widehat{u}_{\widehat{K}}(z)\right)d\sigma(z).

Recall the definition of
p
-mixed volume in (
12.1
).
Formula (
12.18
) together with (
12.5
) gives

\displaystyle\mathrm{Vol}_{p}\left(\widehat{K},\widehat{L}\right):=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n+1}\int_{\partial\widehat{K}}\widehat{u}_{\widehat{L}}^{p}(z)\widehat{u}_{\widehat{K}}^{1-p}(z)d\widehat{\mu}_{K}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n+1}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}\widehat{u}^{p}_{\widehat{L}}\widehat{u}_{\widehat{K}}^{1-p}p_{n}\left(D^{2}\widehat{u}_{\widehat{K}}+\widehat{u}_{\widehat{K}}I\right)d\sigma

(12.19)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n+1}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}\varphi_{L}^{p}\varphi_{K}^{1-p}p_{n}\left(D^{2}\varphi_{K}+\varphi_{K}I\right)d\sigma

Using (
12.7
), (
2.15
) and (
3.24
), we have

\displaystyle p_{n}\left(D^{2}\varphi_{K}(z)+\varphi_{K}(z)I\right)=

\displaystyle p_{n}\left(\cosh rI+A[\varphi_{K}]\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(\cosh r+\left(\cosh r-\widetilde{u}\right)\left(\tilde{\lambda}_{K}\right)_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p_{n}\left(\tilde{\lambda}_{K}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(\cosh r\left(\tilde{\kappa}_{K}\right)_{i}+\cosh r-\widetilde{u}\right)

(12.20)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p_{n}\left(\tilde{\lambda}_{K}\right)p_{n}\left(\cosh r\kappa_{K}-\widetilde{u}\right),

where the right-hand side of (
12.20
) was evaluated at
G^{-1}_{K}\left(z\right)
.
From (
2.16
) and (
2.15
), we obtain

(12.21)

\left(G^{-1}_{K}\right)^{*}\left(d\sigma(z)\right)=\det\left(A[\varphi_{K}]\right)^{-1}d\mu_{K}=\varphi_{K}^{n}p_{n}\left(\tilde{\kappa}_{K}\right)d\mu_{K}\left(G^{-1}_{K}(z)\right).

Inserting (
12.20
) and (
12.21
) into (
12.19
), and using (
12.12
), we get

\displaystyle\mathrm{Vol}_{p}\left(\widehat{K},\widehat{L}\right)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n+1}\int_{\partial K}\varphi_{L}^{p}(z)\varphi_{K}^{1-p}(z)p_{n}\left(\tilde{\lambda}_{K}\right)p_{n}\left(\cosh r\kappa_{K}-\widetilde{u}\right)\varphi_{K}^{n}p_{n}\left(\tilde{\kappa}_{K}\right)d\mu_{K}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n+1}\int_{\partial K}\varphi_{L}^{p}(z)\frac{p_{n}\left(\lambda^{\prime}\kappa_{K}-\widetilde{u}\right)}{\left(\cosh r-\widetilde{u}\right)^{n+1-p}}d\mu_{K}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{p}(K,L).

Hence we obtain (
12.17
).
Formula (
12.16
) follows by taking
L=K
in (
12.17
).
We complete the proof of Lemma
12.2
.
∎

Corollary 12.2
.

Let
p
be a real number. Assume that
K
is a smooth uniformly h-convex bounded domain in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
and
\widehat{K}=\pi(K)
. Then

(12.22)

\int_{\partial\widehat{K}}\widehat{u}_{\widehat{K}}^{1-p}(z)d\widehat{\mu}=\int_{\partial K}\frac{p_{n}\left(\cosh r\kappa-\widetilde{u}\right)}{\left(\cosh r-\widetilde{u}\right)^{n+1-p}}d\mu.

Proof.

Suppose that
L\subset\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
is a geodesic ball centered at the origin, which means
\varphi_{L}(z)\equiv C_{L}
for some constant
C_{L}\geq 1
. Proposition
12.1
gives
\widehat{u}_{\widehat{L}}(z)\equiv C_{L}
.
Recall the definitions of
\mathrm{Vol}_{p}(\widehat{K},\widehat{L})
and
\mathcal{V}(K,L)
in (
12.1
) and (
12.12
), respectively.
Then (
12.22
) follows directly from (
12.17
).
∎

Lemma 12.3
.

Assume that
K
and
L
are smooth uniformly h-convex bounded domains in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. Let
\widehat{K}=\pi(K)
and
\widehat{L}=\pi(L)
. The following statements are equivalent,

(1)

K
and
L
are hyperbolic dilates,

(2)

\widehat{K}
and
\widehat{L}
are dilates.

Proof.

Lemma
12.3
follows directly from Proposition
12.1
and Definition
6.2
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
12.3
.

Let
\Omega_{1}
and
\Omega_{2}
be two h-convex domains in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
with
\Omega_{1}\subset\Omega_{2}
. Since
\varphi_{\Omega_{1}}\leq\varphi_{\Omega_{2}}
, (
12.5
) implies that
\widehat{\Omega}_{1}\subset\widehat{\Omega}_{2}
. Therefore, Proposition
12.3
follows from (
12.16
).
∎

Proof of Theorems
12.4
–
12.6
.

Combining Theorem G with (
12.6
) and (
12.16
), we obtain (
12.13
). By Lemma
12.3
, equality holds in (
12.13
) if
K
and
L
are hyperbolic dilates. Besides, if
1<p\leq 2
, then equality holds in (
12.13
) if and only if
K
and
L
are hyperbolic dilates. Then we complete the proof of Theorem
12.4
.

Theorem
12.5
follows from Theorem H, (
12.16
), (
12.17
) and a similar argument as above.

Inequality (
12.15
) follows from (
12.4
), (
12.22
) and (
12.16
). When
p>1
, by Theorem G and Proposition
12.2
, we know that the equality holds in (
12.15
) if and only if
\Omega
is a geodesic ball centered at the origin. When
p=1
, by the classical isoperimetric inequality and Proposition
12.2
, we have that the equality holds in (
12.15
) if and only if
\Omega
is a geodesic ball. We complete the proof of Theorem
12.6
.
∎

In the last part of Section
12
, we would like to make the following comments on map
\pi
.

(1)

The Firey’s
p
-sum (
p>1
) for convex bodies in Euclidean space depends on the choice of the origin of
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
.
Now we choose a point to be the origin of
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
, i.e.
N=(0,1)\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1,1}
in the hyperboloid model. Then map
\pi
gives a perfect relationship between the hyperbolic
p
-sum and the Firey’s
p
-sum, which was proved in Theorem
12.2
.
We have shown in Proposition
6.2
and Proposition
4.6
that the both hyperbolic
p
-sum and hyperbolic
p
-dilation can be constructed intrinsically in hyperbolic space
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
, which is a different phenomenon comparing with the Firey’s
p
-sum.

(2)

The approach in Section
12
relies on the h-convexity of domains in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. Specifically, we used the horospherical support function to investigate the properties of the map
\pi
. Recall that we defined hyperbolic
p
-sums for sets in Definition
1.3
, and the definition of
\pi
does not require the convexity of domains.
For nonconvex compact sets in Euclidean space, the
p
-Brunn-Minkowski inequality
(p>1)
was proved in
[
57
]
.
Thus, it is natural to study whether the results in Section
12
still hold in a more general setting.
For example, we can ask:
Can one remove the h-convex assumption of
K
and
L
in Theorem
12.4
?

(3)

According to Gardner
[
34
]
, if inequalities are silver currency in mathematics, those that come along with precise equality conditions are gold.
For many important geometric inequalities of multiple domains in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, the equality holds if and only if the domains are dilates. Similarly, the inequalities of a single domain in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
holds equality always require that the domain is a geodesic ball.
So, another strong motivations that suggest us to move geometric inequalities for domains in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
to those in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
are Proposition
12.2
and Lemma
12.3
.
In conclusion, our approach in Section
12
can be used to find many geometric inequalities for h-convex domains in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
by using inequalities for domains in Euclidean space. Furthermore, the approach can give new characterizations of geodesic balls in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. Finally, we will give a concrete example in Theorem
12.7
.

(4)

For inequality (
12.15
), the h-convex assumption of
\Omega
in Theorem
12.6
is not natural.
A domain
\Omega
in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
is called static-convex if the principal curvatures are greater than or equal to
\frac{\widetilde{u}}{\cosh r}
on
\partial\Omega
. The static-convex assumption of domains appeared in many important geometric inequalities, see e.g.
[
17
,
47
,
78
]
.
Therefore, we conjecture that Theorem
12.15
holds for all static-convex domains in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
.

(5)

In this paper, we focus on geometric inequalities of domains in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. Let
\Omega
be a domain in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
and
M=\partial\Omega
be its boundary. It is easy to see that
\nu+X
and
\nu-X
are two light-like, outward pointing normal of
M\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1,1}
, where
\nu+X
is future directed and
\nu-X
is past directed. Besides,
\nu-X=-\varphi^{-1}(z)\left(z,1\right)
. Then we know that
-\xi\left(M\right)
is the intersection of the null hypersurface generated by
M
and hyperplane
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\times\{0\}
, see Corollary
12.1
. If we no longer require that the spacelike submanifold
M^{n}
lies on
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
, then the above structure was well studied in general relativity, see e.g.
[
61
,
62
]
. Hence, it is natural to ask:
How to propose the results in Section
12
for general spacelike submanifold
M^{n}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1,1}
?

Now we give another direct application of the map
\pi
. In
[
15
]
, Brendle, Choi, and Daskalopoulos classified the self-similar solutions of the
\alpha
-Gauss curvature flow for all
\alpha\geq\frac{1}{n+2}
,
n\geq 2
.
For convenience, we do not consider the affine invariant case
\alpha=\frac{1}{n+2}
in the following statement of their result.

Theorem J

(
[
15
]
)
.

Let
\alpha\in(\frac{1}{n+2},+\infty)
and
n\geq 2
. Assume that
\widehat{\Omega}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
is a uniformly convex bounded domain containing the origin in its interior, and satisfies

p_{n}(D^{2}\widehat{u}_{\widehat{\Omega}}(z)+\widehat{u}_{\widehat{\Omega}}(z)I)^{-\alpha}=\widehat{u}_{\widehat{\Omega}}(z),\quad\forall z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}.

Then
\widehat{\Omega}
is a geodesic ball centered at the origin.

Theorem 12.7
.

Let
\alpha\in(\frac{1}{n+2},+\infty)
and
n\geq 2
.
Let
\Omega
be a smooth uniformly h-convex bounded domain in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. Assume that
\partial\Omega
satisfies

(12.23)

p_{n}\left(\cosh r+\frac{\cosh r-\widetilde{u}}{\tilde{\kappa}}\right)^{\alpha}=\gamma\left(\cosh r-\widetilde{u}\right)

for some positive constant
\gamma
.
Then
\Omega
is a geodesic ball centered at the origin.

Proof.

Using (
12.20
), (
3.24
) and Proposition
12.1
, we have that equation (
12.23
) is equivalent to

p_{n}\left(D^{2}\widehat{u}_{\pi\left(\Omega\right)}(z)+\widehat{u}_{\pi(\Omega)}(z)I\right)^{\alpha}=\gamma\widehat{u}^{-1}_{\pi(\Omega)}(z),\quad\forall z\in\mathbb{S}^{n}.

Letting
\widehat{\Omega}=\gamma^{-\frac{1}{1+\alpha n}}\pi(\Omega)
in Theorem J, we have that
\widehat{\Omega}
is a geodesic ball centered at the origin.
By Proposition
12.2
, we know that
\Omega
is a geodesic ball centered at the origin. Therefore, we complete the proof of Theorem
12.7
.
∎

The study of curvature equations in space forms is a longstanding problem in geometric analysis, see e.g.
[
32
,
33
,
41
]
. Combining results in that topic with the map
\pi
in this section can give other characterizations of geodesic balls in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
.

13.
Appendix

13.1.
Intrinsic proof of Lemma
9.2

The following Lemma
13.1
is well known in conformal geometry (see e.g.,
[
43
, Equation (2.1)]
).

Lemma 13.1
.

For any two conformal metrics
g
and
\tilde{g}
, which satisfy
\tilde{g}=e^{2\lambda}g
for some smooth function
\lambda
, we have

(13.1)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathrm{Ric}}_{ij}=

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ric}_{ij}-(n-2)\left(\nabla_{j}\nabla_{i}\lambda-\nabla_{i}\lambda\nabla_{j}\lambda\right)-\Delta\lambda g_{ij}-(n-2)|\nabla\lambda|^{2}g_{ij},

\displaystyle e^{2\lambda}\widetilde{R}=

\displaystyle R-2(n-1)\Delta\lambda-(n-1)(n-2)|\nabla\lambda|^{2},

where
\widetilde{\mathrm{Ric}}_{ij}
and
\widetilde{R}
are the Ricci curvature tensor and scalar curvature with respect to metric
\tilde{g}
.

Now we give an intrinsic proof of Lemma
9.2
.

Proof of Lemma
9.2
.

In this proof, we use normal coordinates around
z_{0}\in(\mathbb{S}^{n},g_{0})
such that the Ricci tensor is diagonal at
z_{0}
.
Taking
\lambda=-\log\varphi
in (
13.1
), at
z_{0}
we have
\tilde{g}^{ij}=\varphi^{2}\delta_{ij}
and

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathrm{Ric}}_{ij}=

\displaystyle(n-1)\delta_{ij}-(n-2)\left(-(\log\varphi)_{ij}-\frac{\varphi_{i}\varphi_{j}}{\varphi^{2}}\right)+\Delta\log\varphi\delta_{ij}-(n-2)\frac{|D\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi^{2}}\delta_{ij}

(13.2)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(n-1)\delta_{ij}+(n-2)\frac{\varphi_{ij}}{\varphi}+\left(\frac{\Delta\varphi}{\varphi}-\frac{|D\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi^{2}}\right)\delta_{ij}-(n-2)\frac{|D\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi^{2}}\delta_{ij}.

Hence

(13.3)

\displaystyle\widetilde{R}:=\tilde{g}^{ij}\widetilde{\mathrm{Ric}}_{ij}=n(n-1)\varphi^{2}+2(n-1)\varphi\Delta\varphi-n(n-1)|D\varphi|^{2}.

Using (
13.2
), (
13.3
) and (
2.15
), at
z_{0}
we have

\displaystyle\tilde{g}^{ik}\widetilde{S}_{kj}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n-2}\left(\varphi^{2}\widetilde{\mathrm{Ric}}_{ij}-\frac{\widetilde{R}}{2(n-1)}\delta_{ij}\right)=\varphi\left(\varphi_{ij}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{|D\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}\delta_{ij}+\frac{1}{2}\varphi\delta_{ij}\right)

(13.4)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varphi A_{ij}[\varphi]+\frac{1}{2}\delta_{ij}=\left(\tilde{\lambda}_{i}+\frac{1}{2}\right)\delta_{ij},

where
\tilde{\lambda}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{\lambda}_{n}
are shifted principal radii of curvature of
M
. This completes the proof of Lemma
9.2
.
∎

13.2.
Theorem
6.4
without uniformly h-convex condition

The original proof of Theorem
6.4
used horospherical support function, which depends on the h-convexity of
\Omega
. In the following Proposition
13.1
, we only assume that
\Omega\subset\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
is a smooth convex bounded domain as we mentioned in Remark
6.1
.

Proposition 13.1
.

Let
n\geq 1
be an integer and
\Omega
be a smooth convex bounded domain in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
, then

\displaystyle\mathrm{Vol}_{w}(\Omega_{\rho})-\mathrm{Vol}_{w}(\Omega)=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{n}\left(\int_{\partial\Omega}\cosh r\sigma_{k}(\tilde{\kappa})d\mu\right)\int_{0}^{\rho}e^{(n-k+1)t}\sinh^{k}tdt

\displaystyle-\sum_{k=0}^{n}\left(\int_{\partial\Omega}(\cosh r-\tilde{u})\sigma_{k}(\tilde{\kappa})d\mu\right)\int_{0}^{\rho}e^{(n-k)t}\sinh^{k+1}tdt.

Proof.

For any position vector
X\in\partial\Omega
, we know
\cosh r=-\langle X,(0,1)\rangle=x_{n+1}
by (
2.3
). Recall that
\overline{\nabla}
and
\widetilde{\nabla}
denote the connections on
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
and
\mathbb{R}^{n+1,1}
respectively.
As
V:=\sinh r\partial_{r}=\overline{\nabla}\cosh r
, we have that
V
is the projection of
(0,-1)=\widetilde{\nabla}x_{n+1}
on
T_{X}\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. Hence,
\tilde{u}:=\langle V,\nu\rangle=-\langle(0,1),\nu\rangle
at
X\in\partial\Omega
.
Denote by
X_{t}
the position vector on
\partial\Omega_{t}
. Then (see e.g.,
[
26
, Remark 14]
)

\displaystyle X_{t}=\cosh tX+\sinh t\nu.

Let
r_{t}
be the geodesic distance form
X_{t}
to
N=(0,1)\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1,1}
, i.e.
r_{t}=d_{\mathbb{H}^{n+1}}(X_{t},N)
. Then (
4.13
) implies

\displaystyle\cosh r_{t}=

\displaystyle-\langle X_{t},(0,1)\rangle=-\cosh t\langle X,(0,1)\rangle-\sinh t\langle\nu,(0,1)\rangle

(13.5)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\cosh t\cosh r+\sinh t\tilde{u}=\cosh re^{t}-\left(\cosh r-\tilde{u}\right)\sinh t.

Now we use normal coordinates for
\partial\Omega
around some point
p\in\partial\Omega
such that
g_{ij}=\delta_{ij}
and
\left(h_{i}{}^{j}\right)={\rm diag}\left(\kappa_{1},\ldots,\kappa_{n}\right)
at
p
. Then

\displaystyle\partial_{i}X_{t}=\cosh t\partial_{i}X+\sinh t\partial_{i}\nu=(\cosh t+\sinh t\kappa_{i})\partial_{i}X=\left(e^{t}+\sinh t\tilde{\kappa}_{i}\right)\partial_{i}X.

It follows that

(13.6)

\displaystyle d\mu_{t}=\sqrt{\det\left(\langle\partial_{i}X_{t},\partial_{j}X_{t}\rangle\right)}d\mu=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}(e^{t}+\sinh t\tilde{\kappa}_{i})d\mu=\sum_{k=0}^{n}e^{(n-k)t}\sinh^{k}t\sigma_{k}(\tilde{\kappa})d\mu.

Using (
13.5
) and (
13.6
), we have

\displaystyle A_{w}(\Omega_{t}):=

\displaystyle\int_{\partial\Omega_{t}}\cosh r_{t}d\mu_{t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\partial\Omega}\left(\cosh re^{t}-\left(\cosh r-\tilde{u}\right)\sinh t\right)\left(\sum_{k=0}^{n}e^{(n-k)t}\sinh^{k}t\sigma_{k}(\tilde{\kappa})\right)d\mu

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{n}\left(\int_{\partial\Omega}\cosh r\sigma_{k}(\tilde{\kappa})d\mu\right)e^{(n-k+1)t}\sinh^{k}t

(13.7)

\displaystyle-\sum_{k=0}^{n}\left(\int_{\partial\Omega}\left(\cosh r-\tilde{u}\right)\sigma_{k}(\tilde{\kappa})d\mu\right)e^{(n-k)t}\sinh^{k+1}t.

Proposition
13.1
follows by integrating the both sides of (
13.7
) from
0
to
\rho
.
∎

13.3.
Origin symmetric assumption in Conjecture
11.3

Lemma 13.2
.

Let
Y\in\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
and
K
be a symmetric domain with respect to
X
in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
.
Then the weighted volume of
K
with respect to
Y=(y,y_{n+1})
is given by

(13.8)

\int_{K}\cosh d_{\mathbb{H}^{n+1}}(P,Y)dv=\cosh\left(d_{\mathbb{H}^{n+1}}(X,Y)\right)\int_{K}\cosh d_{\mathbb{H}^{n+1}}(P,X)dv,

where
P\in K
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we assume that
X=(0,1)
is the north pole of the hyperboloid model. Then

\displaystyle\int_{K}\cosh d_{\mathbb{H}^{n+1}}(P,Y)dv=

\displaystyle-\int_{K}\langle P,Y\rangle dv

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{K}p_{n+1}y_{n+1}dv-\int_{K}\langle p,y\rangle dv

(13.9)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle y_{n+1}\int_{K}p_{n+1}dv,

where
P=(p,p_{n+1})\in K
, and the last equality followed from the symmetry of
K
.
Then (
13.8
) follows by substituting
y_{n+1}=\cosh d_{\mathbb{H}^{n+1}}(X,Y)
and
p_{n+1}=\cosh d_{\mathbb{H}^{n+1}}(X,P)
into (
13.3
). This completes the proof of Lemma
13.2
.
∎

Taking
K=B(X,r)
and
Y=(0,1)
in (
13.8
), we get the weighted volume of geodesic balls not centered at the origin.

Corollary 13.1
.

Let
B(X,r)
be a geodesic ball of radius
r
centered at
X=(x,x_{n+1})
in
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. Then

\mathcal{S}(B(X,r))=x_{n+1}^{\frac{1}{n+1}}\sinh r.

Assume that
X=(x,x_{n+1})\in\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
. Let
r_{1}=\log\frac{11}{10}
,
r_{2}=\log\frac{4}{3}
and
r_{3}=\log\frac{73}{60}
. Let
K=B(X,r_{1})
,
L=B(X,r_{2})
and
\Omega=B(X,r_{3})
be geodesic balls centered at
X
. Then
\Omega=\frac{1}{2}\cdot K+_{1}\frac{1}{2}\cdot L
. In addition, if
x_{n+1}=100^{n+1}
, then Corollary
13.1
implies

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{S}(\Omega)+\sqrt{\mathcal{S}^{2}(\Omega)+1}\right)-\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathcal{S}(K)+\sqrt{\mathcal{S}^{2}(K)+1}\right)-\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathcal{S}(L)+\sqrt{\mathcal{S}^{2}(L)+1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.7533\ldots>0.

If
x_{n+1}=2^{n+1}
, then Corollary
13.1
implies

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{S}(\Omega)+\sqrt{\mathcal{S}^{2}(\Omega)+1}\right)-\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathcal{S}(K)+\sqrt{\mathcal{S}^{2}(K)+1}\right)-\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathcal{S}(L)+\sqrt{\mathcal{S}^{2}(L)+1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-0.0516\ldots<0.

Consequently, the origin symmetric assumption in Conjecture
11.3
can not be removed.

13.4.
Another proof of Theorem
4.5
for
1\leq p\leq 2

In the following Lemma
13.3
, we prove that the Minkowski norm
N
is concave with respect to future time-like vectors.

Lemma 13.3
.

Let
X
and
Y
be future time-like vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1,1}
and
t\in(0,1)
be a real number. Then

(13.10)

N\left(\left(1-t\right)X+tY\right)\geq(1-t)N(X)+tN(Y),

with equality if and only if
X=kY
for some constant
k>0
. Thus,
N(X)
is a concave function defined on the future time-like vectors.

Proof.

The proof is straightforward. Assume that
X=(x,x_{n+1})
and
Y=(y,y_{n+1})
with
x_{n+1}>|x|\geq 0
and
y_{n+1}>|y|\geq 0
. A direct calculation shows

\displaystyle N((1-t)X+tY)=

\displaystyle\left(\left((1-t)x_{n+1}+ty_{n+1}\right)^{2}-|(1-t)x+ty|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\left(\left((1-t)x_{n+1}+ty_{n+1}\right)^{2}-\left((1-t)|x|+t|y|\right)^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left((1-t)^{2}\left(x_{n+1}^{2}-|x|^{2}\right)+t^{2}\left(y_{n+1}^{2}-|y|^{2}\right)+2t\left(1-t\right)\left(x_{n+1}y_{n+1}-|x||y|\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

(13.11)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left((1-t)^{2}N(X)^{2}+t^{2}N(Y)^{2}+2t(1-t)\left(x_{n+1}y_{n+1}-|x||y|\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

with equality if and only if either
x=ky
or
y=kx
for some constant
k\geq 0
.
On the other hand, the AM-GM inequality yields

\displaystyle x_{n+1}y_{n+1}-|x||y|=

\displaystyle\left(x_{n+1}^{2}y_{n+1}^{2}+|x|^{2}|y|^{2}-2x_{n+1}y_{n+1}|x||y|\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\left(x_{n+1}^{2}y_{n+1}^{2}+|x|^{2}|y|^{2}-x_{n+1}^{2}|y|^{2}-y_{n+1}^{2}|x|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(x_{n+1}^{2}-|x|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(y_{n+1}^{2}-|y|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

(13.12)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle N(X)N(Y),

with equality if and only if
x_{n+1}|y|=y_{n+1}|x|
. Then (
13.10
) follows by substituting (
13.12
) into (
13.11
). For future time-like vectors
X
and
Y
, if equality hold in the both (
13.11
) and (
13.12
), then
X=kY
for some constant
k>0
.
This completes the proof of Lemma
13.3
.
∎

By using the above Lemma
13.3
, we can give another proof of Theorem
4.5
for
1\leq p\leq 2
.

Another proof of Theorem
4.5
for
1\leq p\leq 2
.

We first consider the case
p=1
.
The concavity of the Minkowski norm
N
in (
13.10
) implies that

N(ae^{r_{1}}X+be^{r_{2}}Y)\geq aN(e^{r_{1}}X)+bN(e^{r_{2}}Y)=ae^{r_{1}}+be^{r_{2}},

with equality if and only if
X=Y
. This together with Corollary
4.2
gives that
\Omega
is a geodesic ball of radius greater than
\frac{1}{p}\log\left(ae^{pr_{1}+be^{pr_{2}}}\right)
whenever
X\neq Y
. If
X=Y
, then the radius of
\Omega
is
\frac{1}{p}\log\left(ae^{pr_{1}+be^{pr_{2}}}\right)
. Thus we have that Theorem
4.5
holds for
p=1
.

Now we consider the case
1<p\leq 2
.
Let
q
satisfy
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1
.
Taking
A=a^{\frac{1}{p}}\varphi_{K}(z)
,
B=b^{\frac{1}{p}}\varphi_{L}(z)
and

t=\frac{be^{pr_{2}}}{ae^{pr_{1}}+be^{pr_{2}}}

in inequality (
4.19
), we have

\displaystyle\varphi_{\Omega}(z)=

\displaystyle\left(a\varphi_{K}(z)^{p}+b\varphi_{L}(z)^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\left(\frac{ae^{pr_{1}}}{ae^{pr_{1}}+be^{pr_{2}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}a^{\frac{1}{p}}\varphi_{K}(z)+\left(\frac{be^{pr_{2}}}{ae^{pr_{1}+be^{pr_{2}}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}b^{\frac{1}{p}}\varphi_{L}(z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\langle\left(\frac{ae^{pr_{1}}}{ae^{pr_{1}}+be^{pr_{2}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}a^{\frac{1}{p}}e^{r_{1}}X+\left(\frac{be^{pr_{2}}}{ae^{pr_{1}+be^{pr_{2}}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}b^{\frac{1}{p}}e^{r_{2}}Y,(z,1)\rangle

(13.13)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\langle T,(z,1)\rangle,

where the first equality used the assumption
1<p\leq 2
and Theorem
4.1
, and

T:=\left(\frac{ae^{pr_{1}}}{ae^{pr_{1}}+be^{pr_{2}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}a^{\frac{1}{p}}e^{r_{1}}X+\left(\frac{be^{pr_{2}}}{ae^{pr_{1}+be^{pr_{2}}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}b^{\frac{1}{p}}e^{r_{2}}Y.

By the convexity of the domain enclosed by the future light cone of
(0,0)
, it is obvious that
T
is a future time-like vector.
By the concavity of the Minkowski norm
N
, we have

\displaystyle N(T)\geq

\displaystyle\left(\frac{ae^{pr_{1}}}{ae^{pr_{1}}+be^{pr_{2}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}a^{\frac{1}{p}}e^{r_{1}}+\left(\frac{be^{pr_{2}}}{ae^{pr_{1}+be^{pr_{2}}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}b^{\frac{1}{p}}e^{r_{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(ae^{pr_{1}}+be^{pr_{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\geq(a+b)^{\frac{1}{p}}\geq 1,

and equality holds in the first inequality if and only if
X=Y
. Let
\widetilde{B}=B(\frac{T}{N(T)},\log N(T))
.
Then the above inequality implies that the radius of
\widetilde{B}
is greater than
\frac{1}{p}\left(ae^{pr_{1}}+be^{pr_{2}}\right)
whenever
X\neq Y
. On the other hand, Lemma
4.4
implies
\varphi_{\widetilde{B}}=-\langle T,(z,1)\rangle
, and hence (
13.13
) deduces
\widetilde{B}\subset\Omega
.
Consequently, if
p>1
and
X\neq Y
, then there is a geodesic ball
\widetilde{B}
of radius greater than
\frac{1}{p}\left(ae^{pr_{1}}+be^{pr_{2}}\right)
lying inside
\Omega
. If
1<p\leq 2
and
X=Y
, then the identity
\widetilde{B}=\Omega
follows by the same argument as in the previous proof of Theorem
4.5
.

Then we complete the proof of Theorem
4.5
for
1\leq p\leq 2
.
∎
